# Research Log — AI Agents with Python

Each entry is structured, not prose. Downstream agents query this by chapter ID rather than reading the whole file.

Research policy:
- Prefer current official documentation and primary sources.
- Validate smolagents and other external Python APIs with chub before recording API-specific claims; if chub lacks coverage, use official documentation and flag the fallback.
- Use at most one direct quote per source; prefer paraphrase.
- Record material unresolved conflicts under `## Contradiction flags` for the Phase 3 user gate.

---

## ch-01 — Meet Python and AI Agents

### entry-001
source: Python Software Foundation — General Python FAQ, "What is Python?" (https://docs.python.org/3/faq/general.html#what-is-python), Python 3.14.6 documentation, last updated 2026-07-31.
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Canonical beginner-facing definition of Python for the chapter's opener. Establishes that Python is an interpreted, interactive, object-oriented programming language with dynamic typing, high-level data types, support for multiple paradigms (procedural, functional, OO), clear syntax, portability across Linux/macOS/Windows, a rich standard library, and C/C++ extensibility. Anchors the "what kind of tool is Python" beat before any agent work.
paraphrase: Python is an interpreted, high-level, general-purpose programming language that supports several programming styles and uses clear, readable syntax. The same Python program runs on Linux, macOS, and Windows without modification, and the language ships with a large standard library plus a vast ecosystem of third-party packages, so beginners do not have to build everything from scratch.

### entry-002
source: Python Software Foundation — General Python FAQ, "What is Python good for?" (https://docs.python.org/3/faq/general.html#what-is-python-good-for), Python 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Reinforces that Python is general-purpose and batteries-included, with a standard library covering string processing, internet protocols, software-engineering utilities, and operating-system interfaces, plus third-party extensions indexed at the Python Package Index. Pairs with entry-001 to frame Python as the right base for the agent-building work later in the book.
paraphrase: Python is described as a high-level, general-purpose language applicable to many classes of problems. Its standard library already covers areas such as text processing, common internet protocols, testing and logging, and operating-system interfaces, and a wide variety of third-party extensions are available through the Python Package Index. The chapter should signal early that learners will lean on existing libraries rather than reinvent them.

### entry-003
source: Python Software Foundation — "Whetting Your Appetite" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/appetite.html), The Python Tutorial, 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Beginner-friendly positioning of why Python was chosen for this book: simpler than C/C++/Java, cross-platform, interpreted so the compile-and-link step disappears, and supportive of interactive experimentation. Useful for the chapter's "why Python and not another language" beat.
paraphrase: Python is simpler to use than C, C++, or Java; it is available on Windows, macOS, and Unix; and as an interpreted language it removes the compile-and-link step so beginners can experiment interactively, even using the interpreter as a desk calculator. The chapter can lean on this framing to set the welcoming, low-friction tone the intake requested.

### entry-004
source: IBM — "What are large language models (LLMs)?" by Cole Stryker (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/large-language-models).
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Authoritative, beginner-accessible definition of an LLM. Frames LLMs as a category of deep-learning model, trained on massive text corpora, built on transformer neural-network architecture, and operating as statistical prediction machines that repeatedly predict the next token in a sequence. This is the chapter's core contrast point against ordinary Python programs.
paraphrase: A large language model is a category of deep-learning model trained on very large amounts of text and built on a transformer neural network. It works as a statistical prediction machine that repeatedly predicts the next unit of text given the context that came before. This is fundamentally different from ordinary Python: a normal program's output is determined by its code, whereas an LLM's output is a statistically plausible continuation that can vary from call to call and can be subtly wrong.

### entry-005
source: IBM — "What are large language models (LLMs)?", "Evaluating LLMs" section (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/large-language-models).
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Critical safety caveat the chapter must surface up front: LLMs hallucinate (generate plausible-but-false content), inherit biases from their training data, and carry substantial compute, cost, and environmental footprint. The beginner mental model must include that LLM output is untrusted by default and must be verified before it is acted on.
paraphrase: LLMs are powerful but unreliable in two specific ways worth flagging before any agent work begins. First, they can hallucinate — producing false or misleading text that sounds plausible. Second, they can reflect and amplify biases present in their training data. Training and running them also demands significant compute and energy, which affects cost and sustainability. The chapter should therefore teach beginners to treat every LLM response as a draft that needs checking, especially when it feeds into automated action.

### entry-006
source: Hugging Face smolagents documentation, "What are agents? — An introduction to agentic systems" (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/conceptual_guides/intro_agents), cross-checked against chub entry `smolagents/package` (smolagents==1.24.0, updated 2026-03-12). Docs URL and PyPI release both resolve to 1.24.0; chub-validated.
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Provides the working definition of an "AI agent" for the book. Establishes that an AI agent is "a program where LLM outputs control the workflow" and that agency is a continuous spectrum, not a binary label, ranging from "LLM output has no impact on program flow" (☆☆☆ simple processor) to "one agentic workflow can start another" (★★★ multi-agent). Intermediate rungs include LLM-controlled if/else routers, LLM-chosen tool calls, and LLM-driven multi-step loops.
quote: "AI Agents are programs where LLM outputs control the workflow."
paraphrase: An AI agent is a program whose flow of execution is steered by the output of a language model. The word "agent" is not binary — it describes a spectrum of how much control the LLM has over the program's decisions. A program that merely prints an LLM response has no agency; a loop in which the LLM chooses the next tool to call has high agency; a system in which one such loop can start another has the highest. The chapter should use this spectrum to demystify the term and avoid the misleading shorthand "an agent is an autonomous AI."

### entry-007
source: Anthropic — "Building effective agents" (https://www.anthropic.com/research/building-effective-agents), published 2024-12-19, "What are agents?" and "Building blocks, workflows, and agents" sections.
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Useful companion framing for ch-01. Anthropic distinguishes "workflows" — LLMs and tools orchestrated through predefined code paths — from "agents" — LLMs that dynamically direct their own processes and tool usage. In production, agents still operate inside limits: they begin with a human goal, may pause for human feedback at checkpoints, and usually carry explicit stopping conditions such as a maximum number of steps. The chapter should plant early that building an agent means building the boundaries, not just the loop.
paraphrase: A useful design distinction is between workflows and agents. In a workflow, the developer writes the code path that orchestrates LLMs and tools. In an agent, the LLM itself decides what to do next and which tool to call, then reconsiders after each step's result. Even so, production agents are not "set and forget" — they start from a human instruction, may pause for human feedback at checkpoints, and almost always include explicit stopping conditions (such as a maximum number of iterations) to keep the loop bounded. ch-01 should introduce that human control and explicit limits are part of what makes an agent safe to use.

### entry-008
source: smolagents package guide — chub entry `smolagents/package` (smolagents==1.24.0, 2026-03-12), cross-checked against the Hugging Face smolagents conceptual guide (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/conceptual_guides/intro_agents) and the tools tutorial (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.24.0/en/tutorials/tools).
used_in: ch-01
claim/finding: Anchors the chapter's "tool" concept. In smolagents, a tool is a typed Python function exposed to the agent via the `@tool` decorator (or a `Tool` base class); the agent uses the function's name, parameter type hints, and docstring to decide when to call it. Also surfaces the chapter's beginner safety caveat about code-executing agents: smolagents' `CodeAgent` can execute model-generated Python on the developer's machine, and the official guide recommends an isolated executor (E2B, Docker, Modal, or Pyodide+Deno WebAssembly) whenever prompts or retrieved content are not fully trusted.
paraphrase: To the agent, a "tool" is just a Python function with type hints and a clear docstring, exposed through a small decorator. The agent reads the function's name, types, and docstring to decide whether the tool fits the current step, then calls it like any other function. Two practical beginner notes follow. First, the docstring is part of the tool's contract — a vague docstring leads the agent to misuse the tool. Second, smolagents' code-writing agent can run Python that the model itself generates, so for any prompt that is not fully trusted the developer should run the agent inside an isolated environment rather than on their own workstation. This is the chapter's safety caveat anchor and motivates the "human control / autonomy" discussion.

### entry-061
source: smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv`; verified by master via `python -c "import smolagents; ..."` and `inspect.signature` on 2026-08-01. Supersedes entry-006's API claim about smolagents 1.24.0.
used_in: ch-01 (corrective), ch-08 (operative)
claim/finding: The canonical model-import path for the Hugging Face Inference API in smolagents 1.26.0 is `from smolagents import ApiModel`, not `HfApiModel`. Importing `HfApiModel` raises `ImportError: cannot import name 'HfApiModel' from 'smolagents'. Did you mean: 'ApiModel'?`. The rename is the only API-surface breaking change relevant to ch-08's "first agent" example between the 1.24.0 docs cited in entry-006 and the 1.26.0 actually installed. All other model classes the book will use are present under the same name: `InferenceClientModel`, `OpenAIModel`, `AzureOpenAIModel`, `AmazonBedrockModel`, `LiteLLMModel`, `OpenAIServerModel`. The agent classes `CodeAgent`, `ToolCallingAgent`, and `MultiStepAgent` are all present in 1.26.0 with new constructor parameters (`instructions=`, `add_base_tools=`, `managed_agents=`, `planning_interval=`, `provide_run_summary=`, `return_full_result=`, and the `executor_type='local' | 'blaxel' | 'e2b' | 'modal' | 'docker'` switch on `CodeAgent`); the book's ch-10, ch-11, ch-14, and ch-15 chapters will use these new parameters.
paraphrase: When the book introduces the first smolagents agent in ch-08, the import line must read `from smolagents import CodeAgent, ApiModel`, and the model argument must be `ApiModel(...)` rather than `HfApiModel(...)`. The rename is the only API change between smolagents 1.24.0 and 1.26.0 that affects a beginner's first program; everything else (agent classes, the `tool` decorator, the built-in `DuckDuckGoSearchTool`, the local Python executor, the planning interval, the `managed_agents` mechanism) is either identical or has only additive parameters. Phase 6 chapter writer should run the ch-08 example code inside `E:\book_gen\.venv` to verify it executes against smolagents 1.26.0 before writing the manuscript. **Prose requirement (one-time, ch-08 only):** the first prose paragraph that imports smolagents in ch-08 MUST include a short "Naming note" or sidebar — about three sentences — that tells the reader `HfApiModel` was the old name in earlier versions of smolagents and is now `ApiModel`, that this is the only rename the book needs to flag, and that later chapters will use `ApiModel` without repeating the note. The callout appears exactly once in the entire book, in ch-08, and is not repeated in ch-09..ch-18.

## ch-02 — Set Up a Cross-Platform Workspace

### entry-009
source: Python Software Foundation — "Active Python releases" and the per-release `Requires Python` metadata on the smolagents PyPI project page (https://pypi.org/project/smolagents/) combined with chub entry `smolagents/package` (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/), smolagents==1.24.0 documented 2026-03-12; PyPI now shows smolagents 1.26.0 released 2026-05-29 — both versions list `Requires: Python >=3.10`.
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: Picks the minimum Python version ch-02 must recommend so that every later chapter (ch-07 onward) can import smolagents without re-installing. The actively supported Python versions per python.org/downloads are 3.10 (security status, end of support 2026-10), 3.11 (security, 2027-10), 3.12 (security, 2028-10), 3.13 (bugfix, 2029-10), 3.14 (bugfix, planned 2030-10); 3.9 is end-of-life. smolagents 1.24.0 and 1.26.0 both require `Python >=3.10`, so a safe beginner recommendation is any 3.10+ actively supported release, with 3.12 or 3.13 as the durable default. ch-02 should record the version on disk so the reader can repeat the install later.
paraphrase: Python 3.10, 3.11, 3.12, 3.13, and 3.14 are all currently in active support, with 3.9 having reached end-of-life. smolagents lists "Requires: Python >=3.10" on its PyPI metadata, so the book needs at least 3.10 to run any later agent example. A sensible default for beginners is the latest 3.13 bugfix release (the most recent stable line with a long support window that is unlikely to surprise authors of third-party packages); 3.12 is the most conservative pick if a reader needs maximum package compatibility. The chapter should record the exact version chosen so that "reinstall later" remains reproducible.

### entry-010
source: Python Software Foundation — "Using Python on Windows, 4.1 Python install manager" (https://docs.python.org/3/using/windows.html#python-install-manager), Python 3.14.6 documentation, last updated 2026-07-31.
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: The cross-platform beginner setup for Windows now centres on the Python Install Manager (MSIX installer) available from python.org/downloads or the Microsoft Store, which registers the `python` and `py` shell commands. The older "Python launcher for Windows" (`py.exe`) and the "full installer" are explicitly marked deprecated in the same docs section. Default user-script `PATH` entry on Windows is `%LocalAppData%\Python\bin`. ch-02 must flag the Windows installer change as age-sensitive: any prior tutorial that says "tick 'Add Python to PATH' in the legacy installer" is now outdated.
paraphrase: On Windows, the recommended way to install Python is to download the Python Install Manager from python.org or install the Python (default) app from the Microsoft Store. Once installed, the `python` and `py` commands become available in any terminal. By default, package install scripts end up under `%LocalAppData%\Python\bin`, which the user is prompted to add to their `PATH` so that CLI tools installed with pip (such as `jupyter`) are usable. The previous "Python launcher for Windows" and the legacy full installer are deprecated, so book instructions should not lean on them. ch-02 should call out that this section may age quickly because Microsoft Store and python.org URLs change.

### entry-011
source: Python Software Foundation — "Using Python on macOS, 5.1 Installation steps" (https://docs.python.org/3/using/mac.html#installation-steps), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: The macOS chapter should standardise on the official python.org macOS installer (.pkg), which ships as a universal2 binary that runs natively on both Apple Silicon and Intel Macs from macOS 10.15 Catalina onwards. The installer is signed and notarised with the Python Software Foundation Apple Developer ID; it installs the Python framework into `/Library/Frameworks/Python.framework` and places command-line symlinks under `/usr/local/bin` (typically already on `PATH`). The macOS-provided system Python (`/usr/bin/python3`) is an older release and should not be used for the book.
paraphrase: On macOS, the recommended beginner path is to download the "Python for macOS" installer (.pkg) from python.org and double-click it. The package is signed and notarised by the Python Software Foundation, so Gatekeeper will accept it. It installs the Python interpreter into `/Library/Frameworks/Python.framework` and adds the version-specific binaries (for example `python3.14`) to `/usr/local/bin`, which is on the default shell `PATH`. The user can then run `python3.14` from Terminal. Beginners should not rely on the `/usr/bin/python3` that macOS already ships, because Apple updates it on its own schedule and it is typically older than the version the book will use.

### entry-012
source: Python Software Foundation — "Using Python on Unix platforms, 2.1 Getting and installing the latest version of Python" (https://docs.python.org/3/using/unix.html#getting-and-installing-the-latest-version-of-python), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the smolagents PyPI metadata (`Requires Python >=3.10`).
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: On Linux there is no single python.org installer; beginners either use the distro's package manager (apt/dnf/zypper/apk) or install via a third-party distribution such as the `deadsnakes` PPA (Ubuntu) or pyenv. The Python docs explicitly say "Python comes preinstalled on most Linux distributions, and is available as a package on all others," but warn that distro packages often lag the latest release. ch-02 should advise beginners to first check what the distro provides, then upgrade only if that version is below 3.10 (the smolagents floor).
paraphrase: On Linux, Python is usually pre-installed or one `apt install` / `dnf install` / `zypper install` / `apk add` away. The Python docs acknowledge that distribution packages do not always track the latest release, so a beginner whose distro ships Python 3.9 or lower will not be able to run smolagents. If the system Python is too old, the beginner has three realistic choices: install a newer `python3.x` from a third-party source (for example, the `deadsnakes` PPA on Ubuntu), use a tool like pyenv to build a user-local interpreter, or build Python from source. The chapter should recommend the first option that does not require administrator privileges, and keep the version-pinning explicit.

### entry-013
source: Python Software Foundation — "Virtual Environments and Packages, 12.2 Creating Virtual Environments" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/venv.html#creating-virtual-environments), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Python Packaging Authority "Install packages in a virtual environment using pip and venv" (https://packaging.python.org/en/latest/guides/installing-using-pip-and-virtual-environments/).
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: `venv` is the standard-library tool for isolating per-project dependencies, and the docs agree on the directory name `.venv` and on the activation command being different per OS. The Python tutorial shows `python -m venv tutorial-env` and then activates with `tutorial-env\Scripts\activate` on Windows and `source tutorial-env/bin/activate` on Unix/macOS. Activation is shell-specific (.bat/Activate.ps1 on Windows, `activate` on bash, `activate.csh`/`activate.fish` for csh/fish). The PyPA guide confirms activation prefixes the shell with the venv name and that `which python` (Unix) / `where python` (Windows) will point to the venv path while it is active.
paraphrase: A virtual environment is a self-contained directory that contains a copy of the Python interpreter and only the packages installed into that environment. The standard recommendation is to create one per project with `python -m venv .venv`, place the directory inside the project folder, and name it `.venv` so it stays hidden and does not collide with `.env` files. Activation differs by platform: on Windows the command is `.venv\Scripts\activate` (in PowerShell or cmd), and on macOS/Linux it is `source .venv/bin/activate` (with separate `activate.csh` and `activate.fish` scripts for csh and fish shells). Beginners can verify the environment is active by running `where python` on Windows or `which python` on macOS/Linux and seeing the path end in `.venv`. The active environment is restored to the system default by running `deactivate`.

### entry-014
source: Python Packaging Authority — "Install packages in a virtual environment using pip and venv" (https://packaging.python.org/en/latest/guides/installing-using-pip-and-virtual-environments/) and chub entry `pip/package` (https://pip.pypa.io/), cross-referenced with the smolagents install line in chub entry `smolagents/package`.
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: Inside an active `.venv`, the canonical install command is `python -m pip install <package>`. The PyPA guide recommends pinning (`pip install "smolagents==1.26.0"`) for reproducibility and warns that on Linux `pip` may not be installed by default (the user may need `sudo apt install python3-pip` or equivalent). For the book, the chapter's only required package at the ch-02 stage is `smolagents`; JupyterLab is needed for the notebook examples and is installed the same way.
paraphrase: Once a virtual environment is active, every package install should be run as `python -m pip install <package>` rather than as a bare `pip install`. This guarantees the package ends up inside the active `.venv` rather than in the system Python. The PyPA guide recommends pinning a version (`smolagents==1.26.0`) so the reader can reproduce the exact environment a chapter was written against. On Linux, the system Python may not have pip installed; the user has to install it through the distro package manager (`python3-pip` on Debian/Ubuntu, equivalent on others) before `python -m pip` works. The chapter should show this `python -m pip` form on every platform rather than teaching platform-specific shortcuts.

### entry-015
source: Jupyter Project — "Installing Jupyter" (https://jupyter.org/install), JupyterLab 4.5.x docs (chub entry `jupyterlab/package`, jupyterlab==4.5.6, updated 2026-03-12), and PyPA venv guide.
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: JupyterLab is the official Jupyter "notebook-style" UI for the book, and is installed into the same `.venv` as the rest of the project. The official install line is `pip install jupyterlab` followed by `jupyter lab` to launch. The JupyterLab chub entry warns that "JupyterLab only provides the UI and server shell; your code executes in a kernel environment" — so the kernel must be installed in the same `.venv` that holds the project's packages, or imports will fail. For a beginner, the chapter should also show the alternative: `pip install notebook` plus `jupyter notebook` for the classic notebook UI; both ship from the same Jupyter organisation.
paraphrase: JupyterLab is the standard Jupyter "notebook" application and is installed into the active `.venv` with `pip install jupyterlab`, then started by running `jupyter lab` from the project directory. The most common beginner failure is to install JupyterLab into one environment and the project's packages into another; because the actual code cells run in a kernel bound to a specific Python interpreter, packages visible in the active environment are the ones the notebook can import. The chapter should explicitly install JupyterLab into the same `.venv` as smolagents and explain that the "default" kernel in the launcher is the one tied to the active interpreter. The older `jupyter notebook` command is also available for readers who prefer the classic UI.

### entry-016
source: Python Software Foundation — "Command line and environment, 1.1 Command line" (https://docs.python.org/3/using/cmdline.html#command-line), Python 3.14.6 documentation; combined with the ch-02 project-folder convention.
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: The three execution modes beginners will meet are: (a) `python -m <module>` (run a module as a script, e.g. `python -m smolagents.cli` or `python -m http.server`), (b) `python <script>.py` (run a saved file), and (c) `python -c "code"` (run a single statement). The tutorial and the Python docs prefer `python -m` over `python script.py` for two reasons: the launcher works regardless of the current directory's contents, and it avoids accidentally running a same-named local file that shadows a stdlib module. For notebooks, the execution model is cell-by-cell inside the JupyterLab kernel.
paraphrase: Beginners will see four ways to "run Python" in this book. From a terminal, `python script.py` runs a saved Python file; `python -m module` runs a module by name (which is the preferred form for tool invocations and library CLIs because it cannot be hijacked by a same-named local file); and `python -c "code"` runs a tiny snippet inline. From inside JupyterLab, the equivalent is a code cell, which runs in the kernel bound to the active `.venv`. The chapter should teach `python -m` as the default form (it is what `python -m pip install` and `python -m venv` already use) and reserve `python script.py` for saved projects.

### entry-017
source: chub entry `python-dotenv/package` (python-dotenv==1.2.2, updated 2026-03-12) — https://github.com/theskumar/python-dotenv — cross-referenced with the Python docs caution on `PYTHONPATH` in the macOS shell-profile section (https://docs.python.org/3/using/mac.html), the Python FAQ on simple security models (https://docs.python.org/3/faq/general.html), and the smolagents secure-code-execution guide (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.24.0/en/tutorials/secure_code_execution).
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: The beginner-friendly way to handle development secrets is to keep them in a `.env` file at the project root and load them with `python-dotenv`'s `load_dotenv()`. The chub entry on python-dotenv documents the golden rule: `load_dotenv()` does NOT override existing real environment variables unless `override=True` is passed, and it walks upward from the script directory looking for a `.env`. A second API, `dotenv_values()`, returns a dict without mutating `os.environ`, which is useful when the reader wants explicit control. The Python docs and the smolagents secure-execution guide agree on three safety principles that follow from this: (1) never put secrets in source code, in notebooks, or in commands typed into the shell history; (2) keep them in `.env` files or real environment variables, both of which are outside the manuscript and the repository; (3) keep the `.env` file out of version control with `.gitignore`. The smolagents guide adds a related but separate point: `CodeAgent` can execute model-generated Python, so secrets on the developer's machine are reachable by any prompt that a malicious agent can read — the book should defer deeper defences (sandboxing, scoped keys, separate accounts) to ch-14.
paraphrase: `.env` files are the standard way to hold development credentials (Hugging Face tokens, OpenAI keys, etc.) outside of source code. The `python-dotenv` package reads a `.env` file and copies its key=value pairs into `os.environ`, where the rest of the program can read them with `os.environ["HF_TOKEN"]` or `os.getenv("HF_TOKEN")`. Because `load_dotenv()` does not overwrite real environment variables unless explicitly told to, the production environment always wins over the local file — which is the safe default. The chapter must therefore teach three rules at once: keep secrets out of `.py` files and notebook cells, store them in either real environment variables or a local `.env` file, and add `.env` to `.gitignore` while shipping a `.env.example` template with placeholder values so newcomers see the shape of the variables without ever seeing real secrets. A first note that deeper protections belong to ch-14 keeps ch-02 scoped.

### entry-018
source: Python Packaging Authority — "Tutorial: Installing Packages / pyproject.toml" (https://packaging.python.org/en/latest/tutorials/installing-packages/) and the GitHub gitignore repo "Python.gitignore" (https://github.com/github/gitignore/blob/main/Python.gitignore), 2026.
used_in: ch-02
claim/finding: The project-folder convention appropriate for a total beginner is to keep one project per folder, with the `.venv` and any `.env` listed in `.gitignore` inside that folder. The GitHub-curated Python `.gitignore` is the canonical template and lists `.venv/`, `venv/`, `env/`, `.env`, `__pycache__/`, `*.py[cod]`, `*$py.class`, and `*.egg-info/` among the standard exclusions. The chapter should keep the folder layout minimal: a top-level `README.md`, the `.venv/` directory, a `.env` (gitignored) plus `.env.example` (committed), and a `src/` or root-level `.py`/`ipynb` files for the actual code. A `pyproject.toml` from the PyPA tutorial is recommended for any project that grows beyond one notebook, but it is not required for ch-02.
paraphrase: A beginner-friendly Python project should be one folder per project. Inside that folder, the conventional `.gitignore` excludes the virtual environment (`.venv/`), the secrets file (`.env`), Python bytecode caches (`__pycache__/`, `*.py[cod]`), and build artefacts (`*.egg-info/`). The committed artefacts are a `README.md` describing the project, a `.env.example` placeholder file, the source code (a mix of `.py` scripts and `.ipynb` notebooks as the chapter requires), and optionally a `pyproject.toml` once the project grows beyond one script. The chapter should give the literal `.gitignore` lines so beginners copy them verbatim, and should not invent extra configuration files beyond the standard Python packaging layout.

## ch-03 — Write Your First Python Programs

### entry-019
source: Python Software Foundation — “Objects, values and types— (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/datamodel.html#objects-values-and-types), “Naming and binding— (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/executionmodel.html#naming-and-binding), “Assignment statements— (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/simple_stmts.html#assignment-statements), and PEP 8 “Function and Variable Names— (https://peps.python.org/pep-0008/#function-and-variable-names), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: Establishes the accurate beginner mental model for variables, assignment, and dynamic typing. Python data is represented by objects; every object has a type and value; a name refers to an object; and assignment evaluates the right-hand side and binds (or rebinds) the name on the left. The name itself does not permanently acquire the object’s type, so the same name can later be rebound to an object of another type. PEP 8 prescribes lowercase variable names with words separated by underscores.
paraphrase: Treat a variable as a readable label attached to a Python object, not as a typed box. Assignment with `=` attaches a name to the resulting object; assigning that name again moves the label to another object. The object has the type, and `type()` can reveal it; Python does not require a type declaration before assignment. Use descriptive lowercase names such as `trip_distance`, with underscores between words, rather than unexplained one-letter names. Keep `=` distinct from `==`: the first assigns, while the second compares values.

### entry-020
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, “Numbers— and “Text— (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/introduction.html#numbers and https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/introduction.html#text), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: Supplies the chapter’s smallest useful literal and expression set. Whole-number literals produce `int` objects, decimal-point literals produce `float` objects, and text enclosed in matching single or double quotes produces `str` objects; digits inside quotes are text, not a number. The tutorial defines `+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `//`, `%`, and `**`, parentheses for grouping, string concatenation with `+`, string repetition with `*`, and escape sequences such as `\n`. Ordinary `/` always returns a float; `//` is floor division.
paraphrase: Begin with only three value categories: integers, decimal numbers, and strings. An expression combines values and operators to produce a new value. Arithmetic follows normal precedence, with parentheses available when intent should be explicit. Operators depend on type: `+` adds numbers but joins two strings, while adding a string to a number is invalid. Single and double quotes create the same kind of string, so choose the form that avoids unnecessary escaping; introduce `\n` only as the basic newline escape.

### entry-021
source: Python Software Foundation — built-in `print()` documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#print), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: Supports the required canonical first program and clarifies output semantics. `print()` converts its arguments to text, writes them to standard output, separates multiple arguments with a space by default, and ends with a newline by default. A bare expression is not automatically displayed when it appears in a saved script, so explicit output is the portable teaching habit across scripts and notebooks.
paraphrase: The canonical first program is `print("Hello, world!")`. It is useful because one line verifies the whole beginner workflow: Python can parse the file or cell, call a built-in function, and display predictable text. Teach the default one-argument form first. Mention `sep` and `end` only as optional controls after the default behavior is understood; they are not needed for the first program.

### entry-022
source: Python Software Foundation — PEP 498, “Literal String Interpolation— (https://peps.python.org/pep-0498/), status Final, Python-Version 3.6; cross-checked against this book’s established Python 3.10+ floor in `books/ai-agents-with-python/research-log.md:68-72`.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: PEP 498 introduced formatted string literals (f-strings) in Python 3.6, so the complete 3.10+ range supported by this book includes them. Prefixing a string literal with `f` allows expressions inside braces; those expressions are evaluated at runtime and replaced by their formatted values. Doubled braces represent literal braces. No smolagents or other third-party API is involved.
paraphrase: Use f-strings as the first and default interpolation technique: put an `f` before the opening quote and place a previously defined name or small expression inside braces. The result is an ordinary string, which can be passed to `print()`. Keep initial examples to simple name substitution and one small arithmetic expression so they work unchanged at the book’s Python 3.10 floor; defer advanced conversions and format specifications until a real need appears.

### entry-023
source: Python Software Foundation — built-in `input()`, `int()`, and `float()` documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#input, https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#int, and https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#float), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: Defines beginner interactivity without hiding type conversion. `input()` optionally displays a prompt without a trailing newline, reads one line, strips its trailing newline, and always returns a `str`. Numeric-looking input therefore remains text until explicitly converted with `int()` or `float()`; unsuitable text passed to a numeric conversion raises `ValueError`.
paraphrase: Store the result of `input()` in a well-named variable, then print it or include it in an f-string. Emphasize that typing digits does not make the result numeric: convert only when the program genuinely needs arithmetic. This directly explains why joining input text to another string works while trying to add that text to a number raises a type-related error. Reading the resulting error belongs here; catching or recovering from it belongs in a later chapter.

### entry-024
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, “Errors and Exceptions— (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/errors.html), “Resolution of names— (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/executionmodel.html#resolution-of-names), and built-in exception definitions (https://docs.python.org/3/library/exceptions.html#concrete-exceptions), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: Distinguishes the four required beginner error categories and gives a reliable traceback-reading method. `SyntaxError` means the parser could not understand the program; its caret marks where detection occurred, which can be after the actual mistake. `IndentationError` is the `SyntaxError` subclass for incorrect indentation, while inconsistent tabs and spaces produce its `TabError` subclass. `NameError` means name resolution found no binding, often because of a typo or use before assignment. `TypeError` means an operation or function received an object of an inappropriate type. For runtime exceptions, the final line states the exception type and message; preceding frames identify files, line numbers, and source lines.
paraphrase: Teach beginners to read an error from the bottom up: first identify the exception name and plain-language message on the final line, then move to the nearest frame from their own file or notebook cell and inspect the marked expression. For a syntax error, use the reported file, line, and caret as a starting point but also inspect immediately before the caret for a missing quote, parenthesis, or punctuation. Map likely first checks to the four names: spelling/assignment for `NameError`, punctuation for `SyntaxError`, leading whitespace for `IndentationError`, and the types on both sides of an operation for `TypeError`. The goal is interpretation, not exception-handling syntax.

### entry-025
source: Python Software Foundation — “Using the Python Interpreter— (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/interpreter.html) and command-line interface options (https://docs.python.org/3/using/cmdline.html#interface-options), Python 3.14.6; Jupyter Project — Notebook 7.6.1 “The Jupyter Notebook— and “Running Code— (https://jupyter-notebook.readthedocs.io/en/stable/notebook.html and https://jupyter-notebook.readthedocs.io/en/stable/examples/Notebook/Running%20Code.html); chub entry `notebook/package` (Notebook 7.5.5, updated 2026-03-12), package behavior validated.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: A `.py` file passed to the Python interpreter is parsed as a complete non-interactive input and then executed as a script; each terminal invocation provides a clean run through the saved file. A Jupyter notebook is a sequence of cells. `Shift-Enter` sends one code cell to a separate kernel and displays its returned output in the notebook; that kernel preserves variables and other computation state until it is restarted. This persistence enables incremental experiments but can conceal an out-of-order dependency that a clean script run would expose.
paraphrase: Run the same tiny programs in both formats established by ch-02. Use the activated environment’s Python command followed by the `.py` filename for a reproducible top-to-bottom script run. In a notebook, run one code cell with `Shift-Enter` and observe its execution count and output. Because later cells can reuse state from earlier executions, a notebook that appears to work may depend on stale state; restarting the kernel and executing cells from top to bottom is the minimal reproducibility check. Use `print()` when output should behave the same in both formats, since notebooks may also display a final bare expression automatically.
variance_resolution: The current official Notebook page identifies 7.6.1 while chub’s maintainer package guide records 7.5.5. Their cell/kernel execution model agrees; use the current official UI behavior and retain chub only as validation of the package-level kernel distinction. This patch-version lag is not a material contradiction.

### entry-026
source: Python Software Foundation — PEP 8 “Indentation,— “Whitespace in Expressions and Statements,— “Function and Variable Names,— and “String Quotes— (https://peps.python.org/pep-0008/#indentation, https://peps.python.org/pep-0008/#whitespace-in-expressions-and-statements, https://peps.python.org/pep-0008/#function-and-variable-names, and https://peps.python.org/pep-0008/#string-quotes), status Active.
used_in: ch-03
claim/finding: The beginner-relevant subset of PEP 8 is small: use four spaces per indentation level, never mix tabs and spaces, put ordinary spaces around assignment and binary operators, avoid padding just inside parentheses, use lowercase underscore-separated variable names, and choose readable names. PEP 8 does not prefer single over double quotes; consistency is the relevant rule. Its full import, class, function, wrapping, and tooling guidance is beyond this chapter’s needs.
paraphrase: Give learners a short formatting checklist rather than the whole style guide: meaningful `snake_case` names, one space around `=` and arithmetic operators, no spaces immediately inside parentheses, and four spaces whenever a later indented block appears. Either quote style is valid; use one consistently within a small example. Do not require a formatter, linter, type annotations, or advanced PEP 8 rules to complete these first programs”the purpose of style here is to make errors easier to see and code easier to read.

## ch-04 — Make Programs Decide and Repeat

### entry-027
source: Python Software Foundation — Built-in Types, "Boolean Type - `bool`" and "Truth Value Testing" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#boolean-type-bool and https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#truth-value-testing), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Anchors the chapter's "boolean" mental model. The `bool` type has exactly two constant instances, `True` and `False`. `bool` is a subclass of `int`, so `False` and `True` behave like the integers 0 and 1 in numeric contexts (the docs explicitly say "relying on this is discouraged; explicitly convert using `int()` instead"). The truth-value section makes the precise rule that any object can be tested in a condition: an object is considered false only if its class defines a `__bool__()` that returns `False` or a `__len__()` that returns zero. The enumerated false values that beginners will actually meet are `None`, `False`, zero of any numeric type (`0`, `0.0`, `0j`), and the empty sequences/collections `''`, `()` , `[]`, `{}`, `set()`, and `range(0)`. Operations and built-ins that produce a Boolean result return `0`/`False` or `1`/`True` — except the operators `and` and `or`, which return one of their operands.
paraphrase: A boolean is a value that is either `True` or `False`. In Python, almost any object can be used where a condition is expected, not just booleans: an object is considered "falsey" only when it is the constant `None` or `False`, when it is numeric zero, or when it is empty (the empty string `''`, empty tuple `()`, empty list `[]`, empty dict `{}`, empty `set()`, or `range(0)`). Anything else is "truthy". Even though `True` and `False` are technically integers, the chapter should tell beginners to write `int(True)` if they really need a number, rather than rely on that quirk. The chapter should also use this list of false values as the reason a beginner can write `if name:` to mean "name is not the empty string" and `if items:` to mean "items is not empty".

### entry-028
source: Python Software Foundation — Built-in Types, "Comparisons" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#comparisons), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Language Reference, "Expressions" > "Comparisons" (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/expressions.html#comparisons), Python 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Defines the six beginner comparison operators and the chaining rule. Python has eight comparison operations: `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=`, `==`, `!=`, plus `is` and `is not` for object identity. All eight have the same priority (higher than the Boolean operations). `==` always returns a result but for some objects is equivalent to `is`; `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` raise `TypeError` when given a complex number. Objects of different types never compare equal unless one of them defines the comparison. Comparisons can be chained: `x < y <= z` is equivalent to `x < y and y <= z`, except that `y` is evaluated only once and `z` is not evaluated at all when `x < y` is false.
paraphrase: The six beginner-facing comparison operators are `==` (equal), `!=` (not equal), `<` (less than), `<=` (less than or equal), `>` (greater than), and `>=` (greater than or equal). Two objects of different types never compare equal unless one explicitly says so. Two extra operators, `is` and `is not`, mean "the same object in memory" and are not the same as `==`; beginners should default to `==`/`!=` for value comparisons and treat `is` as a separate tool to introduce only when it matters. Python also lets the user chain comparisons so that `0 <= score <= 100` reads as in ordinary math and stops as soon as one part is false. The chapter should teach the chaining form because it makes range checks easier to read and prevents the common bug of writing `score >= 0 and score <= 100` correctly in one place and then dropping the `<=` somewhere else.

### entry-029
source: Python Software Foundation — Built-in Types, "Boolean Operations — `and`, `or`, `not`" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#boolean-operations-and-or-not), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Language Reference, "Expressions" > "Boolean operations" (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/expressions.html#boolean-operations), Python 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Defines the beginner-relevant semantics of `and`, `or`, `not` and the short-circuit gotcha that bites even beginners. The result table is: `x or y` returns `x` if `x` is true, otherwise `y`; `x and y` returns `x` if `x` is false, otherwise `y`; `not x` returns `True` if `x` is false, otherwise `False`. `or` and `and` are short-circuit operators: `or` only evaluates its right operand if the left is false, and `and` only evaluates its right operand if the left is true. `not` has lower priority than non-boolean operators, so `not a == b` is parsed as `not (a == b)` and `a == not b` is a syntax error.
paraphrase: The three boolean operators are `and`, `or`, and `not`. `and` and `or` short-circuit: the right-hand side is only evaluated when the left-hand side does not already determine the answer. This matters for two reasons beginners will meet in this chapter. First, it is the reason the program can safely write `if user is not None and user.age >= 18:` — the second comparison is never run when `user` is `None`, so no `AttributeError` is raised. Second, the operators return one of their operands, not a forced `True` or `False`, so beginners should not rely on the return value being a `bool`; if they need a strict boolean, they should wrap it in `bool(...)`. The chapter also needs to teach that `not` binds loosely, so `not a == b` should be read as "it is not the case that `a == b`" rather than as "a is not equal to b" (the latter is the same answer but the loose-binding reading is the sound one to teach). The official guide warns against using the bitwise forms `&`, `|`, `^` for boolean logic; the chapter should not introduce them at all at this level.

### entry-030
source: Python Software Foundation — Language Reference, "Compound statements" (introductory section) and "8.1. The `if` statement" (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html#compound-statements and https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-if-statement), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Python Tutorial, "4.1. `if` Statements" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html#if-statements), Python 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Provides the exact syntax and indentation rules for the chapter's `if`/`elif`/`else` statements. A compound statement is one or more clauses; each clause consists of a header and a "suite". All clause headers of a single compound statement are at the same indentation level, each header begins with a uniquely identifying keyword, and each header ends with a colon. A suite is a group of statements controlled by a clause and is either semicolon-separated simple statements on the same line as the header or one or more indented statements on subsequent lines. Only the indented form can contain nested compound statements. The `if` statement selects exactly one of its suites by evaluating the expressions one by one until one is true; the `else` clause, if present, runs when all expressions are false. `elif` is short for "else if" and an `if`/`elif`/`elif` chain is a substitute for the `switch`/`case` statements found in other languages.
paraphrase: An `if` statement is built from a header line that ends with a colon and a block of indented statements underneath. The header is `if <expression>:`, optionally followed by any number of `elif <expression>:` lines, and optionally ending with a single `else:`. Python evaluates the expressions in order and runs the body of the first one that is true; if none is true, it runs the `else` body; if there is no `else`, nothing runs. The chapter's three indentation rules are: every statement in one body must be indented by the same amount, the body must be indented more than the header, and a new block must consistently stay at the same deeper level until the next block of the same shallower level. Mixing tabs and spaces in one file is a `TabError`; the beginner setup should use four spaces per level (already established in entry-026). Putting more than one short statement on one line after a colon is legal but discouraged for beginners, and nested compound statements always require the indented form. The chapter should reach for this concrete mental model rather than handing beginners the BNF grammar.

### entry-031
source: Python Software Foundation — Language Reference, "8.2. The `while` statement" (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-while-statement), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Python Tutorial, "4.4. `break` and `continue` Statements" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html#break-and-continue-statements), Python 3.14.6; and the simple-statement references for `break` and `continue` (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/simple_stmts.html#break and https://docs.python.org/3/reference/simple_stmts.html#continue).
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Anchors the `while` loop, the two loop-exit/loop-skip statements, and the safe-iteration pattern that beginners will use. The `while` statement repeatedly tests an expression and runs the first suite while the expression is true; if the expression is false on the first test the suite is never executed. A `break` statement inside the first suite terminates the loop without executing the optional `else` clause's suite; a `continue` statement skips the rest of the suite and goes back to testing the expression. From the tutorial, `break` breaks out of the **innermost** enclosing `for` or `while` loop, and `continue` continues with the next iteration of that loop. The simple-statement docs specify that `break` and `continue` may only appear inside a `for` or `while` loop; using them elsewhere is a `SyntaxError`.
paraphrase: A `while` loop runs the indented body as long as the header expression is true. The two safe-exit tools are `break`, which ends the loop immediately, and `continue`, which skips the rest of the body and jumps to the next test. The simplest beginner pattern is "loop until condition, then `break`": a `while True:` block that contains a `break` when the stopping condition is met, which avoids the most common beginner error of forgetting to advance the loop variable. The chapter should also explicitly say that `break` and `continue` only work inside a loop; using them at top level is a `SyntaxError`. The accompanying `else` clause on a loop ("run only if no `break` happened") is a useful idiom but a beginner-beginner trap if introduced too early; recommend deferring it past the first chapter on loops.

### entry-032
source: Python Software Foundation — Language Reference, "8.3. The `for` statement" (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-for-statement), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Python Tutorial, "4.2. `for` Statements" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html#for-statements), Python 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Defines the beginner-facing `for` loop over strings and lists. The `for` statement evaluates the iterable expression once, gets an iterator from it, and assigns successive items to the target list using the same rules as assignment, executing the suite for each item. When the iterator is exhausted, the optional `else` suite runs and the loop terminates. The tutorial states the difference from C/Pascal: "Python's `for` statement iterates over the items of any sequence (a list or a string), in the order that they appear in the sequence," rather than over an arithmetic progression of indices. The tutorial also warns that code that modifies a collection while iterating over that same collection "can be tricky to get right" and recommends iterating over a copy or building a new collection.
paraphrase: Use `for` to repeat a body once for each item in a sequence (a string, a list, a tuple, or any other iterable). The header `for <name> in <sequence>:` runs the body once per item, in the order the items appear, and on each iteration the loop variable is bound to the current item using ordinary assignment rules. The chapter's first collection-iteration example should iterate character-by-character over a string (`for ch in "hello":`) and then iterate over a list of names (`for name in names:`), so beginners learn the verb "iterate" alongside the syntax. The chapter should also warn against changing the same collection while iterating over it — adding or removing items mid-loop is a beginner footgun that the docs explicitly call out. The most useful beginner composition is `for i in range(len(items)):` paired with `items[i]`, but the tutorial already flags that `enumerate(...)` is the more idiomatic form and the chapter should mention that as a hint rather than the recommended first form.

### entry-033
source: Python Software Foundation — Built-in Types, "Ranges" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#ranges), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Python Tutorial, "4.3. The `range()` Function" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html#the-range-function), Python 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Defines the three `range()` constructor forms and the half-open interval rule that is the chapter's main off-by-one prevention. The `range` type is an immutable sequence of numbers commonly used for looping a specific number of times. The constructors are `range(stop)` and `range(start, stop, step=1)`. The arguments must be integers (or any object that implements `__index__()`); the `step` defaults to `1` and the `start` defaults to `0`; if `step` is zero, `ValueError` is raised. For a positive `step`, the contents are `r[i] = start + step*i` with `i >= 0` and `r[i] < stop`; for a negative `step`, the constraint is `r[i] > stop`. The tutorial makes the rule explicit: "the given end point is never part of the generated sequence; `range(10)` generates 10 values, the legal indices for items of a sequence of length 10." The tutorial also shows that `range(0, 10, 3)` yields `[0, 3, 6, 9]` and `range(-10, -100, -30)` yields `[-10, -40, -70]`, so negative `step` works as expected. Because `range` is an immutable sequence, an empty range is falsey via the truth-value-testing rule in entry-027.
paraphrase: `range(stop)` produces 0, 1, 2, … up to but not including `stop`. `range(start, stop)` produces values starting from `start` and stopping before `stop`. `range(start, stop, step)` adds the step `step` between successive values, which can be negative to count downward. The stop value is always excluded, which is the chapter's main off-by-one beat: `range(5)` has five values (0 through 4), `range(1, 6)` also has five values (1 through 5), and `range(10, 0, -1)` counts 10 down to 1. Beginners should know that `range` does not actually build the list in memory; it is a small object that produces each value on demand, so iterating the same range twice is cheap and printing it directly shows the representation like `range(0, 10)`. The chapter should establish the half-open rule as the source of the off-by-one error to mention in entry-034, and mention that `range(0)` is empty and therefore falsey once the truthiness rule is in place.

### entry-034
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, "4.4. `break` and `continue` Statements" and "4.2. `for` Statements" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html#break-and-continue-statements and https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/controlflow.html#for-statements), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Language Reference, "8.2. The `while` statement" and "8.3. The `for` statement" (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-while-statement and https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-for-statement), and the Assignment statements reference (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/simple_stmts.html#assignment-statements), Python 3.14.6.
used_in: ch-04
claim/finding: Catalogs the four beginner control-flow errors the chapter must give a name to. (a) Infinite loop: a `while` whose condition never becomes false because the body never changes the variable that the condition reads. The Language Reference's `while` statement makes the test explicit ("repeatedly tests the expression and, if it is true, executes the first suite"), so when the condition is `True` literally or always-true the loop never stops. The safe beginner pattern (`while True:` with an explicit `break`) is the language-reference-compliant way to avoid this. (b) Off-by-one: a `range` upper bound written as `range(n)` and used thinking it includes `n`, or a `while` loop that uses `<=` instead of `<` against the same bound. The Tutorial's ranged rule (`range(10)` "generates 10 values, the legal indices for items of a sequence of length 10") is the half-open contract the bug violates. (c) `=` vs `==`: assignment vs comparison. The Assignment statements reference describes `=` as rebinding the name on the left to the result of the right-hand side; a `while x = 5:` line is therefore a `SyntaxError` because assignment is not a condition. (d) Mutating a collection while iterating over it: the Tutorial's "for Statements" section explicitly warns that "code that modifies a collection while iterating over that same collection can be tricky to get right" and recommends iterating over a copy or building a new collection.
paraphrase: Name four beginner control-flow errors in the chapter and give each a one-line fingerprint. (1) The infinite loop: a `while` whose condition stays true forever, usually because the loop variable is never updated inside the body — the recommended safety pattern is `while True:` with an explicit `break`. (2) The off-by-one: a `for i in range(n)` written when the body intends to include `n`, or a `while i <= n` written when the half-open intent was `i < n`; the half-open rule of `range` is the chapter's anchor. (3) The `=` vs `==` mistake: writing `if x = 5:` instead of `if x == 5:`. Python raises `SyntaxError` rather than silently doing the wrong thing, so beginners can learn to read the error. (4) Mutating a list while iterating it (e.g. removing items during a `for item in items:` loop): the Tutorial itself warns that this is "tricky to get right" and recommends iterating over a copy or building a new collection. The chapter should give each error a memorable name, a one-line example of the bad line, and a one-line fix.
