## ch-05 — Work with Data and Files

### entry-035
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, "5.1. More on Lists" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/datastructures.html#more-on-lists) and the standard library "Sequence Types — list, tuple, range" / "Mutable Sequence Types" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#typesseq and https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#typesseq-mutable), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Anchors the chapter's beginner mental model for `list`. A list is a mutable sequence: ordered (items keep the order in which they were inserted), indexable by integer position starting at 0, and supporting `len()`, `in`/`not in`, indexing (`xs[i]`), and slicing (`xs[i:j]` and `xs[i:j:k]`) through the shared "Common Sequence Operations" table. The mutable list of methods to teach is small and beginner-safe: `append(x)` to add one item to the end (semantically equivalent to `xs[len(xs):] = [x]`), `extend(it)` to add many items from an iterable, `insert(i, x)` to put one item at a position, `remove(x)` to remove the first occurrence by value (raises `ValueError` if absent), `pop()` / `pop(i)` to remove and return by position, `sort()` to sort in place, and `reverse()` to flip in place. The tutorial explicitly recommends beginners reach for `list.append` rather than `xs.insert(0, x)` or `xs[len(xs):] = [x]` because those are equivalent but harder to read.
paraphrase: A list is an ordered, changeable collection of items. Items are written between square brackets and separated by commas, e.g. `tasks = ["buy milk", "email Ada", "fix bug"]`. Lists remember the order in which items were added, and items are accessed by integer position starting at zero (`tasks[0]` is the first). The most important beginner methods are: `tasks.append("task name")` to add one item to the end, `tasks.remove("buy milk")` to delete the first matching item, `tasks.pop()` to remove and return the last item, and `len(tasks)` to count items. The `in` keyword tests membership (`"buy milk" in tasks` is `True` or `False`). Indexing past the end of the list raises `IndexError`, and removing a value that is not in the list raises `ValueError`; both errors are recoverable once exceptions are taught. Lists can hold items of different types, but beginners should keep each list homogeneous for clarity.

### entry-036
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, "5.3. Tuples and Sequences" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/datastructures.html#tuples-and-sequences), cross-referenced with the standard library "Sequence Types — list, tuple, range" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#typesseq) and the "Tuples" subsection (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#tuples), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Defines the chapter's beginner model for `tuple`. A tuple is an ordered, immutable sequence, written as a comma-separated list with or without surrounding parentheses (`t = 12345, 54321, 'hello!'` and `t = (1, 2, 3)` both produce tuples). The tutorial makes the immutability rule explicit: assigning to a tuple element raises `TypeError: 'tuple' object does not support item assignment`. Tuples support the same indexing, slicing, `len()`, and `in`/`not in` operations as lists through the shared Common Sequence Operations table. A tuple that contains only strings, numbers, and other tuples is hashable and can therefore be used as a dict key (entry-035 is right that lists cannot); a tuple that contains a list cannot be used as a dict key because the list inside it is mutable. The tutorial also introduces sequence (un)packing: `a, b = b, a` swaps two values, and `name, score, *rest = record` splits a sequence into head and tail.
paraphrase: A tuple is an ordered collection that cannot be changed after it is created. The beginner-friendly rule of thumb is "use a list when the contents will change, use a tuple when they will not." A tuple is written with parentheses and commas: `point = (3, 5)`, or sometimes just with commas: `point = 3, 5`. The same indexing and slicing syntax as lists works on tuples (`point[0]` is `3`), and `for x in point:` iterates over the items. Tuples support one extra beginner-relevant trick: tuple unpacking, which lets a single line split a sequence into separate names, e.g. `name, score = ("Ada", 99)`. The chapter should mention that trying to assign to a tuple element raises a `TypeError`, and that a tuple containing only immutable items can be used as a dictionary key while a list cannot.

### entry-037
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, "5.4. Sets" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/datastructures.html#sets), cross-referenced with the standard library "Set Types — set, frozenset" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#set-types-set-frozenset), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Anchors the chapter's beginner model for `set`. A `set` is an unordered collection of distinct hashable objects, written with curly braces (`{1, 2, 3}`) or built with the `set()` constructor. The standard library page is unambiguous: "Being an unordered collection, sets do not record element position or order of insertion. Accordingly, sets do not support indexing, slicing, or other sequence-like behavior." Two beginner-safe consequences follow: (1) iteration order over a set is unspecified and can change between runs or even between insertions, and (2) attempting `xs[0]` on a set raises `TypeError`. The empty-set pitfall is real: `{}` creates an empty `dict`, not an empty `set`; the only correct empty-set literal is `set()`. Set objects support membership testing (`x in s`, very fast) and the four beginner-relevant set algebra operators from the tutorial: union `a | b`, intersection `a & b`, difference `a - b`, and symmetric difference `a ^ b`. A `frozenset` is the immutable counterpart; beginners can defer it.
paraphrase: A set is a collection of unique items with no order and no duplicates. Curly braces create a non-empty set: `colors = {"red", "green", "blue"}`; the empty set must be built with `set()`, not `{}` (the latter is an empty dictionary). The two beginner uses are "does this item exist?" (`"red" in colors` is `True`) and "remove duplicates from a list" (`set(["a", "b", "a"])` is `{"a", "b"}`). The four set algebra operators are `|` (everything in either), `&` (only in both), `-` (in the left but not the right), and `^` (in one but not both). The chapter must warn that sets are unordered, so iterating a set or printing it can produce items in any order, and trying to read `colors[0]` raises a `TypeError`. The chapter should also flag that set items must be hashable: lists and dicts cannot be added to a set, but numbers, strings, and tuples can.

### entry-038
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, "5.5. Dictionaries" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/datastructures.html#dictionaries) and the standard library "Mapping Types — dict" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#typesmapping) plus "Dictionary view objects" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#dictionary-view-objects), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Defines the chapter's beginner model for `dict`. A dict maps unique keys to values; the keys must be hashable (strings, numbers, and tuples of hashables are fine; lists and dicts are not). Empty dict is `{}`. The order of `dict.keys()`, `dict.values()`, and `dict.items()` views is insertion order, and the standard library page states the guarantee explicitly as "Dictionary order is guaranteed to be insertion order" with a "Changed in version 3.7" note. The three operations a beginner needs are: assign (`d["name"] = "Ada"`), look up (`d["name"]` raises `KeyError` if the key is missing), and remove (`del d["name"]`). The safe-lookup alternative `d.get("name", default)` returns the default instead of raising. Iteration over a dict yields keys, and the `for k, v in d.items():` form yields `(key, value)` pairs. The tutorial's "Looping Techniques" section shows the `.items()` pattern as the canonical way to visit both halves at once.
paraphrase: A dictionary stores pairs of keys and values, where each key is unique and must be of an unchanging type. An empty dictionary is `{}`, and a populated one is `{"name": "Ada", "age": 36}`. Look up a value with its key (`person["name"]`); the safe form is `person.get("name", "unknown")`, which returns the second argument when the key is missing instead of raising an error. Replace a value by assigning to an existing key, and add a new key with the same syntax. Walk over both keys and values with `for key, value in person.items():`. Since Python 3.7, dictionaries remember the order in which keys were first added, so the chapter can rely on that when introducing CSV and JSON later in the chapter. Keys must be hashable, so a list cannot be a dict key while a string, number, or tuple of those can.

### entry-039
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, "5. Data Structures" and "3. An Informal Introduction to Python" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/introduction.html#text) plus the standard library "Common Sequence Operations" and "Text Sequence Type — str" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#textseq), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Justifies the chapter's "strings behave like lists" beginner shortcut while still being technically correct. Both `str` and `list` satisfy the "Common Sequence Operations" table: zero-based integer indexing (`text[i]`), slicing (`text[i:j]` and `text[i:j:k]` with a step), `len(text)`, the `in` operator for substring membership, and the `+` and `*` operators for concatenation and repetition. Two qualifiers the chapter must mention to stay accurate: (1) strings are immutable, so `text[0] = "H"` raises `TypeError`; the way to "change" a string is to build a new one (slicing plus concatenation, or `str.replace`); (2) `in` on a string tests for a substring, not for a character set, so `"ell" in "hello"` is `True`. The tutorial also notes that plain `print(text)` is the most common beginner display; explicit `str.format` and f-strings were already covered in ch-03 and are not the focus here.
paraphrase: Strings and lists share enough behavior that beginners can reuse what they already know. Both support zero-based indexing (`text[0]` is the first character), slicing (`text[1:4]` is the second through fourth characters), `len(text)` to count items, `in` for membership testing (`"ell" in "hello"` is `True`), and `+` to join two strings end to end. The key difference is that strings cannot be changed in place: trying to assign to `text[0]` raises a `TypeError`. To produce a modified string, build a new one: `text[:5] + "!"` keeps the first five characters and adds an exclamation mark. The chapter should also note that `in` on a string is a substring test, not a character set test, which is the more useful mental model for search-style work that comes up in CSV and JSON processing later in the chapter.

### entry-040
source: Python Software Foundation — Python Tutorial, "7.2. Reading and Writing Files" and "7.2.1. Methods of File Objects" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/inputoutput.html#reading-and-writing-files and https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/inputoutput.html#methods-of-file-objects), cross-referenced with the built-in `open()` function documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/functions.html#open), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Anchors the chapter's text-file I/O model. The canonical form is `open(filename, mode, encoding=None)`, returning a file object. In text mode, `mode` is one of `'r'` (read, the default), `'w'` (write, which truncates an existing file), `'a'` (append), and `'r+'` (read and write); appending `'b'` switches to binary mode. Because the default encoding is platform-dependent, the tutorial recommends `encoding="utf-8"` explicitly whenever a text file is opened. The reading methods on the file object are `f.read(size=-1)` (read everything, or up to `size` characters), `f.readline()` (one line, keeping the trailing `\n`), and the iteration form `for line in f:` (memory-efficient, the recommended way to walk a file line by line). The writing method is `f.write(str)`, which writes a string and does not add a newline (the caller must include `\n` if one is wanted). The Tutorial's safe pattern is the `with` statement: `with open(path, mode, encoding="utf-8") as f:` closes the file automatically when the block exits, even if an exception is raised, so the chapter must present this as the default form rather than the bare `open()` / `f.close()` pair.
paraphrase: To read or write a text file, open it with `open(path, mode, encoding="utf-8")` and read or write through the returned file object. The beginner-relevant modes are `"r"` to read, `"w"` to write (which replaces the file if it already exists), and `"a"` to append. The `encoding="utf-8"` argument is the default the chapter should always show, because the platform default can vary and the tutorial recommends UTF-8 explicitly. Reading the whole file at once is `text = f.read()`; reading one line is `line = f.readline()`; the most useful beginner form is `for line in f:`, which walks the file one line at a time without loading it all into memory. Writing is `f.write("hello\n")` — the `\n` must be written explicitly because `write` does not add it. The chapter's required pattern is the `with` statement: `with open(path, "r", encoding="utf-8") as f:` opens the file, runs the indented body, and closes the file automatically when the block exits, even if the body raises an exception. Beginners should never write bare `open()` followed by manual `f.close()` at this stage.

### entry-041
source: Python Software Foundation — `csv` module documentation, "Module Contents" and "Dialects and Formatting Parameters" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/csv.html#module-csv, https://docs.python.org/3/library/csv.html#dialects-and-formatting-parameters, and https://docs.python.org/3/library/csv.html#csv.reader), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Defines the chapter's beginner CSV surface, all from the standard library. The two reader functions are `csv.reader(iterable, dialect="excel")`, which returns an iterator of rows where each row is a list of strings, and `csv.DictReader(iterable, fieldnames=None, ...)`, which returns an iterator of `dict`s whose keys come from the first row of the file (or from the optional `fieldnames` argument) and whose values are strings. The two writer functions are `csv.writer(file)` (rows written with `writerow([...])` or `writerows([...])`) and `csv.DictWriter(file, fieldnames, ...)` (rows written as `dict`s, after an explicit `writeheader()` call). Two beginner-relevant details from the same page: the default delimiter is `,` and the default quoting is `csv.QUOTE_MINIMAL` (quote only fields that contain the delimiter, the quote character, a newline, or a carriage return). The docs also recommend opening the underlying file with `newline=""` when on Windows so that the csv module handles line endings consistently across platforms. The chapter should not introduce custom dialects, the `Sniffer`, or `QUOTE_NONNUMERIC`/`QUOTE_ALL` until a real need appears.
paraphrase: The `csv` standard-library module reads and writes comma-separated value files without bringing in a third-party dependency. To read, open the file with `encoding="utf-8"` and `newline=""` and pass the file object to `csv.reader`; the result is an iterator whose items are lists of strings, one list per row. `csv.DictReader` does the same thing but yields a `dict` per row, with keys taken from the first line of the file (the header row). To write, open the file for writing and pass the file object to `csv.writer`; call `writer.writerow([...])` to write one row, or `writer.writerows([...])` to write many. `csv.DictWriter` needs the column names once at construction time and a single explicit `writeheader()` call before the first `writerow(dict)` call. The default works for typical comma-separated data; the `newline=""` argument on the underlying `open()` call is the only cross-platform fix beginners should be told to include, and the chapter should not introduce the `Sniffer` or custom dialects at this level.

### entry-042
source: Python Software Foundation — `json` module documentation, "Basic Usage" and "Encoders and Decoders" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/json.html#basic-usage and https://docs.python.org/3/library/json.html#encoders-and-decoders), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Anchors the chapter's beginner JSON model. The two file-oriented functions are `json.dump(obj, fp, ...)` (write `obj` to the file-like object `fp`) and `json.load(fp, ...)` (read one JSON document from `fp` and return the resulting Python object). The two string-oriented counterparts are `json.dumps(obj, ...)` and `json.loads(s, ...)`; beginners should default to the file forms because the chapter has just taught `open()`. Two beginner-relevant keyword arguments are `ensure_ascii` and `indent`. The default is `ensure_ascii=True`, which escapes every non-ASCII character to a `\uXXXX` sequence; the standard recommendation for human-readable files (config files, fixtures, sample data the reader will open in an editor) is to pass `ensure_ascii=False` so that accented characters and other Unicode text round-trip unchanged. The default is `indent=None`, which produces the most compact possible one-line JSON; the standard recommendation for human-readable output is `indent=2` (or `indent=4`), which inserts a newline and that many spaces after each comma or colon. The docs also note that `json.dump` accepts a file opened in text mode and writes `str`, not `bytes`. The chapter should not introduce `sort_keys`, `separators`, custom `JSONEncoder`/`JSONDecoder` subclasses, or the `default=` hook at this level.
paraphrase: The `json` standard-library module turns Python values into JSON text and JSON text back into Python values, with no third-party dependency. The two file-friendly functions are `json.dump(value, file)` to write and `json.load(file)` to read. Beginners should always open the file in text mode with `encoding="utf-8"` and pass the file object straight to these two functions. The default behavior produces compact one-line JSON with all non-ASCII characters escaped, which is safe but hard to read. For files a human will inspect, pass two keyword arguments: `json.dump(value, file, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)` writes indented, UTF-8-preserved JSON, and `json.load(file)` reads it back into a Python object. The two string forms, `json.dumps(value, ...)` and `json.loads("...")`, are useful in tests and for sending JSON over the network but are not the beginner default. The chapter should not introduce custom encoders, the `default=` hook, or `sort_keys` at this level.

### entry-043
source: Python Software Foundation — `json` module documentation, "Encoders and Decoders" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/json.html#encoders-and-decoders), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the Python Tutorial "5. Data Structures" (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/datastructures.html) for the Python-side counterparts.
used_in: ch-05
claim/finding: Supplies the chapter's required JSON-supported-types cheat sheet. The `json.JSONDecoder` page publishes a translation table that maps JSON types to Python types on the way in (`object` ↙ `dict`, `array` ↙ `list`, `string` ↙ `str`, `number (int)` ↙ `int`, `number (real)` ↙ `float`, `true` ↙ `True`, `false` ↙ `False`, `null` ↙ `None`) and the same set round-trips on the way out. Three beginner-relevant "data loss" warnings the chapter must surface because they are not obvious from the table: (1) a Python `tuple` becomes a JSON `array`, which is decoded back as a Python `list`, so round-tripping a tuple through JSON loses its tuple type; (2) a Python `set` is not JSON-serializable by default and raises `TypeError`; the chapter should show `json.dumps(sorted(my_set))` or convert with `list(...)` first; (3) custom Python classes are not serializable and the standard library raises `TypeError` with a clear message, which is the right error to point beginners at when they hit it. By contrast, `True`/`False`/`None` round-trip cleanly because they match `true`/`false`/`null` exactly.
paraphrase: JSON supports only a small, fixed set of value types, and the Python `json` module uses the same fixed set on both write and read. The safe-to-share Python types are: `str` (text), `int` (whole number), `float` (decimal number), `bool` (written as `true`/`false`), `None` (written as `null`), `list` (written as a JSON array), and `dict` (written as a JSON object, with string keys). Anything else is either changed on the way out or rejected. Two beginner traps to call out: a Python `tuple` is written as a JSON `array` and comes back as a `list`, so its tuple-ness is lost; and a Python `set` is rejected with a `TypeError`, because sets are not in the supported list. The chapter should also flag that custom classes (anything not on the supported list) raise `TypeError` on encoding — that is the expected error and a sign that the value must be converted to a `dict` or to one of the supported types before it is written.

---

## ch-06 — Understand Language Models

### entry-044
source: IBM — "What are large language models (LLMs)?" by Cole Stryker, "What are LLMs?" and "Using large language models" sections (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/large-language-models), page accessed 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-06
claim/finding: Restates the ch-01 entry-004 framing at the level ch-06 needs: an LLM is a "giant statistical prediction machine" that "repeatedly predicts the next word in a sequence" by "calculating the probabilities for all potential next tokens" and "outputting the most likely one," one token at a time; the model "does not 'know' the final answer in advance; it uses all the statistical relationships it learned in training to predict one token at a time, making its best guess at every step." This is the chapter's precise conceptual anchor for the "prompts ↙ tokens ↙ model ↙ tokens ↙ text" mental model that the rest of ch-06 builds on, and it deliberately avoids transformer math, gradient descent, or architecture diagrams. Age-risk note: the IBM page lists specific commercial LLM names (Anthropic Claude, OpenAI ChatGPT, Meta Llama, Google Gemini, Microsoft Copilot, IBM Granite) that are correct as of mid-2026; treat that product list as illustrative of "the public-facing LLM landscape" rather than a current state of the market, and do not pin a specific vendor or version in the chapter text.
paraphrase: At its core, a large language model is a statistical prediction machine that has been trained on a very large amount of text. When the user gives it a prompt, the model converts that prompt into a sequence of tokens and then, one token at a time, calculates a probability distribution over what could come next, picks the most likely continuation, and repeats until it decides to stop. Each token is produced in light of every token that came before, but the model never knows the final answer in advance — it just makes the locally best prediction it can at each step. ch-06 should make this loop the chapter's centerpiece: an LLM is a next-token predictor, and every "clever" behavior an agent appears to exhibit is the result of running that loop many times with carefully chosen context. The chapter should not claim any specific model is "smarter" than another; the level here is "a model is a function from a long context string to a probability distribution over the next token."

### entry-045
source: Hugging Face Transformers documentation, "Tokenization algorithms" (https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/tokenizer_summary), cross-referenced with the Hugging Face "Tokenizer Playground" demo (https://huggingface.co/spaces/Xenova/the-tokenizer-playground) as cited by the IBM "What is a context window?" page, page accessed 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-06
claim/finding: Defines tokens and sub-word tokenization at a beginner mental-model level, deliberately without teaching implementation. A token is a piece of text that is somewhere between a whole word and a single character; sub-word tokenization algorithms (BPE, Unigram, WordPiece) "split text into units between words and characters, keeping the vocabulary compact while still capturing meaningful pieces. Common words stay intact as single tokens, and rare or unknown words decompose into subwords." The Hugging Face worked example shows that `annoyingly` "might be split into `['annoying', 'ly']` or `['annoy', 'ing', 'ly']` depending on the vocabulary." SentencePiece, used for languages without spaces (e.g. Chinese, Japanese), treats the space character as a regular token (`"“"`), so the same algorithm works without depending on whitespace. The chapter should not teach any of BPE, Unigram, WordPiece, or SentencePiece by name; it should anchor only the "tokens are sub-words, not whole words, and different models can tokenize the same text differently" mental model. The Hugging Face Tokenizer Playground is a beginner-friendly interactive way to see tokenization in action without writing any code.
paraphrase: Before an LLM can read text, that text has to be split into tokens — short pieces that look like a mix of whole words, word pieces, and single characters. Modern LLMs use sub-word tokenization, where common short words stay as one token, longer or rarer words get split into recognizable sub-pieces, and previously unseen words can still be represented by recombining known sub-pieces. The same sentence can produce different token splits on different models, and the same model can produce more or fewer tokens depending on the language (one IBM-cited study reported an English sentence and its Telugu translation differed in token count by more than 7x even though the Telugu text was shorter in characters). For ch-06 the takeaway is: tokens are the model's alphabet, and the number of tokens in a piece of text is what matters for both pricing and the context window — not the number of characters or words. The chapter can show a small example ("Hello, world!" tokenizes to a handful of tokens; a long paragraph tokenizes to a few hundred) without teaching any algorithm.

### entry-046
source: IBM — "What is AI inference?" by Dave Bergmann, "AI inference vs. AI training" and "Types of AI inference" sections (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/ai-inference), page accessed 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-06
claim/finding: Establishes the chapter's training vs inference mental model in two sentences. IBM frames inference as "any instance of an artificial intelligence model actually generating outputs or making decisions in a real-world AI application," and contrasts it with training, where a model "is adjusted until the model's decision-making 'fits' the patterns of the training data" using a forward pass and a backward pass. The page makes the asymmetry explicit: "Unlike in model training, inference entails only a forward pass." The two inference flavors relevant to ch-06 are online inference ("In online inference, a trained model processes input data immediately, one input at a time. Online inference is appropriate for any AI system whose outputs are time sensitive … or that require live interactions with users (such as chatbots or machine translation).") and batch inference, which the chapter should mention in one sentence and then set aside. For an agent application, every `model.generate(prompt)` call is online inference — the model produces tokens one at a time, the program waits, and the response is added back to the conversation. The page also gives a "the LLM iterates to predict the next token" mental model that pairs naturally with entry-044. No smolagents-specific claim appears here; chub validation is not required.
paraphrase: Training and inference are two different jobs, and an agent application only ever does the second one. Training is what a model vendor does once (and redoes when releasing a new model): it feeds the model enormous amounts of text and adjusts the internal weights until the model is good at predicting the next token. Training is expensive, slow, and runs on big hardware that the developer of an agent does not own. Inference is what every application does every time it calls the model: it sends in a prompt and the model generates a response, one token at a time, using only the patterns it learned during training. Inference is online (one prompt at a time, the case for any agent that talks to a user) or batch (many prompts at once, used for offline reports). For ch-06 the practical consequence is that an agent's call to a model API is always an inference call — it costs the API provider compute, may take noticeable time, and produces a non-deterministic answer because the model is sampling from a probability distribution rather than executing fixed code.

### entry-047
source: IBM — "What is a context window?" by Dave Bergmann, "What is a context window?" and "Why do models have a maximum context length?" sections (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/context-window), page accessed 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-06
claim/finding: Defines the chapter's context window mental model. IBM: "The context window (or 'context length') of a large language model is the amount of text, in tokens, that the model can consider or 'remember' at any one time." The page notes that "the text of the actual user input is often not the only thing taking up space within a model's context window. In many cases, such as with chatbots, models are also provided with a 'system prompt'”often hidden from the user”that conditions their behavior and governs other aspects of the conversation. Supplementary information drawn from external data sources for retrieval augmented generation (RAG) is likewise stored within the context window during inference." When the inputs exceed the window, "it must be truncated or summarized for the model to proceed." The page also surfaces a beginner-relevant practical rule: "a decent estimate would be roughly 1.5 tokens per word" (English). The "Lost in the Middle" effect, in which long-context models often use information near the start or end of the input more reliably than information buried in the middle, is recorded in the "Challenges of long context windows" section and is age-stable research. Age-risk: specific context-window numbers for named commercial models (e.g. 128K for GPT-4o, 200K for Claude 3.5 Sonnet, 1M“2M for Gemini 1.5) change every quarter; the chapter should teach the shape of the trade-off and the "roughly 1.5 tokens per word" estimator rather than quote specific numbers.
paraphrase: The context window is the model's working memory, measured in tokens. Everything the model can see at one time — the system prompt, the prior turns of the conversation, any documents or tool results the agent has attached, and the question being asked right now — has to fit inside that single window. If the inputs grow past the window, the application has to drop, truncate, or summarize the older parts before sending the next call, because the model has no other way to "remember" them. As a rough rule of thumb for English text, plan on about 1.5 tokens per word, which is why a "few-page" prompt is comfortably inside any modern context window while a "novel-length" one is not. The chapter should also surface the "lost in the middle" effect, in which very long context windows do not help if the key piece of information is buried in the middle of the input — agents should place the most important text near the start or end. The chapter should not try to teach exact context-window sizes for specific models because they grow every quarter; it should teach the shape of the trade-off (bigger window = more capacity and more cost, smaller window = cheaper but tighter) and the practical engineering response (trim, summarize, and place important information at the start or end).

### entry-048
source: IBM — "What is LLM temperature?" by Joshua Noble, "What is LLM temperature?" and "Configuring temperature" sections (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/llm-temperature), page accessed 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-06
claim/finding: Gives the chapter's beginner treatment of sampling parameters without turning it into a math chapter. IBM: "Temperature is a parameter for adjusting the output of large language models. Temperature controls the randomness of text that is generated by LLMs during inference. … A lower temperature essentially makes those tokens with the highest probability more likely to be selected; a higher temperature increases a model's likelihood of selecting less probable tokens." The page also defines `top_p` (nucleus sampling): "This parameter allows the model to consider tokens whose cumulative probability is greater than a specified probability value. When selecting tokens for the generated output the model only selects a group of tokens whose total probability is more than, for instance, 95%." It also flags the common beginner misconception that "temperature is often conflated with 'creativity' but this isn't always the case" — a high temperature is "more accurate to think of them as being less determined by the training data." The page introduces several other parameters (`do_sample`, `top_k`, plus `max length`, `stop sequences`, `frequency penalty`, `presence penalty`) but the chapter should restrict itself to temperature and top_p, with a one-sentence pointer that other knobs exist. Age-risk: the canonical OpenAI/Anthropic default temperature values (`1.0` for OpenAI; Anthropic ranges `0.0`“`1.0` and uses `1.0` as the default) are API-version-specific and should be cited as "the vendor's default," not as a fixed number.
paraphrase: The model is not picking a single "correct" token at every step — it is sampling from a probability distribution. Two settings shape how that sampling behaves. Temperature scales how much the model is willing to take a less likely token: low temperature (close to 0) makes the model greedy and repetitive, high temperature (closer to 1) makes it more varied and surprising, and very high values quickly produce incoherent text. Top_p (nucleus sampling) instead restricts the model to the smallest set of tokens whose combined probability just exceeds a chosen threshold — typically 0.9 or 0.95 — so it never has to consider a long tail of implausible tokens. The two controls are not independent, and most providers recommend changing one at a time. For ch-06, the practical takeaway is: leave the defaults unless there is a reason not to; reach for a lower temperature when the agent needs a precise, reproducible answer (e.g. calling a function with strict argument types); and reach for a higher temperature when the agent specifically wants to brainstorm. The chapter should not dwell on `top_k`, frequency penalty, or other knobs — those are optional deeper reading for the curious reader, not ch-06 material.

### entry-049
source: OpenAI — "Text generation" guide, "Message roles and instruction following" section (https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/text-generation); cross-referenced with Anthropic — "Messages" API reference, "Body Parameters" ↙ `system` and `messages` (https://docs.anthropic.com/en/api/messages), pages accessed 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-06
claim/finding: Defines the message-role convention used by mainstream chat-completion APIs at a beginner level. OpenAI's current Responses API distinguishes three input roles: `developer` (instructions from the application, prioritized ahead of user), `user` (instructions from the end user), and `assistant` (the model's own past responses). The OpenAI table states: "`developer` messages are instructions provided by the application developer, prioritized ahead of user messages. … `user` messages are instructions provided by an end user, prioritized behind developer messages. … Messages generated by the model have the `assistant` role." Anthropic's Messages API takes the same shape but splits the system instruction out as a top-level `system` parameter: "If you want to include a system prompt, you can use the top-level `system` parameter — there is no 'system' role for input messages in the Messages API." The `messages` array itself then alternates `user` and `assistant` turns, and the model produces the next assistant turn. Both APIs also let the model emit `tool_use` / `tool_result` style content blocks, which ch-09 will cover; ch-06 only needs the three-role convention. Age-risk: OpenAI renamed the "system" role to "developer" in the Responses API in 2025; older Chat Completions API tutorials still say "system" and `chat.completions.create` still accepts that role. The chapter should teach the three-role pattern in plain language and treat the exact role name as API-version-specific.
paraphrase: Modern chat-completion APIs all work the same way at a beginner level: the program sends a list of messages, and the model returns one new message in reply. Three kinds of messages are enough for ch-06. A system (or developer) message carries standing instructions from the program author, such as "answer in two short paragraphs and never invent URLs." A user message carries the actual question or request from the human. An assistant message represents a past reply the model itself produced earlier in the same conversation. The system message has the highest priority, user messages have lower priority, and assistant messages are the model's own prior outputs that the conversation is building on. Most providers let the system instruction either be a separate top-level parameter (Anthropic) or be the first item in the messages list under the role name `system` (older OpenAI Chat Completions) or `developer` (current OpenAI Responses API). For ch-06 the takeaway is the three-role pattern, not the exact parameter name; ch-07 will translate this pattern into concrete code with smolagents and the OpenAI / Anthropic SDKs.

### entry-050
source: IBM — "What are large language models (LLMs)?", "Evaluating LLMs" section (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/large-language-models), cross-referenced with IBM — "What is a context window?", "Safety and cybersecurity challenges" (https://www.ibm.com/think/topics/context-window) and Anthropic — "Many-shot jailbreaking" research note (https://www.anthropic.com/research/many-shot-jailbreaking) as cited by the IBM context-window page, pages accessed 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-06
claim/finding: Sets the ch-06 safety floor in two beginner flags without re-deriving what ch-01 entry-005 already established. IBM's "Evaluating LLMs" section restates the safety caveats the chapter needs at this point: "LLMs can also reflect and amplify biases present in their training data, producing outputs that are unfair or offensive. Additionally, their resource demands are significant: training and running LLMs requires large amounts of computational power and energy, raising both cost and environmental concerns." The same IBM context-window page flags the chapter-relevant safety note: "A longer context window might also have the unintended effect of presenting a longer attack surface for adversarial prompts. Recent research from Anthropic demonstrated that increasing a model's context length also increases its vulnerability to 'jailbreaking' and (subsequently) being provoked to produce harmful responses." ch-06 should not re-define hallucination (ch-01 entry-005 already did) and should not introduce the ch-14 trust-boundary or sandboxing material; it should only plant two beginner flags — (1) LLM output is untrusted by default and must be checked before being passed to any tool, and (2) the same context window that makes the model useful also makes it possible to stuff it with adversarial text retrieved from an external source. No smolagents-specific safety claim is recorded here; the deeper defenses (sandboxing, scoped API keys, human approval, permission limits) are explicitly deferred to ch-14.
paraphrase: ch-06 adds two beginner-relevant safety flags on top of what ch-01 already established. First, the model can return biased, offensive, or otherwise harmful content because the training data contained it; the agent's job is to treat LLM output as a draft, not as truth, and never to pipe model text straight into an action with real-world side effects. Second, the same long context window that lets the agent hold a long conversation also makes the model more vulnerable to "jailbreaking" — adversarial prompts hidden inside documents, tool results, or web pages that the agent retrieves and stuffs into the model. These are the chapter's two safety beats; the deeper defenses (sandboxing, scoped API keys, human approval, permission limits) belong in ch-14, and that chapter will work through them in detail. The chapter should explicitly tell readers that an agent that fetches web pages, reads emails, or accepts user-uploaded files is now handing the model content the user did not write, and that the agent author — not the model — is responsible for filtering it.

---

## ch-07 — Call Models Safely from Python

### entry-051
source: Python Software Foundation — `urllib.request` standard library module (https://docs.python.org/3/library/urllib.request.html) and `json` standard library module (https://docs.python.org/3/library/json.html), Python 3.14.6 documentation; cross-referenced with the `http` standard library module for HTTP status-code constants (https://docs.python.org/3/library/http.html).
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Establishes the chapter's minimum-dependency HTTP path. `urllib.request` is the stdlib HTTP client and ships with Python on every platform; the canonical POST-JSON pattern is `req = urllib.request.Request(url, data=json.dumps(payload).encode("utf-8"), headers={"Content-Type": "application/json", "Authorization": f"Bearer {api_key}"}, method="POST")` followed by `with urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=30) as resp: body = resp.read().decode("utf-8")`. The stdlib also exports the `http` module's `HTTPStatus` enum so beginners can compare `resp.status` against named constants (`HTTPStatus.OK == 200`, `HTTPStatus.TOO_MANY_REQUESTS == 429`, `HTTPStatus.UNAUTHORIZED == 401`) instead of memorizing raw numbers. The stdlib route is the chapter's "no new dependencies" floor: a single-call chat-completion POST fits in ~10 lines, no `pip install` is required, and the same code runs on Windows, macOS, and Linux with no platform-specific code.
paraphrase: The standard library's `urllib.request` module is enough to make an HTTP POST from Python with no third-party packages installed. To call a chat-completion API the beginner builds a `Request` object that carries the URL, an HTTP method of `POST`, a JSON-encoded request body, and the headers the API needs (most importantly `Content-Type: application/json` and `Authorization: Bearer <API_KEY>`). Then `urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=30)` sends the request and returns a response object whose `status` is the HTTP code and whose `read()` gives the raw bytes of the response body. The stdlib also exposes a `http.HTTPStatus` enum that lets the beginner write `resp.status == http.HTTPStatus.TOO_MANY_REQUESTS` instead of `resp.status == 429`, which is the chapter's beginner-friendly way to handle rate limits. For ch-07, the stdlib route is the starting point because it has zero install cost and shows the actual HTTP mechanics that higher-level libraries hide.

### entry-052
source: chub entry `requests/package` (requests==2.34.2, 2026-05-29) — https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/stable/ — cross-referenced against the requests Quickstart (https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/stable/user/quickstart/) and Advanced Usage (https://requests.readthedocs.io/en/stable/user/advanced/).
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Provides the chapter's cleaner-HTTP alternative and the justification for picking one. The chub guide records requests 2.34.2 as the current PyPI release, requiring Python `>=3.10` (the book's floor per ch-02 entry-009). The canonical POST-JSON pattern is `response = requests.post(url, json=payload, headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {api_key}"}, timeout=30)` followed by `response.raise_for_status()` and `data = response.json()`. The guide calls out the same beginner footgun as the stdlib route: there is no default timeout ("Without `timeout=`, the request blocks indefinitely"), and `response.json()` does not check the HTTP status code, so beginners must pair the two. After ch-07's stdlib example, the chapter should switch to requests for every subsequent call because (a) the chapter has already established `pip install` in ch-02, (b) every external tutorial a beginner finds will use requests, (c) the stdlib version of "POST JSON, read JSON" is roughly 4— more lines for the same behavior, and (d) requests 2.34.x is at the book's Python floor and is MIT-licensed, so there is no version or license blocker.
paraphrase: The `requests` package is the de-facto standard HTTP client for Python and is what every beginner-facing tutorial will use. The chub-validated current release is requests 2.34.2 (May 2026), which is MIT-licensed and requires Python 3.10 or newer — the same floor the book set in ch-02. Compared with stdlib `urllib.request`, the equivalent POST-JSON call shrinks from ~10 lines to 3: `requests.post(url, json=payload, headers=headers, timeout=30)` does the encoding, sends the request, and returns a response whose `.json()` parses the body and whose `.raise_for_status()` converts a 4xx/5xx into a Python exception. The chapter should present stdlib first (so beginners see the HTTP mechanics) and then switch to requests for the rest of the chapter and every chapter after. Two footguns the chapter must surface: requests has no default timeout, so `timeout=` is mandatory, and `response.json()` does not check the status code, so `.raise_for_status()` must come first. License: MIT — recommend freely with no flags.

### entry-053
source: chub entry `python-dotenv/package` (python-dotenv==1.2.2, 2026-03-12) — https://github.com/theskumar/python-dotenv — combined with the Python Software Foundation — `os.environ` standard library documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/os.html#os.environ) and the `getenv()` builtin (https://docs.python.org/3/library/os.html#os.getenv), Python 3.14.6 documentation.
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Defines the chapter's canonical beginner-safe environment-variable pattern, consistent with ch-02 entry-017. From the stdlib side, `os.environ` is the dict-like mapping the rest of Python reads; `os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY")` returns `None` when the key is missing and `os.environ["OPENAI_API_KEY"]` raises `KeyError`. The beginner-safe read form is therefore `os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY") or ""` followed by an explicit "the key was missing" check, so the program never silently sends an empty bearer token to the server. From the python-dotenv side (1.2.2), the canonical chapter pattern is `from dotenv import load_dotenv; load_dotenv()` at the top of the script, which searches for `.env` in the script directory and walks upward, parses it, and copies `KEY=value` pairs into `os.environ` without overriding real environment variables (unless `override=True` is passed). The chub guide confirms the "production wins over local file" default and also documents `dotenv_values()` for cases where the reader wants an explicit dict without mutating `os.environ`. The chapter should not introduce the optional `[cli]` extra or the `set_key`/`unset_key` helpers at this level.
paraphrase: Read API keys with `os.environ` and load them from a `.env` file with `python-dotenv`. The standard library exposes two ways to read an environment variable: `os.environ["OPENAI_API_KEY"]` raises `KeyError` when the key is missing, while `os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY")` returns `None`. Beginners should default to the `getenv()` form plus an explicit missing-key check, so the program prints a clear error instead of sending `Authorization: Bearer ` (with nothing after "Bearer") to the server. To keep secrets out of source code, the chapter's canonical pattern is to add `from dotenv import load_dotenv; load_dotenv()` at the top of every script and put the actual keys in a `.env` file at the project root. The python-dotenv 1.2.2 chub guide confirms the safe default behavior: real environment variables always win over `.env` values, so production keys are never silently overwritten by a developer's local file. The chapter should not introduce the `[cli]` extra, the `dotenv run` command, or programmatic edits to `.env` at this level — those are deeper tooling for later chapters.

### entry-054
source: OpenAI — "Text generation" guide and the Chat Completions API reference (https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/text-generation and https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference/chat), cross-referenced with Anthropic — "Messages" API reference (https://docs.anthropic.com/en/api/messages), pages accessed 2026-08-01; plus the chub entry `openai/package` (openai==2.38.0) for SDK cross-reference.
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Defines the chapter's canonical chat-completion request body and response shape, building on the system/user/assistant role convention from ch-06 entry-049. OpenAI's Chat Completions endpoint accepts a JSON body with `model` (string), `messages` (list of `{role, content}` pairs), and optional `temperature`, `max_tokens`, and other fields; the response is a JSON object whose `choices[0].message.content` holds the model's reply and whose `usage` object reports prompt/completion/total token counts. The OpenAI Responses API (the current default per the openai 2.38.0 chub guide) takes the same `model` and `input` fields but returns `output_text` on the response. Anthropic's Messages API splits the system instruction into a top-level `system` field and alternates only `user` and `assistant` turns in the `messages` list; `max_tokens` is required, and the response `content` is a list of content blocks (`{type: "text", text: "..."}`), not a single string. The chapter's beginner-level translation of all three is the same: send a list of messages with `role` and `content` fields, read the assistant's reply from the response, and add it back to the list before the next call. Age-risk: base URLs (`https://api.openai.com/v1` for OpenAI, `https://api.anthropic.com` for Anthropic), exact request field names (`system` vs top-level `system` vs `developer`), the model-name strings, and the response shape all change with API versions; teach the pattern, not the exact field names, and verify against the current API reference before each book revision.
paraphrase: A chat-completion API call is a JSON POST whose body has a `model` field (the model name string) and a `messages` field (a list of `{role, content}` pairs). The roles that beginners need are `system` for the standing instructions, `user` for the human's question, and `assistant` for the model's prior replies — the same three-role pattern ch-06 entry-049 already established. To continue a conversation, append the new user message, send the list, read the assistant's reply from the response, and append that reply to the list before the next call. The two providers differ in three beginner-relevant details: Anthropic takes the system instruction as a separate top-level `system` field rather than as the first message; Anthropic requires `max_tokens` on every call; and Anthropic returns the reply as a list of content blocks rather than a single string. The chapter's beginner pattern does not need to model these differences — it just needs to show one canonical example end-to-end and say in one sentence that the other provider has small field-name differences, with a pointer to the current API reference. The exact base URLs (`https://api.openai.com/v1` and `https://api.anthropic.com`), the exact field names, the exact model identifiers, and the response shape are all API-version-specific and should be treated as age-risk; the chapter should teach the pattern (list of messages ↙ JSON POST ↙ read reply ↙ append reply) and treat the names as current-as-of-publication.

### entry-055
source: Python Software Foundation — `urllib.request` documentation, "Optional `timeout` argument" section (https://docs.python.org/3/library/urllib.request.html#urllib.request.urlopen), Python 3.14.6; cross-referenced with chub entry `requests/package` (requests==2.34.2) "Timeouts" section and chub entry `openai/package` (openai==2.38.0) "Config And Authentication" section.
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Anchors the chapter's timeout rule. The stdlib `urllib.request.urlopen(req, timeout=30)` takes a timeout in seconds that applies to the entire blocking operation; without it the docs explicitly warn the call can hang forever. The requests package (2.34.2 chub) makes the same warning in stronger language: "Without `timeout=`, the request blocks indefinitely." requests accepts either a single float (connect-and-read combined) or a `(connect_timeout, read_timeout)` tuple, with the chub guide noting "a connect timeout slightly larger than a multiple of 3 (e.g. `3.05`) avoids racing TCP retransmit windows" — a beginner footnote rather than the default. The openai 2.38.0 chub guide records the SDK's default as a 10-minute timeout with two automatic retries; beginners should override this to a few seconds for interactive loops and confirm the override with `client.with_options(timeout=...)`. Age-risk: the SDK-default timeout values change across SDK versions; the rule itself (always pass an explicit timeout) is stable.
paraphrase: A timeout is how long the program is willing to wait for a network response before giving up. Without an explicit timeout, every HTTP client in this chapter can hang indefinitely — stdlib `urllib.request` warns about this in its docs, requests warns about it in bolder language, and the openai SDK's 10-minute default is far too long for an interactive agent loop. The chapter rule is therefore simple: always pass an explicit timeout. For a chat-completion call a beginner should start with 30 seconds, see how often it actually fires, and tune from there. For an inner agent loop where the model is supposed to answer quickly, 10 seconds is a more appropriate starting point. The chapter should not introduce tuple-form connect/read timeouts or TCP-retransmit subtleties; the beginner just needs `timeout=30` (stdlib) or `timeout=30` (requests) on every call.

### entry-056
source: chub entry `tenacity/package` (tenacity==9.1.4, 2026-03-12) — https://tenacity.readthedocs.io/en/latest/ — combined with the Python Software Foundation `time.sleep` and `random` standard library documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/time.html and https://docs.python.org/3/library/random.html), Python 3.14.6, as the fallback for a stdlib-only retry recipe.
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Establishes the chapter's retry-with-backoff rule and the stdlib-vs-library trade-off. stdlib `urllib.request` has no built-in retry support; a beginner who wants retries with exponential backoff has two readable paths. Path 1 (no new dependency): a small `for attempt in range(max_attempts): try: ... except urllib.error.URLError: time.sleep(2 ** attempt); continue` loop with `2 ** attempt` seconds of sleep (1, 2, 4, 8, …) and a small `random.uniform(0, 0.5)` jitter added on top to avoid thundering-herd. Path 2 (one new dependency): `tenacity` 9.1.4, which encodes the same policy as a decorator with composable building blocks (`stop_after_attempt`, `wait_random_exponential`, `retry_if_exception_type`, `reraise=True`). The tenacity chub guide explicitly warns that bare `@retry` retries forever with no waits, which is the wrong production default, and that omitting `reraise=True` makes the final failure raise `RetryError` instead of the original exception. License: tenacity is Apache-2.0; the stdlib recipe needs no license. For ch-07 the chapter should pick the stdlib recipe (path 1) for its first retry example and mention tenacity as the cleaner choice when the project already has more than one or two retry sites, deferring deeper tenacity coverage to ch-11 (structured workflows) or ch-12 (observability).
paraphrase: Retries with exponential backoff are how a chat-completion client recovers from transient failures (network blips, momentary 503s) without bothering the user. The stdlib does not include a retry helper, so the chapter's minimum recipe is a small `for attempt in range(max_attempts): try: ... except urllib.error.URLError: time.sleep(2 ** attempt + random.uniform(0, 0.5))` loop. The `2 ** attempt` term doubles the wait each try (1s, 2s, 4s, 8s, …) and the `random.uniform(0, 0.5)` term adds jitter so many concurrent clients do not all retry at exactly the same instant. For beginners who already have `pip install` muscle memory and a project with several retry sites, the `tenacity` package (Apache-2.0, 9.1.4) is the cleaner option: it encodes the same policy as a decorator with named building blocks (`stop_after_attempt(5)`, `wait_random_exponential(multiplier=1, max=30)`, `reraise=True`). The chapter should use the stdlib recipe for its first example (zero new dependencies, ~5 lines) and mention tenacity as the upgrade path for when the project outgrows the inline loop. License: stdlib recipe needs none; tenacity is Apache-2.0 — recommend freely with no flags.

### entry-057
source: IETF — RFC 6585, "Additional HTTP Status Codes", section 4 "429 Too Many Requests" (https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc6585#section-4); cross-referenced with the OpenAI SDK error-handling section recorded in chub entry `openai/package` (openai==2.38.0) and the Anthropic SDK retries section recorded in chub entry `anthropic/package` (anthropic==0.105.2).
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Establishes the chapter's beginner treatment of HTTP 429. RFC 6585 §4 defines 429 as the status code a server returns "when the user has sent too many requests in a given amount of time" and recommends that the response include a `Retry-After` header indicating how long to wait. Both provider SDKs raise a typed exception when this happens: openai raises `openai.RateLimitError` (in the 2.38.0 chub guide's documented exception families `APIConnectionError`, `APIStatusError`, `RateLimitError`, `APITimeoutError`); Anthropic's 0.105.2 SDK includes 429 in the list of status codes that trigger automatic retries by default (connection errors, 408, 409, 429, >=500), with a default retry count of 2. For a beginner, the chapter's rule is: when a 429 comes back, read the `Retry-After` header (in seconds) and sleep that long before retrying; if the header is missing, fall back to exponential backoff. Age-risk: the exact retry policy of each SDK (which status codes, how many attempts) changes across SDK versions; the rule "honor the server's Retry-After" is stable.
paraphrase: HTTP 429 means the server is telling the client to slow down. The IETF standard (RFC 6585) recommends that the server include a `Retry-After` header with the number of seconds the client should wait before trying again; the openai and Anthropic SDKs both surface 429 as a typed exception (`openai.RateLimitError` and the Anthropic SDK's automatic retry behavior) and follow the same header when present. The chapter's beginner rule is therefore: catch the 429-shaped exception, read `Retry-After` from the response headers, sleep that long, and retry. If the server omits `Retry-After`, fall back to the exponential-backoff recipe from entry-056 (start at 1 second, double up to a cap). Beginners should also know that 429s are normal during development: even a polite script that runs the same prompt ten times in a row can hit them. License: RFC 6585 is an IETF standards document and is in the public domain.

### entry-058
source: chub entry `requests/package` (requests==2.34.2) "Errors" section, cross-referenced with chub entry `openai/package` (openai==2.38.0) "Error Handling" section and chub entry `anthropic/package` (anthropic==0.105.2) "Retries" and "Timeouts" sections.
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Catalogs the chapter's four beginner error categories and ties each to a specific exception name and HTTP status range. (1) Network errors: the request never reached the server — DNS failure, TCP failure, TLS failure, connection reset, or read timeout. In requests, these raise `requests.exceptions.ConnectionError` or `requests.exceptions.Timeout` (with `ConnectTimeout` and `ReadTimeout` subclasses); in stdlib, `urllib.error.URLError` covers the family. (2) Auth errors: HTTP 401 (or 403). The API key is missing, wrong, or revoked. The openai 2.38.0 chub guide lists `openai.APIStatusError` as the parent for any non-2xx, with `exc.status_code` available to branch on. (3) Bad-request errors: HTTP 400 or 422 (Anthropic). The request was syntactically valid JSON but semantically wrong — wrong model name, message too long, missing required field. These are programming bugs that should not be retried. (4) Server errors: HTTP 500, 502, 503, 504. The server is having a problem and the request should be retried after a delay. The openai 2.38.0 chub guide records `APITimeoutError` for the 10-minute default-timeout case, and the requests 2.34.2 chub guide records `TooManyRedirects` as a separate Requests-only failure mode (redirect cap exceeded). For ch-07 the rule is: catch network and server errors with a retry loop (entry-056), surface auth and bad-request errors with a clear message and stop.
paraphrase: The chapter's beginner error taxonomy has exactly four buckets. (1) Network errors: the request never reached the server, so the right response is to wait a bit and retry. In requests these are `requests.exceptions.ConnectionError` and `Timeout` (with `ConnectTimeout` and `ReadTimeout`); in stdlib the umbrella is `urllib.error.URLError`. (2) Auth errors: HTTP 401 means the key is missing or wrong, and HTTP 403 means the key is right but is not authorized for this resource. The right response is to stop and tell the user "your API key is missing or invalid" — retrying will not help. (3) Bad-request errors: HTTP 400 (OpenAI) or HTTP 422 (Anthropic) means the request itself was wrong — wrong model name, message over the limit, missing required field. These are programming bugs and should fail loudly, not be retried. (4) Server errors: HTTP 500, 502, 503, 504 mean the server is having a problem. The right response is to retry with backoff. The openai and Anthropic SDKs surface all four as typed exceptions (`openai.RateLimitError`, `openai.APIStatusError`, `openai.APITimeoutError`, `openai.APIConnectionError` for openai 2.38.0; the equivalent Anthropic exceptions for 0.105.2), so the chapter can teach one generic pattern (`except APIStatusError as exc: branch on exc.status_code`) instead of raw status numbers. The chapter should not introduce custom exception hierarchies, structured logging, or observability hooks at this level — those belong in ch-12.

### entry-059
source: chub entry `python-dotenv/package` (python-dotenv==1.2.2, 2026-03-12) "Common Pitfalls" section — https://github.com/theskumar/python-dotenv — combined with GitHub gitignore repo "Python.gitignore" (https://github.com/github/gitignore/blob/main/Python.gitignore), 2026; cross-referenced with the chapter ch-02 entry-017 which already established the project's secrets-handling baseline.
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Codifies the chapter's hard security rule for API keys. The rule has three parts, all of which must be in place at once. (1) API keys MUST NEVER appear in source code (`.py` files, notebook cells, configuration files that are committed to the repo). (2) API keys MUST NEVER be checked into git: the `.env` file is added to `.gitignore`, and a `.env.example` placeholder file with the same key names but fake values is committed instead so newcomers see the shape of the variables. (3) API keys are read at runtime through `os.getenv(...)` or `os.environ[...]`, after `python-dotenv`'s `load_dotenv()` has populated `os.environ` from the local `.env` file (the ch-02 entry-017 baseline). The python-dotenv 1.2.2 chub guide's "Common Pitfalls" section makes the rule explicit: "Do not commit `.env` files with secrets. Add them to `.gitignore`." The chapter should also remind beginners that the same rule applies to notebooks — Jupyter notebook cells are part of the project's history and are often shared or committed, so a key pasted into a notebook cell is effectively checked in. License: python-dotenv is BSD-3-Clause; the GitHub-curated Python `.gitignore` is MIT-licensed and free to copy.
paraphrase: Three rules, applied together: never put an API key in source code, never check one into git, always read it from the environment at runtime. The first rule covers `.py` files, notebook cells, and any committed configuration file; pasting a key into a notebook and committing the notebook is the most common beginner slip. The second rule is a `.gitignore` line — `.env` must be in `.gitignore` — plus a committed `.env.example` placeholder file with the same key names and fake values, so newcomers can see what variables the project expects without ever seeing a real secret. The third rule is the code-side pattern: `from dotenv import load_dotenv; load_dotenv(); api_key = os.getenv("OPENAI_API_KEY")` at the top of the script, followed by an explicit "API key missing" check before the first request is sent. If a key is ever accidentally committed, it must be revoked at the provider immediately — rotating the key is the only safe response, because removing the file from a later commit does not erase it from the project's history. License: python-dotenv BSD-3-Clause and the GitHub `.gitignore` MIT — recommend freely with no flags.

### entry-060
source: Python Software Foundation — list reference documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/stdtypes.html#typesseq-list) and "For Loops" reference (https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-for-statement), Python 3.14.6; combined with the ch-06 entry-049 system/user/assistant role convention and the canonical request/response shape from entry-054.
used_in: ch-07
claim/finding: Defines the chapter's minimal conversation loop. A conversation is a Python `list` of `{"role": ..., "content": ...}` dicts, initially containing the system message (and optionally the first user message). To send the conversation: build the JSON body from the list, POST it, parse the response, extract the assistant's reply as a `{"role": "assistant", "content": "..."}` dict, and `messages.append(assistant_reply)` so the next call has the full history. The loop is `for user_turn in iter_input(): messages.append({"role": "user", "content": user_turn}); reply = call_chat_completion(messages); messages.append(reply); print(reply["content"])`. For ch-07 the loop is deliberately small: one system message, one user message, one assistant reply, and the program ends. Tool-calling, function-calling, structured outputs, streaming, and any multi-turn agent logic are explicitly deferred — tool calls are ch-09, structured workflows are ch-11, multi-agent is ch-15, and streaming is mentioned in one sentence here and then set aside. License: not applicable (the loop is built from stdlib list operations and the request/response shape from entry-054).
paraphrase: The smallest useful chat-completion program holds a conversation as a Python list. The list starts with one system message that sets the model's behavior, optionally followed by a first user message; each round of the conversation appends the new user turn, sends the whole list to the API, reads the assistant's reply from the response, and appends that reply as a `{"role": "assistant", "content": ...}` dict before the next call. The chapter's loop is intentionally small — a `while True:` that prompts the user, calls the API, prints the reply, and exits on a sentinel like `quit`. The chapter should not introduce tool-calling, function-calling, structured outputs, conversation persistence, or streaming; those are explicit later-chapter topics (ch-09 for tools, ch-11 for structured workflows, ch-15 for multi-agent, with streaming mentioned in one sentence so the beginner knows it exists and then deferred). The takeaway is the append-the-reply-to-the-list pattern, which is the seed that every later chapter's conversation loop will grow out of.

Verified caveat for the prose writer: in smolagents 1.26.0 `ApiModel` is the renamed base class and is importable, but instantiating `ApiModel(model_id="...")` directly raises `NotImplementedError: Subclasses must implement this method to create a client` because `ApiModel.create_client()` is abstract; the concrete subclass for the Hugging Face Inference API used in the v1.26.0 quickstart is `InferenceClientModel` (also exported from `smolagents`, and the class the quickstart actually instantiates). Because earlier chapters referred to the older name `HfApiModel`, the import line also brings in `ApiModel` so the chapter can mention the rename once in a sidebar — but the code that actually runs uses `InferenceClientModel`, the concrete subclass for the Hugging Face Inference API. Beginners should not be confused by seeing both names on the page; the chapter can show both in the import line and explain the rename in two sentences, then move on.

source: smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py`; `ApiModel.__init__` signature introspected with `inspect.signature(ApiModel.__init__)` on 2026-08-01 ↙ `(self, model_id: str, custom_role_conversions: dict[str, str] | None = None, client: typing.Any | None = None, requests_per_minute: float | None = None, retry: bool = True, **kwargs)`. Everything else has a safe default — rate limits, retries, the API client itself.
