### entry-064
source: smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`; `CodeAgent.__init__` signature introspected with `inspect.signature(CodeAgent.__init__)` on 2026-08-01 ↙ `(self, tools: list[smolagents.tools.Tool], model: smolagents.models.Model, prompt_templates: PromptTemplates | None = None, additional_authorized_imports: list[str] | None = None, planning_interval: int | None = None, executor: PythonExecutor = None, executor_type: Literal["local","blaxel","e2b","modal","docker"] = "local", executor_kwargs: dict[str, Any] | None = None, max_print_outputs_length: int | None = None, stream_outputs: bool = False, use_structured_outputs_internally: bool = False, code_block_tags: str | tuple[str, str] | None = None, **kwargs)`. Cross-checked against the v1.26.0 quickstart example `CodeAgent(tools=[], model=InferenceClientModel())`.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: `CodeAgent.__init__` requires two positional arguments — `tools` (a list of `Tool` objects; an empty list is permitted) and `model` (a `Model` object such as `InferenceClientModel()`) — both have no default. Optional arguments include `prompt_templates`, `additional_authorized_imports`, `planning_interval`, `executor` (an explicit executor instance), `executor_type="local"`, `executor_kwargs`, `max_print_outputs_length`, `stream_outputs`, `use_structured_outputs_internally`, `code_block_tags`, and `**kwargs`. Passing `tools=[]` yields a no-tools agent that can still run Python and call `final_answer`; passing no `model=` raises `TypeError` because the field is required. The verified v1.26.0 quickstart example is `CodeAgent(tools=[], model=InferenceClientModel())`.
paraphrase: A `CodeAgent` is built from two required pieces: the list of tools it may use (an empty list is fine for the first run, which only does arithmetic), and the model it should call (a `Model` wrapper such as `InferenceClientModel()`). The chapter should not list every optional parameter at this stage — only the two required ones and a one-sentence forward pointer that the agent can be told to run inside a sandbox (Docker, E2B, Modal, or Blaxel) by setting `executor_type="docker"` and similar; deeper sandbox coverage belongs in ch-14.

### entry-065
source: smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\tools.py`; `tool` decorator source and `Tool` base class introspected on 2026-08-01. The decorator's docstring states the contract verbatim: "Function to convert into a Tool subclass. Should have type hints for each input and a type hint for the output. Should also have a docstring including the description of the function and an 'Args:' part where each argument is described." The decorator reads the function name (becomes the tool's `name`), the docstring (becomes the `description`), the parameter type hints (become the `inputs` JSON schema), and the return type hint (becomes the `output_type`). Anchors ch-01 entry-006 (tool = typed Python function with docstring) and ch-01 entry-008 (`@tool` decorator contract); does not duplicate those entries.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The `@tool` decorator wraps an ordinary typed Python function into a `Tool` subclass. It requires type hints on every parameter AND on the return value (the decorator raises `TypeHintParsingException` otherwise), and the docstring must contain both a function description and an `Args:` block listing each argument. For ch-08, the chapter-8 specific practice is one tiny custom tool — either a one-line calculator (`add(a: int, b: int) -> int`) or a `word_count(text: str) -> int` helper — that demonstrates the decorator's contract without any external dependency.
paraphrase: A "tool" in smolagents is a Python function with `@tool` written above the `def` line. The decorator reads the function's name, type hints, and docstring and turns them into a structured description the agent can reason about. Three rules must hold: every parameter needs a type hint, the return value needs a type hint, and the docstring needs to describe what the function does and what each argument means — a vague docstring makes the agent skip the tool, which is one of ch-08's troubleshooting beats. The smallest reasonable chapter-8 tool is a one-line calculator or word-count helper — anything with a clear contract and no network or filesystem dependency.

### entry-066
source: smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`; `CodeAgent.run` signature and docstring introspected with `inspect.signature(CodeAgent.run)` on 2026-08-01 ↙ `(self, task: str, stream: bool = False, reset: bool = True, images: list['PIL.Image.Image'] | None = None, additional_args: dict | None = None, max_steps: int | None = None, return_full_result: bool | None = None) -> typing.Any | smolagents.agents.RunResult`. The installed docstring documents: "stream (`bool`): Whether to run in streaming mode. If `True`, returns a generator that yields each step as it is executed … If `False`, executes all steps internally and returns only the final answer after completion"; "return_full_result (`bool`): Whether to return the full [`RunResult`] object or just the final answer output"; "max_steps (`int`): Maximum number of steps the agent can take to solve the task".
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: `CodeAgent.run(task, ...)` takes a `task` string and returns either the final answer (the default, an `Any` value) or, when `return_full_result=True`, a `RunResult` object holding the full transcript. `stream=False` (default) runs all steps internally and returns only the final value; `stream=True` returns a generator that yields each step as it executes. `max_steps` overrides the agent's default step limit, and `reset=True` (default) clears the conversation between runs. For ch-08 the only argument the chapter calls is `task`; the chapter mentions the final-answer vs RunResult distinction in one sentence and defers streaming and `return_full_result` to later chapters.
paraphrase: To actually use the agent, the chapter calls `agent.run("…")` with the task as a plain English string. By default the call runs every internal step, returns the final answer, and clears the previous run's memory — so the chapter's example prints that return value and is done. Two other modes exist (streaming so the reader sees each step, and "return the full transcript"), but those are deferred; ch-08 only needs to know that `agent.run(task)` returns the answer.

### entry-067
source: smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`; `CodeAgent.step` and `_run_stream` confirmed present via `dir(CodeAgent)` on 2026-08-01, and `MultiStepAgent` is the parent class of `CodeAgent` per the source. Cross-referenced with the v1.26.0 quickstart description and with ch-01 entry-007 (Anthropic "workflows vs agents").
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: `CodeAgent` inherits from `MultiStepAgent`, whose `step()` method runs one iteration of the agent loop — the model proposes an action (a Python code block or a tool call), the code is executed by the sandboxed `LocalPythonExecutor`, the printed output is captured, and that output becomes part of the next prompt. `agent.run()` simply calls `step()` in a loop until the model produces a `final_answer` tool call or `max_steps` is reached. The beginner mental model is "one iteration = one step", and each step has three visible parts: the model's code proposal, the printed execution output, and the new memory entry.
paraphrase: Behind the scenes the agent is doing the same thing over and over: it asks the model to write a small chunk of Python, runs that Python through a sandboxed interpreter, captures the printed output, and feeds the result back into the next prompt. Each pass through this loop is one "step". The chapter's mental model is "one iteration = one step", and the chapter says so once so the reader understands why the loop sometimes prints many blocks of code before producing an answer.

### entry-068
source: smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\default_tools.py`; `FinalAnswerTool` source introspected on 2026-08-01 ↙ `class FinalAnswerTool(Tool): name = "final_answer"; description = "Provides a final answer to the given problem."; inputs = {"answer": {"type": "any", "description": "The final answer to the problem"}}; output_type = "any"; def forward(self, answer: Any) -> Any: return answer`. The `MultiStepAgent` base class detects `final_answer` calls in each step and breaks out of the run loop when one is seen.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: `FinalAnswerTool` is a built-in tool whose only job is to return whatever the model chose as the answer; calling it ends the agent loop because the parent `MultiStepAgent` sees a `final_answer` tool invocation and returns. By default it is added to the agent's tool list automatically (the `add_base_tools=True` default on `MultiStepAgent.__init__`), so a `CodeAgent` constructed with `tools=[]` still has `final_answer` available and can stop the loop. The chapter describes this as "the agent ends its turn by calling a built-in `final_answer` tool — the value passed in is what `agent.run(...)` returns".
paraphrase: The agent knows it is finished because there is a built-in tool called `final_answer` whose only job is to hand back whatever value the model decided on. When the model decides the answer is ready, it calls `final_answer(answer=...)`, the loop ends, and that `answer` value is what `agent.run(...)` returns. The chapter does not introduce custom termination conditions at this level — `final_answer` is the only stop signal ch-08 needs.

### entry-069
source: Hugging Face — "User access tokens" (https://huggingface.co/docs/hub/security-tokens), accessed 2026-08-01; smolagents==1.26.0 installed in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py`, `InferenceClientModel.__init__` source introspected on 2026-08-01 ↙ default `model_id` is `"Qwen/Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Thinking"` per the v1.26.0 docstring; the docstring also states: "token (`str`, *optional*): Token used by the Hugging Face API for authentication … If not provided, the class will try to use environment variable 'HF_TOKEN', else use the token stored in the Hugging Face CLI configuration." Cross-referenced with ch-02 entry-017 (python-dotenv 1.2.2 chub guide + `os.environ` pattern) and ch-07 entry-053 (the same `load_dotenv()` + `os.getenv()` baseline).
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The Hugging Face Inference API authenticates by reading the `HF_TOKEN` environment variable (or the token stored in the Hugging Face CLI configuration) when no explicit `token=` argument is passed; the value can be set in the shell, in a `.env` file loaded by `python-dotenv`, or via `huggingface-cli login`. The chapter checks that `HF_TOKEN` is set before the first run and prints a clear "missing token" error rather than failing inside the model call. For the ch-08 default model the chapter picks `Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct` — a coder-tuned 7-billion-parameter model that fits CodeAgent's code-writing paradigm and is small enough for beginners on modest hardware; this is NOT the v1.26.0 package default, which is `Qwen/Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Thinking` (an 80B model impractical as a beginner default).
paraphrase: Before the first run the reader needs a Hugging Face access token in the `HF_TOKEN` environment variable. The cleanest pattern — already established in ch-02 and ch-07 — is to put `HF_TOKEN=hf_...` in a `.env` file and let `python-dotenv`'s `load_dotenv()` populate `os.environ`. The chapter checks for the token up front and prints a clear "missing token" error rather than failing deep inside the API call. For the model itself the chapter picks one specific model id, `Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct`, a coder-tuned 7B model that fits the CodeAgent paradigm and stays small enough for beginners to reason about. The chapter should NOT expose the package's 80B default — that model is not appropriate as a beginner recommendation.

### entry-070
source: am-research dispatch prompt for ch-08 (2026-08-01), invoking ch-01 entry-061 corrective (the operative `HfApiModel ↙ ApiModel` rename fact). This entry is a prose-side requirement, not an API claim.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The Phase 6 writer for ch-08 MUST include the one-time "Naming note" sidebar in the first prose paragraph that imports smolagents. Do not repeat this note in ch-09..ch-18.
paraphrase: The writer of ch-08 places a short three-sentence sidebar in the very first prose paragraph that imports smolagents — saying (1) that `HfApiModel` was the older name in earlier smolagents releases and is now `ApiModel`, (2) that this is the only rename the book needs to flag, and (3) that later chapters use `ApiModel` without repeating the note. The sidebar appears exactly once in the entire book, in ch-08, and the writer must explicitly not repeat it in any of ch-09 through ch-18.

### entry-071
source: Hugging Face smolagents — "Secure code execution" tutorial (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/tutorials/secure_code_execution), accessed 2026-08-01. The tutorial's "Our local Python executor" section documents that the local executor is a custom `LocalPythonExecutor` that walks the AST of generated code, denies imports by default (pre-authorized builtins: `statistics`, `numpy`, `itertools`, `time`, `queue`, `collections`, `math`, `random`, `re`, `datetime`, `stat`, `unicordata`), caps total elementary operations to prevent infinite loops, and rejects access to unauthorized submodules. The same tutorial includes a `WARNING` block stating: "It's important to understand that no local python sandbox can ever be completely secure … Running LLM-generated code in your local environment always carries some inherent risk. The only way to run LLM-generated code with truly robust security isolation is to use remote execution options like E2B or Docker." Cross-referenced with ch-01 entry-008 (smolagents' code-executing agents run model-generated Python).
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: Even on the default `executor_type="local"`, smolagents does NOT use the vanilla Python interpreter — it uses a custom `LocalPythonExecutor` that adds a layer of protection (denied imports by default, an operation cap, blocked submodule access). However, the project's own tutorial warns explicitly that no local sandbox can ever be 100% safe, and that real isolation requires a remote executor (E2B, Docker, Modal, or Blaxel). For ch-08, the chapter's safety rule is: the first run should be on a small, harmless task (something like "add these two numbers"); detailed sandboxing options are deferred to ch-14.
paraphrase: The agent writes its own Python and runs it on the reader's machine. smolagents does ship a custom local executor that adds a layer of protection — denied imports by default, a cap on operations, blocked access to most submodules — but the project's own tutorial warns that no local sandbox can ever be 100% safe. The chapter therefore tells the beginner to keep the first run on a small, harmless task (something like "add these two numbers") and to defer real sandboxing — Docker, E2B, Modal, Blaxel — to ch-14, where it is covered in depth.

### entry-072
source: am-research synthesis from smolagents==1.26.0 installed source (verified error behaviors on 2026-08-01: `HfApiModel` raises `ImportError` per entry-061; `ApiModel(...)` instantiation raises `NotImplementedError` per entry-062; `LocalPythonExecutor` blocks non-authorized imports per entry-071), plus chub `smolagents/package`, plus Hugging Face HF token docs (https://huggingface.co/docs/hub/security-tokens). Cross-referenced with ch-02 entry-017 and ch-07 entry-053 for the env-var baseline.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: Four error categories beginners will hit on the first agent run, with one-line preventive fixes the chapter should include. (a) Missing `HF_TOKEN`: `InferenceClientModel()` constructs fine but the first `generate()` call raises `HfHubHTTPError` (HTTP 401); preventive check is `assert os.getenv("HF_TOKEN"), "set HF_TOKEN in your .env file"` before constructing the model. (b) Private or wrong repo id: pointing `model_id` at a private or non-existent repository returns 404 from the HF Hub; the chapter shows the reader how to read the model-id line in the error. (c) Vague tool docstring: the agent ignores the tool and falls back to raw Python or fails to converge; the chapter's first tool example uses a specific docstring with an `Args:` block. (d) Forgot to import the tool function: passing a `Tool` built from `my_tool` while forgetting `from my_tools import my_tool` raises `NameError` at agent construction time, not at `.run()` time.
paraphrase: Four mistakes beginners will make on the first run, and how the chapter heads each off. (1) Forgetting `HF_TOKEN` — the model instantiates fine but the first call returns 401; the chapter adds a one-line "missing token" check before the agent is built. (2) Naming the model with a private or wrong repository id — the call returns 404; the chapter shows how to read the model-id line in the error. (3) Writing a tool whose docstring is too vague — the agent skips the tool and the loop runs longer than expected; the chapter's first tool example has a short, specific docstring with an `Args:` section. (4) Forgetting to `import` the tool function — Python raises `NameError` before the agent ever runs; the chapter shows the import line explicitly.

### entry-073
source: am-research synthesis from smolagents==1.26.0 installed source (`Model` base class and `generate` method signatures introspected on 2026-08-01; `ChatMessage` dataclass in `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py` with fields `role`, `content`, `tool_calls`, `raw`, `token_usage`) and ch-07 entry-058 (no-network error handling pattern).
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: For ch-08's "demonstrate the agent loop without a network call" demo, the chapter uses a tiny stub model — a small class that subclasses `Model` and implements `generate(messages, ...)` to return a `ChatMessage` with a fixed answer (e.g. a one-line stub that always returns `"5"` and short-circuits the loop by emitting `final_answer`). Justification: a stub lets the reader see the entire loop run end-to-end (tool invocation, final-answer step, return value) without needing `HF_TOKEN` or any network access; the deliberately-broken-model alternative was considered and rejected because it fails before the loop starts, so the reader never sees what the agent actually does. The stub must return a `ChatMessage` with `role=MessageRole.ASSISTANT` and a string `content`; the chapter wraps it as a one-paragraph aside rather than a code listing.
paraphrase: For readers who want to see the agent loop without setting up a token or hitting the network, the chapter includes a short "offline demo" box. The box shows a tiny stub model — a class with one method that returns a fixed string answer wrapped in a `ChatMessage`. Wired in place of `InferenceClientModel`, the stub runs the full loop locally: the model "thinks", the agent executes code, and `agent.run(...)` returns the stub's answer. The deliberately-broken-model alternative (just instantiate with a bad token and let it crash) was considered and rejected — it stops before the loop starts, so the reader never sees what the agent actually does. The stub approach is one extra paragraph and gives the reader a working offline demo.

## ch-09 — Give Agents Useful Tools

### entry-074
source: chub `smolagents/package` (cached maintainer guide for smolagents==1.24.0, retrieved 2026-08-01), validated against installed smolagents==1.26.0 at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\tools.py`; `inspect.signature(smolagents.tool)` and `inspect.getsource(smolagents.tool)` run 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: The exact public import is `from smolagents import tool`. `@tool` accepts a plain Python callable and returns an instantiated, dynamically created `Tool` subclass. The decorator derives `name`, `description`, `inputs`, and `output_type` from the function's schema, binds the original function body as the tool's `forward` method, and preserves its signature. The decorated name therefore refers to a `Tool` object, not the original function, although calling the object invokes the wrapped function.
paraphrase: Write an ordinary Python function, then place `@tool` above it. smolagents turns that function into a tool object the agent can inspect and call; the body still does the work, while its name, types, and docstring become the agent-facing contract.

### entry-075
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0, `smolagents._function_type_hints_utils.get_json_schema` and `smolagents.tool` source introspected 2026-08-01; chub `smolagents/package` (1.24.0) confirms the same core contract.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: Every parameter must have a Python type hint and a docstring description, and a non-zero-argument tool must have a return annotation. `get_json_schema()` converts these into the JSON schema sent to the model; absent parameter hints, absent argument descriptions, or an absent return hint cause schema/decorator construction errors before the agent runs. The Python function name becomes `Tool.name` verbatim, so beginner tools should use descriptive snake_case names such as `word_count`.
paraphrase: The type hints are not optional decoration: smolagents reads them to tell the model which arguments are legal. Name every tool clearly, annotate every input and the return value, and describe every input in the docstring so mistakes fail while the program starts rather than halfway through an agent run.

### entry-076
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 docstring-schema parser in `smolagents._function_type_hints_utils.py`, inspected 2026-08-01; chub `smolagents/package` (1.24.0) documents Google-style `Args:` examples.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: The tool description is derived from the function docstring's leading description, while parameter descriptions come from a recognized argument section. The installed parser supports Google-style `Args:`/`Arguments:`/`Parameters:` sections and NumPy-style `Parameters` followed by a dashed underline. A useful description must say both what the tool does and when to use it. Thus “Counts words— is legal but weak, while “Count the number of words in a string. Use this when the user asks how long a piece of text is.— gives the model a selection rule.
paraphrase: Treat the docstring as instructions to the agent, not as a note to yourself. Google and NumPy layouts both work, but the opening description should include a clear “use this when…— sentence so the model can distinguish nearby tools.
variance_resolution: chub's 1.24.0 guide demonstrates only Google style; installed 1.26.0 parser source explicitly recognizes both Google and NumPy forms, so the chapter may show both while using Google style in the smallest runnable.

### entry-077
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `Tool.__call__`, `handle_agent_output_types`, and `FinalAnswerTool.forward` source introspected 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: Verified return behavior differs from the dispatch's proposed coercion wording. A normal decorated-tool call returns the Python value from `forward()` unchanged: a `str` remains `str`, a `dict` remains `dict`, a number remains numeric, and `None` remains `None`. With `sanitize_inputs_outputs=True`, strings become `AgentText`; dicts and ordinary numbers are still not automatically JSON-encoded/stringified. `FinalAnswerTool.forward(answer)` likewise returns `answer` unchanged. String returns are nevertheless preferred for predictable model-facing observations; tools wanting JSON text must call `json.dumps()` explicitly, and numeric tools wanting text must call `str()` explicitly.
paraphrase: Return a string unless another type is genuinely useful. smolagents 1.26.0 does not silently turn dictionaries into JSON or numbers into text at the tool boundary, so make that conversion yourself when the model should receive a textual result; avoid accidental `None` by returning a clear success or failure message.
variance_resolution: This is a material correction to the requested “dicts are JSON-encoded; numbers are coerced to strings— claim. Installed 1.26.0 source is authoritative for this book's pinned environment.

### entry-078
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 agent prompt templates and `MultiStepAgent._setup_tools`/`initialize_system_prompt` source inspected 2026-08-01; chub `smolagents/package` (1.24.0) describes agent tool selection from typed names and descriptions.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: Agent construction stores tools keyed by `tool.name` and always ensures a `final_answer` tool exists. The initialized system prompt renders available tools with names, descriptions, and input schemas. On each step, the model chooses an action/tool from that advertised set; therefore descriptions directly influence selection. ToolCallingAgent emits structured tool calls, while CodeAgent writes code that invokes the advertised tool names.
paraphrase: The agent does not discover what a function means by reading its body. It sees a menu made from each tool's name, description, and argument schema, then chooses from that menu one step at a time. Specific descriptions produce better choices than vague labels.

### entry-079
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `dir(smolagents)`, `inspect.signature` for required built-ins, and `smolagents.agents.TOOL_MAPPING`, verified 2026-08-01; duckduckgo-search==8.1.1 verified with `importlib.metadata`.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: Public built-ins present in 1.26.0 include `DuckDuckGoSearchTool(max_results=10, rate_limit=1.0)`, `VisitWebpageTool(max_output_length=40000)`, `WikipediaSearchTool(user_agent=..., language='en', content_type='text', extract_format='WIKI')`, `WebSearchTool(max_results=10, engine='duckduckgo')`, `SpeechToTextTool`, `PythonInterpreterTool(authorized_imports=None, timeout_seconds=30)`, `FinalAnswerTool`, `UserInputTool`, and `ApiWebSearchTool(endpoint='', api_key='', api_key_name='', headers=None, params=None, rate_limit=1.0)`. The installed base-tool mapping is narrower: `python_interpreter`, `web_search` (a `DuckDuckGoSearchTool`), and `visit_webpage`.
paraphrase: smolagents ships several ready-made tools, but “available to import— does not mean “automatically attached.— Beginners should import only the built-in they need and pass its instance in `tools=[...]`; the package's base-tool shortcut adds only a small default set.
variance_resolution: This list is pinned to smolagents==1.26.0. `WebSearchTool` and `ApiWebSearchTool` are separate public classes, while `add_base_tools` currently maps the `web_search` name specifically to `DuckDuckGoSearchTool`; re-check on upgrades.

### entry-080
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.__init__` and `_setup_tools` source plus `CodeAgent`/`ToolCallingAgent` signatures, inspected 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: `add_base_tools` is inherited through `**kwargs` by both `CodeAgent` and `ToolCallingAgent`; its exact default is `False`. When true, both receive web search and webpage visiting, but `python_interpreter` is added only to ToolCallingAgent because CodeAgent already executes Python. `final_answer` is added regardless. Explicit tool instances provide a smaller, auditable capability set and configurable constructor values; `add_base_tools=True` is a convenience for exploratory tasks that genuinely need the whole default set.
paraphrase: Prefer `tools=[...]` in the chapter so readers can see exactly what the agent may do. Use `add_base_tools=True` only when the default search-and-browsing bundle is intentional; it is off unless requested.

### entry-081
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `ToolCallingAgent.execute_tool_call`, step-processing, `AgentToolExecutionError`, and CodeAgent local-executor integration source inspected 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: A ToolCallingAgent invokes a selected tool as a normal Python call with sanitized inputs/outputs. If the tool raises, smolagents catches the underlying exception, wraps it as `AgentToolExecutionError` with the tool name, supplied arguments, exception type/message, and guidance to fix the call, records that error in the current step, and continues the bounded agent loop so the model can retry; it does not terminate the whole run immediately merely because one tool call failed. CodeAgent instead exposes tool objects to its configured Python executor, so calls occur while model-generated code is evaluated.
paraphrase: Tool failures become feedback for the next agent step. The model sees that the named tool failed and why, then may correct its arguments or choose another route; developers should still catch expected network/file errors inside the tool and return a concise recovery message rather than use exceptions as ordinary control flow.

### entry-082
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `CodeAgent.create_python_executor`, `LocalPythonExecutor`, and `ToolCallingAgent.execute_tool_call` source inspected 2026-08-01; cross-reference ch-08 entry-071 and ch-14 forward pointer.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: Decorated functions are ordinary Python and may close over variables, mutate globals, or perform file/network side effects. ToolCallingAgent calls them in the host process with the full authority of the user's Python code. CodeAgent registers tools as callable/static tools inside its configured executor; with the default local executor, generated code is AST-restricted, but invoking an authorized tool still runs that tool's Python implementation and its side effects. This carries forward the LocalPythonExecutor caveat already established in entry-071; ch-09 should reference it once and defer isolation details to ch-14.
paraphrase: A tool is a capability boundary, not a safety boundary. If its function can write a file, send a request, or change shared state, the agent can trigger that behavior; keep beginner tools read-only or harmless and revisit real isolation in ch-14.

### entry-083
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 decorator/schema and `MultiStepAgent._setup_tools`/`_validate_tools_and_managed_agents` source inspected 2026-08-01; `FinalAnswerTool` implementation verified.
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: Four beginner errors deserve explicit checks: missing type hints or argument descriptions prevent schema creation; vague/absent docstrings prevent construction or cause poor selection; returning `None` produces an actual `None` rather than a useful final-answer-like value; and naming a custom tool `final_answer` shadows the framework's automatic `FinalAnswerTool` because `_setup_tools` uses `setdefault`. Duplicate names among explicitly supplied tools/managed agents raise `ValueError`, but a single custom `final_answer` is not rejected, making that collision especially dangerous.
paraphrase: Before running, inspect four things: every input and return is typed, the docstring says when to use the tool, every path returns a useful value, and the name does not collide with `final_answer` or another tool. These small checks prevent most first-tool failures.

### entry-084
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `InferenceClientModel(model_id=..., token=None, ...)`, `CodeAgent`, `tool`, and `FinalAnswerTool` signatures/source inspected 2026-08-01; chub `smolagents/package` consulted first; model choice carried forward from ch-08 entry-069 (`Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct`).
used_in: ch-09
claim/finding: The smallest runnable extends ch-08 rather than introducing a new agent class: construct `InferenceClientModel(model_id='Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct', token=os.getenv('HF_TOKEN'))`, define two or three tiny typed `@tool` functions (for example `word_count`, a deterministic text transform, and one simple lookup), pass them explicitly to `CodeAgent`, and call `.run()`. CodeAgent's prompt already includes `final_answer`, and `.run()` returns that final value by default. Check `HF_TOKEN` before model construction and exit with “Set HF_TOKEN or switch to a model your key supports— so auth/model-entitlement failures are understandable. Keep `DuckDuckGoSearchTool` as an optional explicit third capability guarded by `try/except`, or use local-only `PythonInterpreterTool` in a ToolCallingAgent sidebar rather than muddling CodeAgent's already-present Python execution.
paraphrase: Reuse the first-agent shape from ch-08, add only a few small tools, and let the same agent solve a prompt that needs more than one capability. Fail before the network call when no token is present, and keep search failure non-fatal so the example remains teachable.

---

## ch-10 — Guide Agents with Instructions and Memory

### entry-085
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.__init__` (verified `instructions: str | None = None` kwarg) and `CodeAgent.initialize_system_prompt` / `ToolCallingAgent.initialize_system_prompt` source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (inspected 2026-08-01); cross-checked against the v1.26.0 official `MultiStepAgent` reference at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents, which labels the parameter "Custom instructions for the agent, will be inserted in the system prompt."; chub `smolagents/package` cached at 1.24.0 was consulted first but the parameter behavior is the same in both versions, so the cached chub guide is sufficient for the contract.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: The `instructions` kwarg is a user-level sentence (or short paragraph) of guidance that smolagents splices into the default system prompt — it does NOT replace the prompt. The two `initialize_system_prompt` implementations both pass `self.instructions` under the Jinja variable name `custom_instructions` to `populate_template(self.prompt_templates["system_prompt"], ...)`. In the bundled 1.26.0 default template the relevant block reads (paraphrased from a verbatim introspect of the literal template string): `{%- if custom_instructions %}\n{{custom_instructions}}\n{%- endif %}` placed between the final numbered rule ("11. Don't give up!") and the literal `Now Begin!` line. With a one-line `instructions="ALWAYS GREET WITH HOWDY."` value the rendered system prompt grew by exactly 25 characters (8 480 ↙ 8 505), confirming the insertion is a single block and is conditional on a non-empty value. Passing the same content via `prompt_templates={"system_prompt": ...}` is a completely different operation: it strips the tool list, the Thought/Code/Observation loop wording, the eleven numbered rules, the `<end_plan>` tag instruction, and the conditional `custom_instructions` block — which is why the brief recommends `instructions` for almost every beginner use case.
paraphrase: Treat `instructions` as a sticky note for the agent that is appended to the system prompt. It is the right place to write a one- or two-sentence house style ("Answer in plain English, no bullet points" or "Always end your reply with the source you used"), a domain framing ("You are helping a beginner Python student with short, runnable examples"), or a constraint ("Never invent URLs; only quote URLs the model has actually seen this turn"). Do not paste the full system prompt into `instructions` — that is a different operation, and the chapter will show why in entry-086.
quote: "Custom instructions for the agent, will be inserted in the system prompt." (v1.26.0 reference docs, `MultiStepAgent.instructions` parameter description)

### entry-086
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (no `system_prompt` kwarg in 1.26.0, confirmed by triggering `TypeError: MultiStepAgent.__init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'system_prompt'` on 2026-08-01); the canonical override path is `prompt_templates={"system_prompt": ...}` built on top of the exported `EMPTY_PROMPT_TEMPLATES` dict (also in `smolagents/agents.py`); the v1.26.0 reference page documents the `prompt_templates` parameter at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents and the `PromptTemplates` typed dict.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: In 1.26.0 there is no `system_prompt=` kwarg on `MultiStepAgent.__init__`. The only way to replace the default system prompt wholesale is to pass `prompt_templates` whose `"system_prompt"` key is your custom string. `PromptTemplates` is a `TypedDict` with four keys: `system_prompt: str`, `planning: PlanningPromptTemplate` (a sub-dict), `managed_agent: ManagedAgentPromptTemplate` (a sub-dict), and `final_answer: FinalAnswerPromptTemplate` (a sub-dict). The framework exposes `EMPTY_PROMPT_TEMPLATES` so a caller can write `prompt_templates={**EMPTY_PROMPT_TEMPLATES, "system_prompt": "..."}` to override only the system prompt while keeping the default planning/final-answer templates. When a custom `system_prompt` is provided, the rendered prompt no longer advertises the available tools (no `{{tools}}` rendering), no longer describes the Thought/Code/Observation loop, no longer prints the `final_answer` rule, and no longer carries the `{% if custom_instructions %}` block — so the agent will not know which tools it has or how to call them. A live test on 2026-08-01 confirmed: `CodeAgent(tools=[hello], model=Stub(), max_steps=1, prompt_templates=custom)` whose `system_prompt` is the literal string "CUSTOM ONLY" produces a 63-character system prompt and the resulting step throws `Error in code parsing` on the first iteration because the agent has no scaffolding to emit `<code>...</code>` blocks. The chapter's beginner rule is therefore: use `instructions` to add guidance, and do not touch `prompt_templates` until ch-11/15 introduce it deliberately.
paraphrase: The single most common beginner mistake in this chapter is reaching for "the system prompt" as if it were a free-form string. In 1.26.0 it is not a constructor argument; it is one slot inside a four-slot template dict, and replacing it kills the tool menu and the step loop with it. Tell beginners to use `instructions=` for everything that fits in a paragraph, and reserve `prompt_templates=` for ch-11/15 when the chapter deliberately wants to redesign the loop. The rule is "if you have to ask whether you should replace the system prompt, you should not."

### entry-087
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 default `prompt_templates["system_prompt"]` for `CodeAgent` introspected at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` on 2026-08-01 (template is a single multi-line Jinja string beginning "You are an expert assistant who can solve any task using code blobs…"); same template is consumed by `CodeAgent.initialize_system_prompt` (which passes `tools`, `managed_agents`, `authorized_imports`, `custom_instructions`, `code_block_opening_tag`, and `code_block_closing_tag` into `populate_template`) and by `ToolCallingAgent.initialize_system_prompt` (which passes only `tools`, `managed_agents`, and `custom_instructions`); the framework's `PlanningPromptTemplate` and `FinalAnswerPromptTemplate` are populated from the same `prompt_templates` dict, with `planning.initial_plan`, `planning.update_plan_pre_messages`, `planning.update_plan_post_messages`, `final_answer.pre_messages`, and `final_answer.post_messages` as the named sub-templates.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: The default system prompt that beginners see is a single Jinja string stored on each agent's `prompt_templates["system_prompt"]`. For `CodeAgent` it is the same prompt that appears in the chapter's runnable output: it starts with "You are an expert assistant who can solve any task using code blobs", introduces a Thought/Code/Observation cycle, then shows three worked examples using the `<code>...</code>` tag pair, then lists the eleven numbered rules (rule 7 says "Don't name any new variable with the same name as a tool: for instance don't name a variable 'final_answer'"), then conditionally appends the `custom_instructions` block (entry-085), then ends with the literal "Now Begin!" line. The `ToolCallingAgent` system prompt is a sibling template that uses the same `{%- if custom_instructions %}{{custom_instructions}}{%- endif %}` injection point and the same `{{tools}}` rendering, but describes JSON tool calls instead of `<code>` blocks. The `planning` sub-template adds the model's `<end_plan>` stop sequence and the `update_plan_pre_messages`/`update_plan_post_messages` history-aware revisions; the `final_answer` sub-template adds the recovery path used when a step throws. The chapter's prose should describe this layered structure at a high level: a system prompt that lists tools and rules, a planning sub-template that produces `PlanningStep`s, and a final-answer sub-template that produces the `final_answer` tool call — without teaching the user how to author Jinja.
paraphrase: Beginners do not need to read the default system prompt, but they do need to know it exists in three named pieces. The system prompt tells the model what kind of worker it is and what tools it has; the planning template tells it how to re-plan periodically; the final-answer template tells it how to recover when a step fails. The `instructions` parameter adds a small block to the system prompt; the `prompt_templates` parameter can replace any of the three pieces wholesale. Both knobs matter in later chapters; in ch-10 the chapter should only touch `instructions` and `planning_interval`.

### entry-088
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified `planning_interval: int | None = None` kwarg) and `_run_stream` source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the planning trigger reads: `if self.planning_interval is not None and (self.step_number == 1 or (self.step_number - 1) % self.planning_interval == 0):`); the v1.26.0 official reference documents `planning_interval` as "Interval at which the agent will run a planning step" at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents; chub `smolagents/package` consulted first; default verified as `None` on 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: `planning_interval` is an optional integer that forces a `PlanningStep` to run on a fixed cadence inside the ReAct loop. The default is `None` (no periodic re-planning). When set to a positive integer `N`, a planning step is emitted whenever `step_number == 1` (so a plan exists before the first action) and again whenever `(step_number - 1) % N == 0`, which yields planning steps before action 1, before action `N+1`, before action `2N+1`, and so on. The planning step uses `self.prompt_templates["planning"]["initial_plan"]` (or `update_plan_pre_messages`/`update_plan_post_messages` for revisions) and renders against the same `tools` and `managed_agents` that the system prompt uses. The trade-off is purely a token-vs-control one: each planning step is an extra model call that costs input tokens for the entire step history and output tokens for a written plan; in return the model gets a structured chance to re-survey what it knows, throw out a dead end, and pick a new tool path. The chapter's beginner rule is "leave `planning_interval=None` until a task demonstrably needs more than ten steps; then try `planning_interval=5` or `planning_interval=10` and watch the token bill." For very long tasks the framework's `max_steps=20` ceiling (entry-091) is the harder limit; `planning_interval` is a quality knob, not a safety knob.
paraphrase: `planning_interval` is a step counter that asks the agent to stop, write a short plan ("facts survey ↙ plan ↙ `<end_plan>` tag"), and then keep going. The default is off, which is right for short tasks. For tasks that need a lot of steps, a small interval (every 4 to 6 actions) is a cheap way to keep the model from drifting; for short tasks the same interval just burns tokens. Tell beginners to think of it as a re-plan timer, not a memory trick.
variance_resolution: Verified directly against 1.26.0 installed source; the v1.26.0 reference page documents the kwarg identically; chub's 1.24.0 guide describes the same trigger logic, so this entry is stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0.

### entry-089
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified `provide_run_summary: bool = False` kwarg) and `MultiStepAgent.__call__` source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the `__call__` method, which is invoked only by a manager agent, appends a `<summary_of_work>` block to the answer when `provide_run_summary=True`); the v1.26.0 reference page documents the parameter as "Whether to provide a run summary when called as a managed agent" at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents; chub `smolagents/package` consulted first.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: `provide_run_summary` is a top-level agent constructor kwarg with default `False`, but it does NOT cause anything to print at the end of a top-level `.run()` call. The kwarg is consumed only inside `MultiStepAgent.__call__`, which the source comment says is "called only by a managed agent": after the managed agent finishes its `self.run(full_task, **kwargs)`, the manager wraps the result with `self.prompt_templates["managed_agent"]["report"]` and, if `provide_run_summary=True`, appends a `<summary_of_work>...</summary_of_work>` block built from `self.write_memory_to_messages(summary_mode=True)`. For a top-level agent that is never called as a managed agent, this kwarg has no observable effect, and the beginner "turn it off for cleaner notebook output" framing in the dispatch brief is therefore technically a no-op for the chapter's standalone agents. The chapter's honest framing is: "this kwarg is for the multi-agent case in ch-15; for now leave it at `False`, which is the default." The kwarg is also explicitly absent from `CodeAgent.__init__` and `ToolCallingAgent.__init__` signatures — both forward the kwarg via `**kwargs` to `MultiStepAgent.__init__`, so `CodeAgent(..., provide_run_summary=True)` is legal even though the docs only document it on the parent class.
paraphrase: The brief says `provide_run_summary` "produces a final summary at the end of `.run()`". The verified behavior is narrower than that: it is a multi-agent knob that lets a child agent attach a step-by-step summary to the report it sends back to its manager. For the ch-10 standalone agents it is invisible; for the ch-15 manager/managed wiring it becomes important. Tell beginners the default (`False`) is correct, do not flip the switch, and bookmark the kwarg for the multi-agent chapter.
variance_resolution: Brief-correction: the dispatch characterized `provide_run_summary` as a top-level run output. Installed 1.26.0 source and the v1.26.0 reference page both confirm it fires only inside `__call__`, i.e. only when the agent is being used as a managed agent. Chub's 1.24.0 guide describes the same behavior. This is not a contradiction between two primary sources; it is a correction to the brief.

### entry-090
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.run` signature (verified `return_full_result: bool | None = None` kwarg) and `RunResult` dataclass source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (fields: `output: Any | None`, `state: Literal["success", "max_steps_error"]`, `steps: list[dict]`, `token_usage: TokenUsage | None`, `timing: Timing`; the deprecated `messages` field is documented as removed in 1.25.0 and the deprecated accessor raises `FutureWarning`); the v1.26.0 reference page documents `return_full_result` at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents; chub `smolagents/package` consulted first; live behavior verified on 2026-08-01 with a stub model.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: `return_full_result` controls whether `.run()` returns the bare final answer or a `RunResult` dataclass. Default is `False`; the agent returns whatever the last `FinalAnswerStep.output` was (an `AgentText` for a string answer, an `AgentImage` for an image, a Python object for any other return type). Setting `return_full_result=True` returns a `RunResult` with five fields: `output` (the same final answer), `state` (the literal string `"success"` or `"max_steps_error"`), `steps` (the full agent memory as a list of dicts from `AgentMemory.get_full_steps()`, including `TaskStep`, `ActionStep`, `PlanningStep`, and `FinalAnswerStep` records with their model inputs, observations, and tool calls), `token_usage` (a `TokenUsage(input_tokens, output_tokens)` aggregated from the steps, or `None` if any step did not report tokens), and `timing` (a `Timing(start_time, end_time, duration)` from the start of the run to its end). The dataclass also carries a deprecated `messages` property that warns `FutureWarning`; the chapter should not teach that accessor. The class is `@dataclass` so `RunResult.dict()` returns a JSON-serializable summary, which is what tests in ch-13 will assert against. `.run()` accepts `return_full_result=None` (the per-call default) which falls back to `self.return_full_result` (the constructor-level default), so a one-shot call can flip the mode without rebuilding the agent.
paraphrase: Most of the time `.run()` returns the answer as a string. When the chapter needs to inspect what the agent actually did — how many steps it took, what tokens it used, whether it ran out of steps — pass `return_full_result=True` (either on the constructor or on the specific `.run()` call) and read the five fields of the resulting `RunResult`: the final `output`, the literal `state` ("success" or "max_steps_error"), the per-step `steps` list (which is what ch-12 will show the reader), the optional `token_usage`, and the `timing`. ch-13 tests will assert on these fields; ch-12 will walk through them with the reader.
variance_resolution: The deprecated `messages` field appears in the `RunResult` docstring as "Deprecated version 1.22.0 / Parameter 'messages' is deprecated and will be removed in version 1.25. Please use 'steps' instead." A live test on 2026-08-01 against installed 1.26.0 still imports cleanly without raising the deprecation warning until the property is accessed, so the chapter can teach `RunResult.steps` and ignore the deprecated alias.

### entry-091
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified `max_steps: int = 20` kwarg) at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`; the v1.26.0 reference documents it as "Maximum number of steps the agent can take to solve the task"; a whole-package grep on 2026-08-01 confirms there is NO `max_duration`, `timeout`, or wall-clock-bound kwarg on `MultiStepAgent.__init__` (the only `duration` token in the package is the read-only `Timing` field on `RunResult` and `MemoryStep`); the v1.26.0 reference page lists no time-bound kwarg; chub `smolagents/package` consulted first.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: The agent's safety ceiling in 1.26.0 is an action-step count, not a wall-clock duration. `MultiStepAgent.__init__` accepts `max_steps: int = 20`; the same kwarg on `MultiStepAgent.run(..., max_steps=None)` falls back to `self.max_steps`. Planning calls happen alongside the action loop and add token spend and latency without consuming that action-step budget. When the action loop exhausts its budget, `.run()` returns a `RunResult` with `state="max_steps_error"`. There is no built-in wall-clock bound. A `concurrent.futures` or `asyncio` timeout stops waiting but does not terminate synchronous agent execution; use a watchdog flag checked each step when actual cooperative termination is required.
paraphrase: Beginners should think of `max_steps` as the safety valve that stops an agent that is going in circles. The default of 20 is fine for the chapter's runnable examples; raise it (e.g. to 30 or 50) only when the task genuinely needs many steps. There is no built-in wall-clock cap, so any "give up after N minutes" rule has to live outside the agent. The ch-13 testing chapter will read `RunResult.state == "max_steps_error"` to detect when the cap fired and assert the run completed before the cap.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source, the v1.26.0 reference page, and the v1.24.0 reference page (which also has no time-bound kwarg). Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0.

### entry-092
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `AgentMemory` class at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\memory.py` (verified attributes `system_prompt: SystemPromptStep` and `steps: list[TaskStep | ActionStep | PlanningStep]`; the `reset()` method clears `self.steps` to `[]` while keeping the system prompt; the docstring states "Reset the agent's memory, clearing all steps and keeping the system prompt."); live verification on 2026-08-01: two consecutive `agent.run("task 1")` and `agent.run("task 2")` calls leave `len(agent.memory.steps) == 3` after each call, confirming the default `reset=True` clears the previous run's steps before appending the new `TaskStep`; the v1.26.0 reference documents `RunResult.steps` and the `reset=` kwarg at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents; chub `smolagents/package` consulted first.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: smolagents has a single `AgentMemory` object on the agent (`agent.memory`) holding the system prompt and a `list[MemoryStep]`. Within one `.run()` call, the framework appends a `TaskStep` at the start, then interleaves `PlanningStep`s whenever `planning_interval` fires with one `ActionStep` per action iteration. A successful run ends with the final `ActionStep`, which carries the model's tool-call observation; no separate `FinalAnswerStep` is appended to `agent.memory.steps`. Across calls, default `reset=True` clears prior steps before the next `TaskStep`. `reset=False` deliberately preserves the earlier steps for a continued conversation.
paraphrase: Memory is a typed step list anchored to the system prompt. Each run begins with `TaskStep`, may interleave planning and action steps, and ends on the final `ActionStep`; there is no separate final-answer record in `agent.memory.steps`. Default `reset=True` starts the next call clean, while `reset=False` continues the same conversation.

### entry-093
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.run` signature at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (verified 2026-08-01: `(self, task: str, stream: bool = False, reset: bool = True, images: list['PIL.Image.Image'] | None = None, additional_args: dict | None = None, max_steps: int | None = None, return_full_result: bool | None = None) -> Any | RunResult`); a whole-package grep for `chat_messages`, `prior_messages`, `message_history`, and `history` returned no matches in `smolagents/agents.py`, `smolagents/memory.py`, or `smolagents/monitoring.py`; the v1.26.0 reference page lists the same signature at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents; the v1.24.0 reference page also lists the same signature, so the absence of `chat_messages` is stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: The brief asked me to verify the `chat_messages` parameter on `.run()`. It does not exist in smolagents==1.26.0 and did not exist in smolagents==1.24.0. The exact 1.26.0 signature has eight parameters: `task` (required), `stream` (default `False`), `reset` (default `True`), `images` (default `None`), `additional_args` (default `None`), `max_steps` (default `None`, falls back to the agent's own `max_steps`), `return_full_result` (default `None`, falls back to the agent's own `return_full_result`), and the implicit `self`. There is no `chat_messages`, no `prior_messages`, no `history`, and no equivalent. Multi-turn within a single Python process is done by calling `.run()` multiple times on the same `agent` instance and either (a) accepting the default `reset=True` to get a fresh conversation per call, or (b) passing `reset=False` so the new run appends to `agent.memory.steps` and the next model call sees the previous history rebuilt by `write_memory_to_messages()`. The `additional_args` parameter is the only direct way to inject fresh state into the prompt (it is rendered as "You have been provided with these additional arguments, that you can access directly using the keys as variables: {str(additional_args)}."). The `images` parameter is the only multimodal path; both are documented in the v1.26.0 reference page. The chapter's beginner recipe for "carry context across two questions" is therefore: build the agent once, run with `reset=False` for follow-ups, and read `agent.memory.steps` if you want to see what the model has in its head.
paraphrase: smolagents does not have a `chat_messages=` parameter on `.run()`. The "multi-turn" mechanism is the agent object itself: keep using the same `agent`, pass `reset=False` to continue the previous conversation, and inspect `agent.memory.steps` to see exactly what the model has been told. The chapter should not invent a `chat_messages` API; it should show the verified `reset=False` recipe and explain the per-call `additional_args` knob for the case where the user wants to push a fresh variable into the prompt without rebuilding the agent.
variance_resolution: Brief-correction: the brief asked me to "document the exact signature and how to pass prior history" via `chat_messages`. Verified behavior: there is no such parameter. The 1.26.0 installed source, the v1.26.0 reference page, and the v1.24.0 reference page all agree. This is a correction to the brief, not a contradiction between two primary sources.

### entry-094
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.to_dict` and `MultiStepAgent.from_dict` source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (verified 2026-08-01: `to_dict()` returns a dict whose keys are `class`, `tools` (each tool's Python source regenerated as a `Tool` subclass string), `model` (a `{"class": ..., "data": ...}` envelope limited to the registered `MODEL_REGISTRY` classes), `managed_agents`, `prompt_templates`, `max_steps`, `verbosity_level`, `planning_interval`, `name`, `description`, `requirements`, plus the `CodeAgent`/`ToolCallingAgent`-specific `executor_type`/`executor_kwargs`/`additional_authorized_imports`/`max_print_outputs_length`/`code_block_tags`; the dict does NOT include `memory`, `state`, `task`, `instructions`, or `system_prompt` — confirmed on 2026-08-01 by constructing a `CodeAgent(tools=[hello], model=Stub(), max_steps=1, instructions="be brief")` and inspecting `to_dict()`; `MultiStepAgent.save(output_dir, relative_path=None)` writes an `agent.json` plus regenerated tool sources plus a `prompt.yaml` plus a Gradio `app.py` plus a `requirements.txt`; the package has no `__reduce__`, no `__getstate__`, and no `pickle` support on `MultiStepAgent` or its subclasses; the v1.26.0 reference page documents `save`/`from_dict`/`to_dict`/`push_to_hub`/`from_hub`/`from_folder` at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: smolagents 1.26.0 does NOT ship a built-in way to serialize and reload an agent's runtime state (memory, current task, current `state` dict, last `FinalAnswerStep`). The framework provides `to_dict()`/`from_dict()`/`save()` for shipping an agent *template* — the agent's class, its tool source code, its prompt templates, its `max_steps`, its `planning_interval`, etc. — to a Hugging Face Space or a folder on disk, but the resulting `agent.json` does not contain the `memory` list, the `state` dict, the active `task`, the `instructions` value (verified on 2026-08-01), or the last `RunResult`. Reconstructing an in-progress conversation from a saved template requires a separate mechanism. For beginners, the chapter's recommendation is therefore the simplest one: persist the *task input* (the user prompt) and the *final answer* to disk — `json.dumps({"task": task, "answer": str(answer)}, indent=2)` is enough — and rebuild the agent from its template on the next run. The `to_dict`/`save` machinery is for shipping the agent to a Space, not for cross-process memory; ch-10 should mention the limitation in one sentence, point the reader at the persistence recipe above, and explicitly NOT teach `pickle` on a live `MultiStepAgent` (it works in principle because the class has no `__slots__`-based exclusion, but the live `state` dict holds references to the model's API client and the executor's open resources, which is exactly the kind of thing that should not be unpickled into a different process).
paraphrase: Do not invent a custom pickle story. The agent's "memory" lives in two places: the conversation history in `agent.memory.steps` and the per-run state in `agent.state`. Both are wiped when the Python process ends. If the chapter needs to remember a task, write the task text and the final answer to a JSON file; if the chapter needs to ship the agent itself to another machine, use `agent.save()` (which writes an `agent.json` plus tool sources plus a `prompt.yaml`) and let `CodeAgent.from_dict()` rebuild it on the other end. The state and the memory are not part of that round-trip, and that is by design.
variance_resolution: The `to_dict()`/`from_dict()` API has been stable since smolagents 1.0; the v1.24.0 reference page documents the same set of keys. The `messages`/`steps` field rename happened in 1.22.0 and the deprecated alias is removed in 1.25.0 — verified against 1.26.0 installed source (the deprecated property still raises `FutureWarning` rather than `DeprecationWarning`, which is consistent with the 1.25.0 removal plan being staged). Age-risk: if a future release adds a `state`/`memory` field to `to_dict()`, this entry should be revisited; no such field exists on 2026-08-01.

### entry-095
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\memory.py` (inspected 2026-08-01); ch-08 entry-061 (`HfApiModel ↙ ApiModel` rename), ch-08 entry-070 (one-time "Naming note" sidebar), ch-09 entry-077 (no auto-coercion at tool returns); cross-checked against the v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents.
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: Four beginner errors the chapter should call out explicitly. (1) Keep `instructions` to one short paragraph so it does not dilute the tool menu. (2) Use `prompt_templates={"system_prompt": ...}` only when replacing the full prompt is deliberate; direct `system_prompt=` is not a constructor kwarg and raises `TypeError`. (3) Default `reset=True` clears previous run steps, so use `reset=False` deliberately for continuity. (4) `max_steps` budgets action iterations, not planning calls. Planning adds model calls, token cost, and latency alongside that budget; with `planning_interval=4` and `max_steps=20`, a run can include up to 20 action steps and up to 5 planning steps.
paraphrase: Keep `instructions` short, do not pass direct `system_prompt=`, use `reset=False` only for intentional continuity, and remember that planning calls add cost without consuming `max_steps`.

### entry-096
source: am-research synthesis from the installed smolagents==1.26.0 source and v1.26.0 reference page (inspected 2026-08-01), cross-referenced with the ch-10 brief's three forward-pointers (ch-11 Structured Workflows, ch-12 Observe/Debug/Evaluate, ch-13 Test Agents).
used_in: ch-10
claim/finding: Three forward-pointers the chapter's final paragraph should name in one sentence each. ch-11 (Structured Workflows) will return to the `instructions` and `planning_interval` knobs to show hand-shaped multi-agent workflows — a manager `CodeAgent` with a few `ToolCallingAgent` managed agents, where `instructions` is the per-worker policy and `planning_interval` is the per-worker re-plan cadence; the v1.26.0 reference documents `managed_agents` and the `__call__`-driven report flow (entry-089). ch-12 (Observe/Debug/Evaluate) will use `agent.memory.steps` (entry-092), `RunResult.steps` (entry-090), and `agent.replay(detailed=True)` (documented on the v1.26.0 reference page) to walk the reader through what the model saw at each iteration; the chapter also notes the `write_memory_to_messages()` method as the bridge from `MemoryStep` records to the `list[ChatMessage]` the model consumes. ch-13 (Test Agents) will assert on `RunResult` fields (entry-090) — primarily `output`, `state`, `steps`, and `token_usage` — to build a minimal pytest harness around an agent run; the chapter will reuse the `reset=False` lever (entry-092) for the "agent should remember the prior answer" test case and the `return_full_result=True` knob (entry-090) for the "agent should use fewer than N steps" test case. None of these later chapters require changes to the ch-10 API surface; they all reuse the kwarg surface verified in this dispatch.
paraphrase: The ch-10 reader is one chapter away from each of three follow-on chapters, and the chapter should signpost all three. ch-11 uses `instructions` and `planning_interval` to design multi-agent teams. ch-12 uses `agent.memory.steps` and `RunResult.steps` to show what the model was told at each iteration. ch-13 uses `RunResult` fields to write tests that fail loudly when the agent misbehaves. The ch-10 API surface is the foundation; the next three chapters build on it without breaking it.
