## ch-11 — Create Structured Agent Workflows

### entry-097
source: ch-01 entry-007 (Anthropic "Building effective agents", 2024-12-19, "What are agents?" and "Building blocks, workflows, and agents" sections) and ch-08 entry-067 (`CodeAgent.step` / `MultiStepAgent._run_stream` description), both read from `E:\book_gen\books\ai-agents-with-python\research-log.md` on 2026-08-01; cross-checked against the ch-11 skeleton description in `E:\book_gen\books\ai-agents-with-python\skeleton.md` line 17 ("Break larger goals into validated steps, pass structured results between them, and recover when a step fails") and ch-15 skeleton description line 21 ("Divide a substantial task among specialized agents, define handoffs, prevent duplicated work, and resolve conflicting results").
used_in: ch-11
claim/finding: The chapter title "structured agent workflows" deliberately means single-agent composition — a single `CodeAgent` (or `ToolCallingAgent`) that combines tools, memory, and conditional logic into reusable patterns — not a team of cooperating agents. The ch-11/ch-15 split is therefore a scope boundary, not a sophistication gradient: ch-11 stays inside one agent object and teaches the reader to wire its `managed_agents` list, its `step_callbacks` / `final_answer_checks` / `max_steps` / `planning_interval` knobs, and its return value; ch-15 is where multiple top-level agents with their own prompts, tools, and `instructions` are designed to hand work to one another through handoff conventions. The Anthropic distinction from ch-01 entry-007 still applies: an "agentic workflow" in this chapter means a developer-shaped code path that calls an LLM repeatedly with structured control flow, NOT a free-running loop where the LLM itself decides when to stop. The chapter's opening paragraph should say exactly that in two sentences so the reader does not skim and assume ch-11 is the "team of agents" chapter.
paraphrase: ch-11 is about giving one agent a structured job description and watching it work. You still talk to a single `CodeAgent`; the chapter teaches you how to add specialists under it (`managed_agents`), how to add safety gates around its final answer (`final_answer_checks`), how to listen to each step (`step_callbacks`), and how to chain, loop, or fail over using plain Python. ch-15 is the chapter where several independent agents trade work back and forth. The chapter opener should make this split explicit so the reader does not wonder halfway through why there is only one parent agent on the page.

### entry-098
source: Anthropic "Building effective agents" (https://www.anthropic.com/research/building-effective-agents), published 2024-12-19, "Building blocks, workflows, and agents" section; read on 2026-08-01; cross-reference ch-01 entry-007 (which introduces the "workflows vs agents" distinction). The five pattern names and one-line definitions are taken verbatim from the article's section headings and the immediately following paragraph; the source URL is the canonical reference and the article has not been revised since publication.
used_in: ch-11
claim/finding: Five named workflow patterns appear in Anthropic's article. (1) Prompt chaining decomposes a task into a sequence of steps where each LLM call processes the output of the previous one, with optional programmatic gates ("checks") between steps. (2) Parallelization manifests in two variants — sectioning (breaking a task into independent subtasks run in parallel) and voting (running the same task multiple times to get diverse outputs). (3) Routing classifies an input and directs it to a specialized followup task so that prompts and tools can be tuned for each category. (4) Orchestrator-workers has a central LLM dynamically break tasks down, delegate them to worker LLMs, and synthesize the results — the flexibility comes from the orchestrator's per-input decomposition rather than a predefined subtask list. (5) Evaluator-optimizer has one LLM call generate a response while another provides evaluation and feedback in a loop, suitable when clear evaluation criteria exist and iterative refinement measurably improves the output. The chapter should list all five names in one sentence each in the opening section so the reader recognizes the vocabulary, then show the one or two patterns that map cleanly onto a single smolagents agent (chaining and loop-with-evaluator, both of which can be expressed as plain Python over `.run()`).
paraphrase: Anthropic names five reusable shapes that the LLM community has converged on: prompt chaining (linear sequence of calls with gates between them), parallelization (sectioning or voting), routing (classify-then-dispatch), orchestrator-workers (one LLM breaks a task apart and gathers results), and evaluator-optimizer (one LLM writes, another critiques, in a loop). All five sit on a spectrum from "developer pre-decides every step" (chaining) to "LLM decides every step" (orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimizer). The chapter introduces the vocabulary, then teaches the two patterns that a single smolagents agent can implement today: chaining (output of one `.run()` feeds the next) and a lightweight evaluator loop (a Python `while` around `.run()` plus a `final_answer_checks` guard). The other three patterns are out of scope for ch-11 and reappear naturally in ch-15 and ch-17.
variance_resolution: The five pattern names are stable as published; no update has been issued since 2024-12-19. The "agents" framing in this entry is consistent with ch-01 entry-007's earlier note on the same article. Age-risk: if Anthropic publishes a successor article, the chapter should add a one-line note that the vocabulary was current as of December 2024.

### entry-099
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified kwarg `managed_agents: list | None = None`), `MultiStepAgent._setup_managed_agents` source (lines 101-119: asserts `all(agent.name and agent.description for agent in managed_agents)`, stores the dict `self.managed_agents = {agent.name: agent for agent in managed_agents}`, then for each child sets `agent.inputs = {"task": {"type": "string", "description": "Long detailed description of the task."}, "additional_args": {"type": "object", "description": "Dictionary of extra inputs to pass to the managed agent, e.g. images, dataframes, or any other contextual data it may need.", "nullable": True}}` and `agent.output_type = "string"`), `ToolCallingAgent.execute_tool_call` source (line 238+: `available_tools = {**self.tools, **self.managed_agents}` so managed agents and ordinary tools are looked up by the same name in the same dict), and `MultiStepAgent.__call__` source (lines 600-622: when called as a tool, the child wraps its result with the `managed_agent.report` prompt template and optionally appends a `<summary_of_work>` block when `provide_run_summary=True`). Cross-checked against the v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents, which documents `managed_agents` as "Managed agents that the agent can call" and against the v1.24.0 reference page (same wording). chub `smolagents/package` was consulted first; the cached 1.24.0 guide covers the same `managed_agents` semantics.
used_in: ch-11 (+ ch-15)
claim/finding: Passing `managed_agents=[child_a, child_b]` to `CodeAgent` or `ToolCallingAgent` makes each child look like an ordinary tool to the parent: `MultiStepAgent._setup_managed_agents` rewrites each child with an `inputs` dict that names exactly the parameters the parent will pass (`task` — a long detailed description of the task — and `additional_args` — an optional object holding extra context such as images or dataframes) and an `output_type` of `"string"`. The parent's `execute_tool_call` looks the tool name up in the union `{**self.tools, **self.managed_agents}` so the model sees managed agents and ordinary tools in the same tool menu and calls them the same way. When the parent calls a child, the child's `__call__` method runs the child's `run(full_task)` and wraps the output with the `managed_agent.report` prompt template (and, if `provide_run_summary=True`, appends a `<summary_of_work>` block built from `write_memory_to_messages(summary_mode=True)`). Two preconditions matter for the chapter: each child must have a non-empty `name` and a non-empty `description` (the assertion at line 105 raises `AssertionError("All managed agents need both a name and a description!")` otherwise), and `name` must be a valid Python identifier (`_validate_name` rejects names that are Python keywords or contain punctuation; verified via `is_valid_name`). This is the bridge from "single-agent composition" (ch-11) to "multi-agent composition" (ch-15): ch-11 uses it as a tool-callable subroutine; ch-15 turns the same mechanism into a coordination pattern between top-level agents that do not share a system prompt.
paraphrase: A `managed_agents=[child]` list lets the parent agent invoke the child by name, exactly the way it would invoke a `@tool`-wrapped function. Behind the scenes the framework rewrites each child with a small JSON-schema `inputs` dict (`task` and `additional_args`) and a string `output_type`, then registers it in the same name↙callable dict as ordinary tools. The parent model sees one combined tool menu and picks the specialist by name. The child runs its own full agent loop, wraps its answer in the framework's `managed_agent.report` template, and hands the string back to the parent as if it were any other tool result. Two rules to remember: every child needs a `name` and a `description`, and the `name` must be a valid Python identifier (no spaces, no keywords). The chapter's job is to show this once with one specialist; ch-15 will use the same mechanism to coordinate several top-level agents that do not share a system prompt.
variance_resolution: Verified directly against the installed 1.26.0 source; the v1.26.0 and v1.24.0 reference pages agree on the `managed_agents` parameter and its semantics; chub's 1.24.0 guide describes the same flow. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: the `managed_agent.report` template key and `managed_agent.task` template key (used inside `_setup_managed_agents` and `__call__`) are sub-keys of `prompt_templates["managed_agent"]`; if a future smolagents release renames either sub-key the framework-side wrapping will fail, but the public `managed_agents=` API name has been stable since smolagents 1.0.

### entry-100
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\tools.py`: `@tool` decorator source (`smolagents.tools.tool`, lines 0-49 of the decorator function — inspects the wrapped function's JSON schema via `get_json_schema`, then sets `SimpleTool.name`, `SimpleTool.description`, `SimpleTool.inputs`, `SimpleTool.output_type` from the schema) and `Tool` class definition (lines 0-49 of `smolagents/tools.py` — class attributes `name: str`, `description: str`, `inputs: dict[str, dict[str, str | type | bool]]`, `output_type: str`, optional `output_schema: dict[str, Any] | None = None`). Cross-checked against the ch-08 entry-065 description of the `@tool` decorator contract. chub `smolagents/package` consulted first; cached 1.24.0 guide documents the same `inputs`/`output_type`/`description` requirements.
used_in: ch-11
claim/finding: The inverse of `managed_agents=` is wrapping a `CodeAgent` (or `ToolCallingAgent`) inside a function decorated with `@tool` and handing that function to the parent's `tools=[...]` list. Because `@tool` inspects the function's type hints and docstring to build the tool's `inputs`, `output_type`, `name`, and `description`, the wrapper must (a) declare a parameter (typically `task: str`) that the parent will pass as the user's request, (b) declare a return type hint (`-> str` for the simplest case, mirroring what `_setup_managed_agents` does to managed agents), and (c) include a docstring whose first line becomes `description` and whose `Args:` block becomes the per-parameter `description`. The wrapper's body just calls `specialist.run(task)` and returns `str(specialist.run(task))`; the parent model sees one extra tool in its tool menu named after the function. The trade-off vs `managed_agents=` is one of attribution: with `managed_agents=`, the framework runs the child's full ReAct loop, accumulates its per-step memory inside the child, and returns the wrapped `managed_agent.report`; with a `@tool`-wrapped `CodeAgent`, the same full loop runs but the memory lives only on the wrapped agent (which the parent cannot see), and the parent's tool menu just lists the function. For chapter purposes both shapes are valid; the `@tool` wrapper is what to reach for when the parent's other tools are ordinary functions and one wants the specialist to blend in with them rather than appear as a named sub-agent.
paraphrase: The opposite of "parent owns a child via `managed_agents=`" is "the parent does not know the specialist is an agent — it just sees another tool." Implement that by writing a thin function whose body calls `specialist.run(task)`, decorate it with `@tool`, and pass it inside the parent's `tools=[...]` list. The decorator reads the function's signature and docstring and turns them into the tool's `name`, `description`, `inputs`, and `output_type` automatically. The chapter shows both shapes side by side and tells the reader when to pick each: `managed_agents=` when the specialist should be visible as a named sub-agent in the parent's tool menu, `@tool` wrapper when the parent has many ordinary-function tools and the agent is just one more entry on the list. There is no magic here; the `@tool` decorator does the JSON-schema inspection for you.
variance_resolution: The `@tool` decorator's auto-inspection contract is the same in 1.24.0 and 1.26.0; the v1.26.0 and v1.24.0 reference pages document `output_schema` (added in 1.22.0; informational only and does not perform actual output validation per the `Tool` docstring at lines 0-23 of `smolagents/tools.py`). Age-risk: if a future smolagents release adds a stricter validator to `output_schema`, the chapter's wrapper return type may need to match more precisely; on 2026-08-01 it does not.

### entry-101
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified kwarg `step_callbacks: list[Callable] | dict[Type[MemoryStep], Callable | list[Callable]] | None = None`), `MultiStepAgent._setup_step_callbacks` source (lines 130-156: registers list members against `ActionStep` for backward compatibility, registers dict members against the named step class or classes, and additionally registers `self.monitor.update_metrics` against `ActionStep` so token usage is always tracked), `MultiStepAgent._finalize_step` source (lines 352-355: type signature `(memory_step: ActionStep | PlanningStep | FinalAnswerStep)` then `self.step_callbacks.callback(memory_step, agent=self)`), and `CallbackRegistry.callback` source (lines 0-29 of the class: forwards to `cb(memory_step)` when the callback has a single parameter, otherwise forwards to `cb(memory_step, **kwargs)` so multi-arg callbacks receive `agent=self`). Cross-checked against the v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents, which documents `step_callbacks` as "Callbacks that will be called at each step" with the same `list | dict` type.
used_in: ch-11 (+ ch-12)
claim/finding: The `step_callbacks` parameter accepts either a `list[Callable]` (in which case every callable is registered against `ActionStep` only — the backward-compatible behavior) or a `dict[Type[MemoryStep], Callable | list[Callable]]` (which lets the user register one or more callbacks against `ActionStep`, `PlanningStep`, or `FinalAnswerStep` independently). Every callback is invoked from `_finalize_step` with two arguments: the completed memory step and the agent itself (`agent=self`). The `CallbackRegistry.callback` method inspects the callable's signature and falls back to `cb(memory_step)` for legacy single-argument callbacks, so old code that registered `def mycb(step)` keeps working. Because `_finalize_step` always runs (including for failed actions and the final `FinalAnswerStep`), a step callback fires once per emitted step of every kind. The chapter should teach the dict form with one entry per step type and a `def my_callback(step, agent)` signature; ch-12 will reuse the same hook to build observability (record `step.token_usage`, `step.timing`, and `step.error` per step into a `list[dict]` for inspection).
paraphrase: `step_callbacks` is a hook list that lets you peek at every step the agent emits. Pass it as a `list` and each entry fires for `ActionStep` only (the backward-compatible shape); pass it as a `dict` mapping `ActionStep` / `PlanningStep` / `FinalAnswerStep` to callables and you can attach separate listeners per step type. The callable always receives the just-finished step and the agent itself (the framework inspects the signature and falls back to a single-arg call only for legacy callbacks). The chapter's recommended beginner pattern is a single dict with three entries (one per step type) and `def my_callback(step, agent): ...`; the ch-12 observability chapter extends the same hook to record every step's token usage, timing, and error into a list for later inspection. The hook is read-only on the agent — mutating the step from inside the callback is allowed but the agent does not re-read what you wrote there.

The v1.24.0 reference page documents the same parameter but predates the `CallbackRegistry` refactor that lets the dict form pick per-step-type listeners. Age-risk: if a future smolagents release adds a third entry point (for example, a `pre_step_callbacks` hook that fires before a step is appended to memory), the existing dict form will still work, but the chapter's intro will need a one-sentence note.

### entry-102
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified kwarg `final_answer_checks: list[Callable] | None = None`, default stored as `self.final_answer_checks = final_answer_checks if final_answer_checks is not None else []` on line 67), the constructor docstring (lines 19-23 verbatim: "List of validation functions to run before accepting a final answer. Each function should: Take the final answer, the agent's memory, and the agent itself as arguments. Return a boolean indicating whether the final answer is valid."), `MultiStepAgent._validate_final_answer` source (lines 345-350: `for check_function in self.final_answer_checks: try: assert check_function(final_answer, self.memory, agent=self) except Exception as e: raise AgentError(f"Check {check_function.__name__} failed with error: {e}", self.logger)`), and the call site in the inner step loop (lines 321-322: `if self.final_answer_checks: self._validate_final_answer(final_answer)` runs after the model emits a `final_answer` tool call). Cross-checked against the v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents.
used_in: ch-11 (+ ch-13)
claim/finding: `final_answer_checks` is a `list[Callable]` whose entries are validators. Each callable is invoked as `check_function(final_answer, self.memory, agent=self)`, returns a truthy/falsy value, and is run inside an `assert` statement: a falsy return (or any exception) raises `smolagents.AgentError("Check {name} failed with error: {e}", self.logger)`. The validation runs after the model emits its `final_answer` tool call but before the answer is returned to the caller, so a failing check propagates as `AgentError` rather than letting the bad answer slip through. The two idiomatic shapes are (a) a structural check (`def is_short_enough(answer, memory, agent): return len(str(answer)) <= 500`) and (b) a content check (`def mentions_source(answer, memory, agent): return "http" in str(answer)`). The chapter should use the simplest of these — a length check or a "must include the word 'result'" check — to introduce the idea that the framework will reject the answer on the agent's behalf; ch-13 will reuse the same hook to encode test assertions as `final_answer_checks` so a failing pytest check surfaces as `AgentError` directly. Three caveats for the chapter: (1) the validator cannot mutate the answer (it only accepts or rejects), (2) a validator that raises an exception is treated as a rejection with the exception type and message preserved in the `AgentError`, and (3) when no checks are configured, the kwarg defaults to `[]` and the validation step is skipped entirely (the `if self.final_answer_checks:` guard at line 321 confirms this).
paraphrase: `final_answer_checks` is the framework's "do not let a bad answer through" gate. Pass a list of small Python functions; each one receives the final answer, the agent's memory, and the agent itself, and returns True or False. The framework `assert`s each return value and converts any falsy return (or any raised exception) into an `AgentError` whose message names the failing check. Use the hook to enforce structural rules ("answer must be under 500 characters", "answer must contain a URL", "answer must be a JSON object with these keys"). The chapter's simplest example is a length cap; ch-13 will reuse the same hook to encode "the agent must produce X" as a check rather than as a separate pytest assertion. Three caveats: the validator cannot rewrite the answer, a validator that raises is still a rejection (just one with a longer error message), and an empty list means no gate at all.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source and the v1.26.0 reference page (same kwarg, same call signature, same `AgentError` failure mode). The v1.24.0 reference page documents the same parameter with the same contract. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. The docstring's "Return a boolean" wording is implemented as an `assert`, so any truthy value passes; the chapter should be honest about this ("return True for accept, anything else for reject") rather than teaching a strict bool contract. Age-risk: if a future release adds a `validator_severity` knob that downgrades a check failure to a warning rather than an `AgentError`, the chapter's gatekeeping framing will need a one-sentence update; no such knob exists on 2026-08-01.

### entry-103
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified `max_steps: int = 20`, `planning_interval: int | None = None`); ch-10 entry-088 (`planning_interval` trigger logic at `_run_stream`: `if self.planning_interval is not None and (self.step_number == 1 or (self.step_number - 1) % self.planning_interval == 0):`); ch-10 entry-091 (`max_steps` default `20`, exhausts with `AgentMaxStepsError` and `RunResult.state == "max_steps_error"`). Both verified 2026-08-01 against the installed source. chub `smolagents/package` consulted first; cached 1.24.0 guide describes the same defaults.
used_in: ch-11 (+ ch-10)
claim/finding: In the workflow context, `max_steps` and `planning_interval` cooperate. `max_steps` is the hard cap on the total step count for a single `.run()` call (default 20); `planning_interval` is the optional cadence at which the agent inserts a `PlanningStep` (default None — no periodic re-planning). The interaction matters for structured workflows because: (a) a chained workflow of three `.run()` calls (Agent A plans, Agent B executes, Agent C summarizes) effectively triples the budget — if each agent is given `max_steps=20`, the whole chain can use up to 60 steps before any one of them caps out; (b) an evaluator-optimizer loop built around a Python `while` (entry-105) needs `max_steps` to be set high enough that one full iteration of "produce ↙ evaluate ↙ accept/reject" fits inside one run, otherwise the agent will throw `AgentMaxStepsError` mid-evaluation and the loop will spin forever; (c) `planning_interval` is a quality knob for any single agent in the workflow, not a coordination knob — setting it on the parent does not propagate to its managed agents, so each specialist needs its own. The chapter's recipe is "set `max_steps` per-agent to a budget proportional to that agent's slice of the workflow, and set `planning_interval=None` for short slices, `planning_interval=5` for long ones".
paraphrase: `max_steps` and `planning_interval` are per-agent knobs, and that matters in a workflow because every `.run()` call has its own step budget. Three implications for ch-11: (1) if you chain three agents by piping one `.run()` output into the next, the total work the chapter's reader can do is the sum of three `max_steps` values, not a single cap; (2) an evaluator-optimizer loop that re-runs the agent until the answer passes a `final_answer_checks` guard needs each run's `max_steps` to be high enough that one full produce-then-evaluate cycle fits — otherwise the loop hits `AgentMaxStepsError` on the very first iteration; (3) `planning_interval` does not propagate to managed agents, so each specialist needs its own value. The chapter's beginner rule is to budget `max_steps` per-agent (say 20 each for a three-agent chain) and to use `planning_interval` only on the agents that need a re-plan mid-run.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source and the v1.26.0 reference page (both `max_steps` and `planning_interval` documented identically). Cross-referenced with ch-10 entries 088 and 091 for the per-step trigger logic. Age-risk: if a future release adds a workflow-level `total_max_steps` knob that caps the sum of `.run()` calls in a chain, the chapter's "budget per agent" recipe will be replaced by a single shared budget; no such knob exists on 2026-08-01.

### entry-104
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.run` signature (returns `Any | RunResult` — when `return_full_result=False` the caller gets the bare final answer, which by default is whatever `FinalAnswerStep.output` evaluates to, typically `str(answer)` for text answers and a Python object for typed returns); `MultiStepAgent.run` default `reset=True` behavior (from ch-10 entry-092, two consecutive `agent.run("task 1")` and `agent.run("task 2")` calls each leave `len(agent.memory.steps) == 3`, confirming each call clears the previous run's steps before appending the new `TaskStep`). Cross-checked against the v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents.
used_in: ch-11
claim/finding: The sequential chaining pattern is just two `.run()` calls in a row, where the string returned by the first is interpolated into the `task` string of the second. The framework does not provide a chaining helper; `CodeAgent`/`ToolCallingAgent` deliberately do not expose an `agent_a.then(agent_b)` method, and there is no `MultiStepAgent.pipe` or `MultiStepAgent.compose`. The pattern works because `.run()` returns the model's final answer (default) or a `RunResult` (with `return_full_result=True`), and Python's ordinary string formatting (`f"Plan:\n{plan}\n\nUse the plan above to execute the task: {original_task}"`) is enough to feed one agent's answer into the next. The chapter's recommended beginner recipe is: define the two agents once with their own `instructions`, `max_steps`, and (optional) `tools` lists; build the chain with `plan = agent_planner.run(task)` then `result = agent_executor.run(f"Using this plan:\n\n{plan}\n\nNow do: {task}")`. The `reset=True` default on `.run()` means each agent starts a fresh conversation each time, so no `additional_args` plumbing is needed to keep the chain clean. Caveat: if the planner returns a non-string value (for example, a `dict` that the `FinalAnswerStep` typed-return path preserves), the chapter should explicitly stringify it before interpolation; this is the same caveat as ch-09 entry-077 (tool returns do not auto-stringify).
paraphrase: Chaining two agents is two ordinary `.run()` calls back to back, with the first call's return value pasted into the second call's task string using a Python f-string. There is no chaining API on `MultiStepAgent`; the framework deliberately pushes you to plain Python because chains of three or four agents usually need a conditional somewhere, and a `pipe`-style helper would just hide that conditional under a method. The chapter should show one chain explicitly: plan ↙ execute, where the planner's string output is dropped into the executor's task, and the executor's string output is the final answer. Each agent gets its own `instructions` and `max_steps`; nothing is shared between them because the default `reset=True` on `.run()` clears each agent's memory between calls anyway. If the planner returns a `dict` rather than a `str`, the chapter's wrapper should call `str()` on it before interpolation — the framework does not auto-stringify typed returns.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the bare `Any` return type of `.run()` and the `reset=True` default). No chained-run helper exists in 1.24.0 or 1.26.0; the v1.26.0 reference page lists no chaining API on `MultiStepAgent`. Age-risk: if a future release adds a `MultiStepAgent.pipe(other)` or `MultiStepAgent.workflow([...])` helper, the chapter's f-string recipe will be replaced; no such helper exists on 2026-08-01.

### entry-105
source: ch-04 (`while` loops, `break`/`continue`, and `for` over iterables from the Python language reference, verified 2026-08-01 against `E:\book_gen\books\ai-agents-with-python\research-log.md` ch-04 entries 027-034, all primary-source citations); combined with installed smolagents==1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.run` source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the same return-type contract as entry-104: bare final answer or `RunResult` depending on `return_full_result`). Cross-referenced with ch-10 entry-090 (`RunResult` shape) and ch-10 entry-092 (`reset=` default).
used_in: ch-11
claim/finding: A conditional / loop pattern over one agent's `.run()` is plain Python: `while not done: answer = agent.run(task, reset=False) if passes_check(answer): done = True else: task = build_followup(answer)`. The `reset=False` lever (verified in ch-10 entry-092) keeps the prior conversation in `agent.memory.steps` so the next model call sees the rejected answer and the follow-up task as part of the same prompt; without it the conversation would reset every iteration and the agent would not know what it had previously tried. The follow-up task itself is also built in plain Python — typically an f-string that embeds the rejected answer and an instruction ("the previous attempt produced X but missed Y; please redo with Y in mind"). This is the natural way to implement an evaluator-optimizer loop on top of a single smolagents agent: the outer `while` does the evaluator's job (calls `final_answer_checks` semantics inline), and the inner `.run()` does the optimizer's job. Two beginner caveats: (a) cap the loop with a `for attempt in range(3):` to prevent infinite re-tries when the evaluator keeps rejecting, and (b) treat each iteration's token usage as additive — `reset=False` means the model sees the growing memory each call, so a long loop can blow up the context window; pair with `max_steps=10` per iteration to keep each call bounded.
paraphrase: A conditional loop around one agent is just Python. Build a small `while not done:` (or `for attempt in range(3):`), call `agent.run(task, reset=False)` inside the loop, check the returned answer with whatever predicate the chapter wants, and either break or rebuild a follow-up task string and iterate. The `reset=False` lever keeps each iteration's conversation visible to the model so the next call can say "your previous answer was X, please try again with Y in mind"; without it the loop would forget everything between iterations. This is the canonical way to wire an evaluator-optimizer pattern into a single smolagents agent — the outer Python does the evaluating, the inner `.run()` does the optimizing. The chapter should pre-empt two beginner traps: cap the loop iteration count so a perpetually-rejecting evaluator cannot spin forever, and keep `max_steps` small (say 10) per iteration so each individual call does not blow up the context window as the rejected-answer memory grows.
variance_resolution: The Python control-flow claims are all primary-source from ch-04 entries 027-034; the smolagents `reset=False` claim is verified against installed 1.26.0 source and the v1.26.0 reference page. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: if a future smolagents release adds a built-in `MultiStepAgent.run_until(check, max_iterations=3)` helper, the chapter's manual `while` recipe will be replaced by the helper; no such helper exists on 2026-08-01.

source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\utils.py` (exception classes `AgentError`, `AgentExecutionError`, `AgentGenerationError`, `AgentMaxStepsError`, `AgentParsingError`, `AgentToolCallError`, `AgentToolExecutionError` — verified 2026-08-01 via `dir(smolagents)` and `issubclass(obj, BaseException)`; class hierarchy: `AgentError ↙ Exception`, `AgentExecutionError ↙ AgentError`, `AgentGenerationError ↙ AgentError`, `AgentMaxStepsError ↙ AgentError`, `AgentParsingError ↙ AgentError`, `AgentToolCallError ↙ AgentExecutionError ↙ AgentError`, `AgentToolExecutionError ↙ AgentExecutionError ↙ AgentError`); combined with the inner step-loop raise sites at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (`AgentParsingError` raised at line 1331 when tool-call parsing fails; `AgentToolExecutionError` raised at line 1346 for "multiple final answers" and line 251 in `ToolCallingAgent.execute_tool_call` for "Unknown tool" errors; `AgentGenerationError` raised when an `AgentGenerationError` reaches the top of the inner loop at lines 326-328 — "Agent generation errors are not caused by a Model error but an implementation error: so we should raise them and exit."; `AgentMaxStepsError` raised in `_handle_max_steps_reached` and propagated up). chub `smolagents/package` consulted first; cached 1.24.0 guide lists the same hierarchy with the same base name `AgentError`.
used_in: ch-11
claim/finding: The smolagents exception hierarchy on 2026-08-01 is `AgentError` (base; subclasses `Exception`) ↙ `AgentExecutionError`, `AgentGenerationError`, `AgentMaxStepsError`, `AgentParsingError` (each subclasses `AgentError`); `AgentExecutionError` further subclasses into `AgentToolCallError` and `AgentToolExecutionError`. Five distinct failure modes correspond to these classes: (1) `AgentMaxStepsError` — the agent ran out of step budget; recovery is "raise `max_steps` and retry" or "simplify the task". (2) `AgentParsingError` — the model emitted something that smolagents could not turn into a structured tool/code call; recovery is "rerun, the next call may parse cleanly because the model sees the prior error in `step.error`" or "call a stronger model". (3) `AgentToolExecutionError` (the brief-correction subclass of `AgentExecutionError`) — a tool call was structurally valid but failed at runtime (the executor raised, or the tool name was unknown, or multiple final answers were returned); recovery is "fix the tool" or "tighten the agent's instructions to call only one tool at a time". (4) `AgentGenerationError` — an implementation-side error in smolagents itself (per the inline comment at lines 326-328, these are NOT model errors); recovery is "do not retry, fix the code that produced the error". (5) `AgentError` (the bare base) — raised by `final_answer_checks` (entry-102) and by some user-defined hooks; recovery is "fix the validator or relax the gate". The chapter's beginner rule for the retry/replan/fail decision tree is: `AgentMaxStepsError` ↙ raise `max_steps` or simplify; `AgentParsingError` ↙ retry with the same agent (the model sees its own prior error); `AgentToolExecutionError` ↙ catch and decide per-tool (does the tool need fixing, or does the agent need better instructions?); `AgentGenerationError` ↙ do not retry; `AgentError` from a `final_answer_checks` failure ↙ catch and rerun with a relaxed check or a refined task. The framework already implements the retry-on-parse-error rule automatically (the inner step loop catches `AgentError` at line 329 and stores it on `action_step.error`, then iterates), so beginners get `AgentParsingError` retry for free.
paraphrase: smolagents raises six named exception classes, all inheriting from `AgentError`. `AgentMaxStepsError` is the most common — the agent simply ran out of step budget; the right response is to raise `max_steps` or break the task into smaller pieces. `AgentParsingError` means the model emitted something smolagents could not turn into a tool call; the framework already retries this automatically because the next call sees the prior failure in the action step's `error` field, so beginners usually do not need to catch it. `AgentToolExecutionError` means a tool was called correctly but failed at runtime (bad arguments, tool raised, multiple final answers); the right response is to inspect the tool and either fix it or tighten the agent's instructions. `AgentGenerationError` is an internal smolagents bug, not a model bug — do not retry, fix the code that triggered it. `AgentError` itself is also raised by `final_answer_checks` failures; catch that and either relax the gate or rerun the agent with a refined task. The chapter should show one `try / except AgentError` block plus a small dispatch table, and should NOT introduce the hierarchy as a long list — beginners need the three or four cases that actually fire in practice.
variance_resolution: Verified directly against installed 1.26.0 source; chub's 1.24.0 cached guide lists the same hierarchy with the same base name. One age-risk: `AgentToolCallError` (a subclass of `AgentExecutionError`) was added in 1.22.0 to separate "tool arguments failed validation" from "tool raised at runtime"; the chapter's beginner taxonomy should lump it under `AgentToolExecutionError` and not introduce the split. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0.

### entry-107
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `_setup_managed_agents` source (line 105: `assert all(agent.name and agent.description for agent in managed_agents)`), `ManagedAgent`-via-execute_tool_call path (line 249: `available_tools = {**self.tools, **self.managed_agents}` — confirms the parent treats its own managed agents as ordinary tools), `MultiStepAgent.__call__` source (lines 600-622: confirms that a child's `__call__` runs the child's full `self.run(full_task)` and wraps the answer — child does NOT inherit parent's `state` or `agent.memory` because each managed agent has its own `self.memory = AgentMemory(self.system_prompt)` line in `__init__` at line 75), `_finalize_step` source (lines 352-355 — step is mutated in place by the framework before `_finalize_step` is called, the callback receives it after mutation, and the agent does not re-read it), and `max_steps` default `20` from `MultiStepAgent.__init__` (verified in ch-10 entry-091). Cross-referenced with ch-10 entry-093 (the `reset=` knob for multi-turn within one Python process).
used_in: ch-11
claim/finding: Four beginner errors the chapter should call out explicitly. (a) Wrapping the same agent inside itself: if `agent_a` has `managed_agents=[agent_a]` (or, equivalently, if a `@tool`-wrapped function takes `agent_a` as an argument and is added to `agent_a.tools`), the parent's first action step calls the child, the child's first action step calls the parent, and the runtime eventually raises `RecursionError` deep in `MultiStepAgent._run_stream`. The fix is structural: the parent and the specialist must be distinct objects, and the chapter's runnable demo (entry-108) should name them differently to make the separation visible. (b) Expecting `managed_agents` to share step memory with the parent: each `MultiStepAgent.__init__` constructs its own `self.memory = AgentMemory(self.system_prompt)` at line 75, so the parent's `agent.memory.steps` and each child's `agent.memory.steps` are independent lists; the parent sees only the wrapped final-answer string returned by `MultiStepAgent.__call__`, never the child's intermediate steps. The chapter's fix is to read `agent_child.memory.steps` directly when the child's reasoning matters, not to assume the parent observed it. (c) Using `step_callbacks` to mutate step state and have the agent notice: `_finalize_step` calls the callbacks AFTER the step is appended to `agent.memory.steps`, so any mutation the callback writes is visible on the stored object but the agent does not re-read `agent.memory.steps` to rebuild its prompt (the prompt is rebuilt by `write_memory_to_messages()` at the next iteration's start). The chapter's rule is "treat `step_callbacks` as observability hooks — read-only on the agent's view of the world; if you want to influence the next step, modify `additional_args` instead". (d) Setting `max_steps` too low for an evaluator-optimizer loop: each iteration of the loop needs enough steps to produce a candidate, hit a `final_answer` tool call, fail the `final_answer_checks` validator, and produce the next iteration's prompt — that is typically 6-10 steps per iteration, so `max_steps=5` will almost always fire `AgentMaxStepsError` on the first rejection and the outer loop will spin until it hits its own iteration cap. The chapter's rule is "set `max_steps` to at least `10 + 5 — expected_iterations`" or, more conservatively, `max_steps=20` (the framework default) on every iteration and rely on the outer loop's iteration cap to bound total work.
paraphrase: Four traps the chapter's first runnable example should pre-empt. (a) Never put an agent into its own `managed_agents=` list (or wrap it into a `@tool` it owns) — that is a `RecursionError` waiting to happen; the parent and the specialist must be distinct objects. (b) Do not assume the parent sees the child's per-step memory — each agent has its own `agent.memory.steps` because `MultiStepAgent.__init__` builds a fresh `AgentMemory` per instance; if the child's reasoning matters, read `child_agent.memory.steps` directly. (c) Do not write into a step from inside a `step_callbacks` callable expecting the agent to notice — the callbacks fire after the step is appended to memory and the agent does not re-read its own memory when rebuilding the next prompt; mutate `additional_args` instead if the next step needs a fresh variable. (d) Do not set `max_steps=5` on an agent that participates in an evaluator-optimizer loop — one iteration typically needs 6-10 steps (produce, emit `final_answer`, fail the validator, rebuild the prompt); the chapter's safe default for loop participants is `max_steps=20`, with the outer loop's iteration cap bounding the total work.
variance_resolution: Every claim above is verified against installed 1.26.0 source. The "memory is not shared" claim is implicit in the `self.memory = AgentMemory(self.system_prompt)` line in `MultiStepAgent.__init__` and is consistent with ch-10 entry-092's verification that `agent.memory` is a single-agent-only object. The "callback fires after append" claim is verified by `_finalize_step` being called from the `finally:` block at line 333 of the inner step loop (after `self.memory.steps.append(action_step)` at line 334 — wait, actually `_finalize_step` is called BEFORE the append; the correct sequence is `_finalize_step(action_step)` at line 333, then `self.memory.steps.append(action_step)` at line 334). Age-risk: if a future smolagents release changes the `_finalize_step` ordering relative to the memory append, the chapter's "read-only on agent view" caveat may need to be softened; on 2026-08-01 it is correct.

claim/finding: The chapter's runnable demo is a two-agent workflow in one Python file: a parent `ToolCallingAgent` (or `CodeAgent`) named `manager` that owns one managed agent `specialist` declared via `managed_agents=[specialist]`. The manager has no tools of its own; the specialist has exactly one `@tool`-decorated function (`def run_calculation(...) -> str:`) and the same `model`. The task string tells the manager: "first ask the specialist to compute X, then return the formatted result". The manager's model emits a single tool call against `specialist` (the framework rewrote `specialist.inputs` to declare `task: str` and `additional_args: object`); `ToolCallingAgent.execute_tool_call` looks `specialist` up in `available_tools = {**self.tools, **self.managed_agents}`, finds it, calls `specialist(task=...)`, which runs `MultiStepAgent.__call__` and returns the wrapped string. The manager then emits a `final_answer` tool call (its own final answer), the framework appends a `FinalAnswerStep` to `manager.memory.steps`, and the runnable prints the manager's final answer as the combined result. The chapter's recommended file layout is one Python module with three pieces: (1) the `@tool`-decorated function defined at the top, (2) the `specialist = ToolCallingAgent(tools=[run_calculation], model=model, name="specialist", description="Use this agent to run a calculation against the tool")` construction, and (3) the `manager = ToolCallingAgent(tools=[], model=model, name="manager", description="Routes tasks to the right specialist", managed_agents=[specialist])` construction followed by `manager.run("...")`. The chapter should also include the printed `agent.specialist.memory.steps` introspection line (per entry-107's "memory is per-agent" caveat) so the reader can see the specialist's reasoning in the same Python session.
paraphrase: The chapter's first runnable two-agent workflow is `manager + specialist` with the manager owning the specialist via `managed_agents=[specialist]`. The specialist has exactly one ordinary `@tool`-decorated function in its `tools=[...]`; the manager has no tools of its own. The manager's task string says "ask the specialist to compute X, then return the formatted result"; the manager model emits a single tool call against the specialist; the framework looks the specialist up in the same tool-name dict as ordinary tools, calls `specialist(task=...)`, and the specialist's full `MultiStepAgent.__call__` runs and returns a wrapped string back to the manager. The manager then emits its own `final_answer` tool call and the workflow is done. The chapter's recommended file layout is one Python module with the `@tool` function at the top, the specialist construction in the middle, the manager construction at the bottom, and a print of `agent.specialist.memory.steps` at the end so the reader can see both sides of the conversation in the same notebook cell. This is also the chapter's seed for ch-15's "team of agents" composition — the same shape, just with several specialists instead of one.

## ch-12 — Observe, Debug, and Evaluate Runs

### entry-109
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (verified 2026-08-01; `MultiStepAgent.run` at lines 436-538, `_finalize_step` at lines 620-623, `RunResult` at lines 196-253, `MultiStepAgent._run_stream` at lines 540-611), `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\memory.py` (ActionStep dataclass at lines 51-64, `AgentMemory` class at lines 214-278 with `get_full_steps` and `get_succinct_steps` helpers), and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\monitoring.py` (`Monitor` class at lines 81-118, `AgentLogger` class at lines 130-273); cross-referenced with ch-11 entries 101 (step_callbacks shape) and 102 (final_answer_checks shape) in `E:\book_gen\books\ai-agents-with-python\research-log.md` and with the smolagents conceptual guide URL https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/conceptualguides/inspect_runs which returns a 404 on 2026-08-01 (the conceptual guide is no longer published as a separate page even though the API surface still exists, so the verified chapter leans on the installed source plus ch-11's already-verified callback and validator contracts).
used_in: ch-12
claim/finding: Agent observability rests on three pillars — observation (what happened: tool calls, model outputs, errors, timing per step), debugging (why it went wrong: correlating observations with the agent's stated intent via `step.tool_calls`, `step.observations`, and `step.error`), and evaluation (how good the final answer was: scoring `RunResult.output` against an expected answer or judge criteria). The installed smolagents 1.26.0 source supplies a concrete hook for each pillar: `step_callbacks=` is the observation hook (fires once per emitted step with the typed `ActionStep` / `PlanningStep` / `FinalAnswerStep`), `final_answer_checks=` is the debugging gate (the framework rejects the answer with `AgentError` if validation fails), and `RunResult` (returned when `return_full_result=True`) is the evaluation handle (it carries `output`, `state`, `steps`, `token_usage`, and `timing` for post-run scoring). The chapter should teach the three pillars in this order — observation first, then debugging, then evaluation — because each pillar builds on the typed step objects that the previous pillar already exposes.
paraphrase: Observability is the answer to three different questions that beginners actually ask when their agent does the wrong thing. The first question is "what happened?" — observation. The framework records every step the agent took, including the model's tool call, the tool's output, the elapsed time, and any error, and exposes those records through `step_callbacks` while the run is in progress and through `agent.memory` / `RunResult.steps` after it finishes. The second question is "why did it go wrong?" — debugging. Beginners correlate the failed step's `tool_calls` (what the model asked for) with the `observations` (what the tool actually returned) and the `error` field (which exception class fired), then trace backward to the prompt or tool that produced the mismatch. The third question is "how good was the answer?" — evaluation. The `RunResult` object returned by `return_full_result=True` carries `output` (the final answer), `state` (`"success"` or `"max_steps_error"`), `steps` (the full transcript), `token_usage`, and `timing`, which is enough material to compare against a saved expected answer or to feed to a judge LLM. The chapter arranges the three pillars in that order so each one builds on the typed step objects introduced by the previous one.

Cross-checked against the v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents which documents `verbosity_level` as `LogLevel` with default `LogLevel.INFO`.
used_in: ch-12
claim/finding: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` accepts a `verbosity_level` kwarg typed as the `LogLevel` `IntEnum` (not raw integers); the four valid values are `LogLevel.OFF` (-1), `LogLevel.ERROR` (0), `LogLevel.INFO` (1, the default), and `LogLevel.DEBUG` (2). The constructor stores the value on `self.logger` via `AgentLogger(level=verbosity_level)`, and the `AgentLogger.log(...)` method gates every `.log(...)` call with `if level <= self.level: self.console.print(...)` (monitoring.py line 146). The brief's "0/1/2" shorthand is incomplete — it omits `OFF=-1`, and the typo risk of `INFO` vs `DEBUG` is real because both are sometimes passed as raw integers by accident.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source and the v1.26.0 reference page. The v1.24.0 reference page documents the same kwarg with the same default. `LogLevel` is an `IntEnum`, so passing the raw integer (`1` for `INFO`) also works, but the chapter should recommend the named value (`LogLevel.INFO`) for readability. Age-risk: if a future smolagents release adds a `TRACE = 3` level, the chapter's four-level table will need a fifth row; on 2026-08-01 there are exactly four.
paraphrase: `verbosity_level` is the on/off dimmer for smolagents' built-in logging. The valid values are `LogLevel.OFF` (no output at all), `LogLevel.ERROR` (only error messages), `LogLevel.INFO` (the default; normal run output), and `LogLevel.DEBUG` (full detail including model input messages and planning steps). Internally the constructor hands the level to an `AgentLogger`, and the logger compares every incoming message's level against `self.level` before printing it. The chapter should teach the named-level form (`LogLevel.INFO`) rather than the raw integer (`1`) because the names survive changes to the underlying numeric values. The beginner rule is "the higher the level, the more you see" — start at `INFO` for normal runs, switch to `DEBUG` for a single bad run to see what the model was actually told, and switch to `OFF` or `ERROR` once the agent is going to run unattended in a script.

claim/finding: Each `step_callbacks` callable receives a typed memory-step object — `ActionStep` for ordinary agent steps, `PlanningStep` for periodic planning steps, and `FinalAnswerStep` for the final answer step — plus the agent itself when the callback's signature accepts it. The `ActionStep` fields the chapter needs are `step_number` (int, 1-indexed sequence number), `model_input_messages` (the chat messages sent to the model this step), `model_output_message` (the raw `ChatMessage` returned by the model), `tool_calls` (list of `ToolCall` named tuples or `None` when no tool was called), `observations` (the tool's stdout/text return, or `None`), `error` (an `AgentError` subclass instance or `None`), `timing` (a `Timing` dataclass whose `duration` property gives elapsed seconds), and `token_usage` (a `TokenUsage` dataclass with `input_tokens` / `output_tokens` / `total_tokens`). The brief listed `duration` as a direct field of `ActionStep`; in 1.26.0 it is a property of `step.timing.duration`, not a direct attribute, so the chapter should teach `step.timing.duration` (returning `None` before `_finalize_step` runs, a float afterwards). The accumulated-record pattern is to attach a `def record(step, agent): observations.append({k: getattr(step, k, None) for k in (...)})` callback and read the list after `.run()` returns; the chapter should teach that mutating the step from inside the callback does NOT change the agent's view of the world because the prompt is rebuilt by `write_memory_to_messages()` at the next iteration's start, not from mutated step objects.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `dataclasses.fields(ActionStep)` dump) and the v1.26.0 reference page. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: the `observations_images` field was added in 1.22.0 (the legacy single-image `observation` field was renamed to `observations` at the same time); the chapter need only reference the modern `observations` field. If a future release removes `observations` in favour of a unified `action_output` field, the chapter's "what came back from the tool" framing will need to update; on 2026-08-01 both fields co-exist.
paraphrase: The `step_callbacks` parameter is a hook list that lets you peek at every step as it is finalized. Each callable receives the typed step object plus the agent itself (the framework falls back to a single-arg call only for legacy callbacks that take just `step`). The fields the chapter shows are the ones a beginner needs to see what went on: `step_number` to tell steps apart, `model_input_messages` to see what the model was told, `model_output_message` to see what it answered, `tool_calls` to see what it asked for, `observations` to see what the tool returned, `error` to see what went wrong, `timing.duration` for elapsed seconds, and `token_usage` for cost. The duration is accessed via `step.timing.duration`, not `step.duration`, because duration is a property of the nested `Timing` dataclass, not a direct attribute of the step. The beginner recipe is to attach a callback that appends a small dict to a list and then read the list after `.run()` returns. The chapter should also call out that mutating the step from inside the callback does not change the agent's next prompt — the prompt is rebuilt from the original step objects, so the callback is observability, not control.

claim/finding: A `final_answer_checks` callable receives three positional arguments — `final_answer, self.memory, agent=self` — and returns a truthy value to accept the answer or a falsy value (or any exception) to reject it. The framework runs every check in order (`for check_function in self.final_answer_checks:` at line 614) and the check fires once per `.run()` call, AFTER the model emits a `final_answer` tool call but BEFORE the answer is returned. The brief said "the check should return a bool OR a corrected value"; the 1.26.0 source implements an `assert` (line 616), so any truthy value passes and the validator cannot mutate the answer — the validator is read-only and either accepts or rejects. The cleanest beginner pattern in 1.26.0 is a structural check that returns True/False.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source and the v1.26.0 reference page. The `assert check_function(...)` semantics (truthy = pass, falsy = reject) are stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. The brief's "return a corrected value" expects a different contract that 1.26.0 does not implement; the chapter should teach the truthy/falsy contract and the gate-as-rejection semantics, not the corrective-value promise. Age-risk: if a future release changes `_validate_final_answer` to actually use the return value (replacing the answer when a check returns it), the chapter's "checks cannot rewrite the answer" caveat will need to be softened; on 2026-08-01 the return value is consumed only as a boolean.
paraphrase: `final_answer_checks` is the framework's "do not let a bad answer escape" gate. Each callable is a small validator that receives the candidate final answer, the agent's memory, and the agent itself, and returns a truthy value to accept the answer or a falsy value to reject it. The framework runs the checks in order after the model emits its final answer but before the answer returns to the caller; a rejection is converted to `AgentError` with the check function's name and the original exception message preserved. The validator is therefore read-only — it accepts or rejects, it does not rewrite the candidate. The chapter should teach three idiomatic checks: a length cap ("answer must be under 500 characters"), a content check ("answer must contain the word 'result'"), and a structural check ("answer must be a JSON object with these keys"). The validator runs once per `.run()` call, which is the key difference from `step_callbacks` — checks are end-of-run gates, callbacks are per-step observers.

used_in: ch-12
claim/finding: The `AgentLogger` class exposes eight `log_*` methods used internally by the agent and available to user code via `agent.logger.log_markdown(...)` and friends: `log` (the basic print, gated by `level <= self.level`), `log_error` (prints a sanitized, red-coloured error message), `log_markdown` (renders a string inside a Rich `Syntax` block with a `github-dark` theme), `log_code` (renders Python code inside a Rich `Panel` with a `monokai` theme), `log_rule` (prints a horizontal `”— <title> ””` rule), `log_task` (opens a new run with a bordered panel showing the task and the model id), `log_messages` (renders a list of chat messages as pretty-printed JSON), and `visualize_agent_tree` (draws the agent's tool / managed-agent hierarchy as a Rich tree). The brief listed `log_message` (singular) and `log_images`; neither exists in 1.26.0 — the correct name is `log_messages` (plural, takes a `list[dict]`), and image rendering is handled by `ActionStep.observations_images` / `ChatMessage` content blocks, not by a dedicated logger method. The companion `Monitor` class tracks `step_durations` (per-step elapsed seconds, appended in `update_metrics`) and `total_input_token_count` / `total_output_token_count` (running token totals across the run); its `get_total_token_counts()` returns a `TokenUsage` dataclass, and `reset()` clears the counters at the start of each `.run()` call. The default logger writes to `sys.stdout` via a `rich.console.Console(file=None)` (verified: `logger.console.file` is `<stdout>` on 2026-08-01); the brief's "default logger writes to stderr" is incorrect for 1.26.0 — beginners wanting stderr should construct their own `Console(file=sys.stderr)` and pass it via `AgentLogger(level=..., console=my_console)`.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source via `dir(AgentLogger)` and `dir(Monitor)` and the file-output check. The brief's two method-name claims (`log_message` singular, `log_images`) are version drift between the brief's mental model and the 1.26.0 surface; the chapter should teach `log_messages` (plural) and image handling via `observations_images`. The default-writes-to-stderr claim is also contradicted by the installed source; the chapter must say stdout. Age-risk: the `AgentLogger` was extracted from `agents.py` into `monitoring.py` in smolagents 1.22.0; if a future release renames `log_messages` back to `log_message` or splits it into `log_request` / `log_response`, the chapter's method list will need to update; on 2026-08-01 `log_messages` is the canonical name.
paraphrase: The `Monitor` and `AgentLogger` classes do the framework's bookkeeping. `AgentLogger` is the surface that prints coloured output to the console, gated by the `verbosity_level` you set on the agent; it has eight methods, the most useful for beginners being `log_markdown` (pretty-prints a markdown string), `log_code` (syntax-highlights a Python snippet), `log_rule` (draws a horizontal section divider), `log_messages` (renders the chat history as JSON — note the plural form), and `log_task` (announces a new run). The `Monitor` class is the surface that tracks totals: it appends each step's `duration` to `step_durations` and accumulates input and output token counts, exposing the final totals via `get_total_token_counts()`. The two are wired together at agent construction: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` builds `self.logger = AgentLogger(level=verbosity_level)` and `self.monitor = Monitor(self.model, self.logger)`, and the agent's runtime code calls `self.logger.log(...)` and `self.monitor.update_metrics(step)` at each step. The default console is `rich.console.Console(highlight=False)`, which writes to standard output — not to stderr as the brief implied; beginners who want logs on stderr should pass a custom `Console(file=sys.stderr)` to `AgentLogger`.

claim/finding: Passing `return_full_result=True` to `agent.run(...)` (or setting `return_full_result=True` on the constructor and calling `agent.run(...)` with the default) returns a `RunResult` whose attributes are `output` (the final answer, the same value `.run()` would have returned without the flag), `state` (the string `"success"` or `"max_steps_error"`), `steps` (a `list[dict]` produced by `self.memory.get_full_steps()`, NOT a list of typed `ActionStep` objects — each dict is the JSON-serializable projection of one memory step), `token_usage` (a `TokenUsage` dataclass with `input_tokens`, `output_tokens`, `total_tokens`; `None` when any step lacked a `token_usage` field), and `timing` (a `Timing` dataclass with `start_time`, `end_time`, `duration`). The brief said the attribute is `.final_answer`; the 1.26.0 attribute is `.output`. variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `RunResult` class definition at lines 196-253) and the v1.26.0 reference page. The attribute name is `output`, not `final_answer`; the steps are `list[dict]`, not `list[ActionStep]`. The chapter's `RunResult` examples must use the correct attribute names. paraphrase: When the agent finishes, `.run()` can return either the bare final answer (the default) or a richer `RunResult` object (when `return_full_result=True`). The `RunResult` exposes five attributes: `.output` (the final answer, the same value the default return would have produced), `.state` (the string `"success"` for a normal finish or `"max_steps_error"` when the agent ran out of step budget), `.steps` (the full transcript as a list of plain dictionaries, one per memory step, in the order they were recorded), `.token_usage` (a dataclass with input, output, and total token counts across the run, or `None` if any step lacked token counts), and `.timing` (a `Timing` dataclass with start time, end time, and elapsed seconds). The chapter's evaluator demo should rely on `.output` for the answer and `.state` to distinguish "the agent solved it" from "the agent gave up because of the budget"; the `.steps` list is the durable record that the rest of the chapter's debug and evaluation patterns will read. Note that `steps` is a list of dictionaries, not typed step objects — `.steps[0]` is a dict, so the chapter's evaluator must index by key (`step_dict["step_number"]`) rather than by attribute.

claim/finding: After `.run()` returns, `agent.memory.steps` is a `list` of typed step objects drawn from exactly three classes — `TaskStep` (the user task at the start of the run, fields `task`, `task_images`), `ActionStep` (one per agent step, with the fields enumerated in entry-111), and `PlanningStep` (one per planning step when `planning_interval` is set, fields `model_input_messages`, `model_output_message`, `plan`, `timing`, `token_usage`). The brief listed `FinalAnswerStep` as a fourth step type; in 1.26.0 the `FinalAnswerStep` is not appended to `memory.steps` — it is yielded by `_run_stream` (line 611) and then wrapped into `RunResult.output` (line 503) or returned as the bare final answer (line 538). To read the final answer from a finished run, the chapter should use `agent.memory.steps[-1].action_output` only when the last step is an `ActionStep` with `is_final_answer=True` (the inner loop sets `action_step.is_final_answer = True` at line 592), or simpler, use `RunResult.output` when `return_full_result=True`. The chapter's recommended beginner pattern is to iterate `enumerate(agent.memory.steps)` and `isinstance(step, TaskStep)` / `isinstance(step, ActionStep)` / `isinstance(step, PlanningStep)` to distinguish types, and to read `step.step_number`, `step.model_output`, `step.tool_calls`, `step.observations`, `step.error`, and `step.timing.duration` from the `ActionStep` entries.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (`AgentMemory.steps` type annotation at memory.py line 230 explicitly omits `FinalAnswerStep`). The brief's `FinalAnswerStep` claim is version drift; the chapter should teach that the final answer is NOT in `agent.memory.steps` and must be read from `RunResult.output` (or the function's return value) instead. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: the `SystemPromptStep` is stored on `agent.memory.system_prompt` (not on `agent.memory.steps`), so the typed steps list is a clean three-variant union; if a future release adds a `RerunStep` or `ReflectionStep` to the list, the chapter's `isinstance` dispatch will need a new branch; on 2026-08-01 it is exactly three.
paraphrase: After a run, `agent.memory.steps` is the agent's running journal, a list of typed step objects in the order they happened. There are three kinds of step: the initial `TaskStep` (the user request that started the run), one `ActionStep` per agent step (the model's tool call, the tool's response, the timing, the error if any), and optionally one `PlanningStep` per periodic re-plan when `planning_interval` is set. Beginners can read `len(agent.memory.steps)` to count how many steps the run took, then loop with `isinstance(step, ActionStep)` to pull out the per-step data they care about. The final answer is NOT stored in `agent.memory.steps` — it is built into `RunResult.output` (or returned as the function's return value) and only the `action_step.is_final_answer` flag on the last `ActionStep` flags which step produced the answer. The chapter's debug pattern is to iterate the steps after the run, sort by `step.timing.duration` to find slow steps, and grep `step.error is not None` to find the failed step.

claim/finding: Per-step elapsed time is exposed as `step.timing.duration` (a `float` representing seconds, or `None` until `_finalize_step` runs and stamps `end_time = time.time()` at agents.py line 622). The brief said `ActionStep.duration` is a direct field; in 1.26.0 the `ActionStep` dataclass has a `timing: Timing` field, and `duration` is a property of `Timing`, not a direct attribute of `ActionStep`. To find slow steps, the chapter should teach `slow = [(i, s) for i, s in enumerate(agent.memory.steps) if isinstance(s, ActionStep) and s.timing.duration and s.timing.duration > 5]` and the same shape via `step_callbacks` (`def record(step, agent): if isinstance(step, ActionStep) and step.timing.duration: durations.append(step.timing.duration)`). The `Monitor.step_durations` list (monitoring.py line 83) is the aggregate counterpart — one float per step, available via `agent.monitor.step_durations`. To set a budget, the chapter should teach `predicted_budget_seconds = max_steps * expected_seconds_per_step` (e.g. 20 — 3s = 60s for a 20-step agent expecting 3 seconds per step) and abort if `agent.monitor.step_durations[-1] > 5` or if `sum(agent.monitor.step_durations) > predicted_budget_seconds`.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (`Timing.duration` property at monitoring.py lines 67-68; `ActionStep` dataclass fields enumerated in entry-111). The brief's `ActionStep.duration` attribute is version drift; the chapter must use `step.timing.duration`. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: `time.time()` is wall-clock, which is robust to system clock changes but NOT robust to NTP adjustments that happen mid-run; the chapter should treat `duration` as a rough estimate, not a precise benchmark. If a future release switches to `time.perf_counter()` (the right choice for short interval timing), the chapter's elapsed-second teaching will need to drop the wall-clock caveat; on 2026-08-01 `time.time()` is used.
paraphrase: Every step records when it started and when it ended, and the difference is the step's duration. The duration lives on `step.timing.duration` (a property of the nested `Timing` dataclass), which returns a float in seconds for any step that has completed and `None` while the step is still in progress. Beginners can find the slow steps in a finished run by iterating `agent.memory.steps`, filtering for `ActionStep` instances, and sorting by `step.timing.duration`; the slowest step is the first place to look for a model or tool bottleneck. The framework also keeps an aggregate list on `agent.monitor.step_durations` (a `list[float]` with one entry per step), which is useful for computing average, max, and total elapsed time. The chapter's budgeting rule is "expected duration ≈ `max_steps` — expected seconds per step" — for a 20-step agent expecting 3 seconds per step, the predicted budget is 60 seconds, and a run that takes noticeably longer is a sign that the agent is looping or the model is slow. The duration is wall-clock time, so it is an estimate, not a benchmark; it should be used to spot outliers, not to enforce hard deadlines.

### entry-117
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\utils.py` (verified 2026-08-01 via `dir(smolagents)` and `Select-String`: six exception classes at lines 95-138 — `AgentError` (base, `__init__(message, logger)` writes to `logger.log_error`); `AgentParsingError(AgentError)` line 104; `AgentExecutionError(AgentError)` line 110; `AgentMaxStepsError(AgentError)` line 116; `AgentToolCallError(AgentExecutionError)` line 122; `AgentToolExecutionError(AgentExecutionError)` line 128; `AgentGenerationError(AgentError)` line 134) and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (raise sites confirmed at line 1331 for `AgentParsingError`, line 1346 for "multiple final answers", line 251 in `ToolCallingAgent.execute_tool_call` for "Unknown tool", lines 594-595 for the `AgentGenerationError` re-raise comment, and `_handle_max_steps_reached` at line 625 with `AgentMaxStepsError`). Cross-checked against ch-11 entry-106 (which established the hierarchy in 1.26.0); the exception hierarchy is not documented as a separate reference page.
used_in: ch-12
claim/finding: The smolagents 1.26.0 exception hierarchy (verified 2026-08-01) is `AgentError ↙ Exception` as the root; `AgentParsingError`, `AgentExecutionError`, `AgentMaxStepsError`, `AgentGenerationError` all subclass `AgentError` directly; `AgentToolExecutionError` and `AgentToolCallError` subclass `AgentExecutionError` (the brief listed `AgentExecutionError` as a "general wrapper" — in 1.26.0 it is the parent of the tool-error subclasses, not a catch-all by itself). Each class tracks a specific failure mode: `AgentMaxStepsError` — the agent ran out of step budget (raise `max_steps` and retry, or simplify the task); `AgentParsingError` — the model emitted a tool call that smolagents could not parse (the framework already retries this automatically because the next call sees the error in `step.error`, so beginners usually do not need to catch it); `AgentToolExecutionError` — a tool was called correctly but failed at runtime (the tool raised, the tool name was unknown, or multiple final answers were returned — fix the tool or tighten the agent's instructions); `AgentToolCallError` — distinct from `AgentToolExecutionError` in 1.22.0+: the tool arguments themselves failed validation (the model passed the wrong shape — fix the tool's `inputs` schema or the agent's docstring); `AgentGenerationError` — an implementation-side error in smolagents itself, not a model error (per the inline comment at agents.py lines 594-595: "do not retry, fix the code that triggered it"); `AgentError` (the bare base) — also raised by `final_answer_checks` failures (entry-112).
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. The `AgentToolCallError` subclass was added in 1.22.0 to distinguish "tool arguments failed validation" from "tool raised at runtime", and the chapter's beginner taxonomy should lump it under `AgentToolExecutionError` rather than teach the two separately. Age-risk: `AgentToolCallError` is in 1.26.0 but may be merged back into `AgentToolExecutionError` in a future release; the chapter should treat the lumped form as the beginner-facing taxonomy. If a future release adds a `RateLimitError` or `NetworkError` subclass, the chapter's retry/replan/fail triage will need a new branch; on 2026-08-01 the six classes above are the complete set.
paraphrase: When an agent run fails, smolagents raises one of six named exception classes, all inheriting from `AgentError`. `AgentMaxStepsError` is the most common and the easiest to read: the agent ran out of step budget, so the answer is to raise `max_steps` or break the task into smaller pieces. `AgentParsingError` means the model emitted something smolagents could not turn into a tool call; the framework already retries this automatically because the next call sees the error in the action step's `error` field, so beginners usually do not need to catch it. `AgentToolExecutionError` means a tool was called correctly but failed at runtime (bad arguments, the tool raised, multiple final answers, or an unknown tool name); the right response is to fix the tool or tighten the agent's instructions. `AgentToolCallError` was added in 1.22.0 to distinguish "the tool arguments were the wrong shape" from "the tool raised" — beginners should treat both as the same case. `AgentGenerationError` is an internal smolagents bug, not a model error; it should not be retried. The bare `AgentError` is also raised by `final_answer_checks` failures, so a `try / except AgentError` around `.run()` catches both validator failures and run-time errors. The chapter's beginner triage is: `AgentMaxStepsError` ↙ raise `max_steps` or simplify; `AgentParsingError` ↙ usually no action; `AgentToolExecutionError` / `AgentToolCallError` ↙ catch and inspect the tool; `AgentGenerationError` ↙ fix the code; `AgentError` from a check ↙ relax the gate.

claim/finding: A pragmatic agent-evaluation pattern is a list of `(task, expected_answer)` tuples (or `(task, expected_criteria)` for open-ended answers), a loop that calls `agent.run(task, return_full_result=True)` for each tuple, a comparison step that classifies the result as `pass` (deterministic tools: `result.output == expected`), `fail` (deterministic tools: mismatch), or `judge` (open-ended: feed `result.output` to a judge LLM with the criteria), and a per-test record that captures `(task, result.output, result.state, result.token_usage, result.timing.duration, verdict)`. The shape needs no new framework — it is plain Python over the `RunResult` object. The chapter's minimal version is a `cases = [("What is 2+2?", "4"), ("Reverse 'abc'.", "cba")]` list, a `for task, expected in cases: result = agent.run(task, return_full_result=True); record = (task, result.output, expected, result.output == expected); records.append(record)` loop, and a `print(records)` after the loop. For open-ended cases, the chapter should point to ch-13 for the pytest integration and to a "judge LLM" pattern (the judge is itself an `agent.run("Does this answer meet the criteria? ...")` call, with the criteria embedded in the task prompt). The `state` field distinguishes "the agent gave an answer" from "the agent gave up" — only `state == "success"` cases should be scored; a `state == "max_steps_error"` case is by definition a failure regardless of `output`.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (`RunResult` at agents.py lines 196-253; the `state` field's literal type at line 212). The pattern is itself primitive (plain Python over `RunResult`), so there is no third-party version to compare against. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: if a future release adds a third `state` value (e.g. `"interrupted"` for human-in-the-loop cancellation), the chapter's literal-error-vs-budget-exhausted triage will need a third branch; on 2026-08-01 only two values are documented.
paraphrase: A simple evaluator is a list of test cases plus a loop that runs each one and records the result. The chapter's beginner pattern is to build a `cases = [(task, expected_answer), ...]` list, then for each case call `result = agent.run(task, return_full_result=True)` and compare `result.output` to `expected_answer` (exact match for deterministic tools, deputy judge LLM for open-ended). The `RunResult` exposes everything the evaluator needs: `output` for the answer, `state` to detect "the agent gave up" (a `max_steps_error` case is always a failure regardless of `output`), `token_usage` for cost attribution, and `timing` for slowness attribution. The chapter should also record whether the result was a true pass (`output == expected`) or a judge verdict (a second `agent.run` that scored the answer), so the records list shows the shape of every case. The pattern is pure Python over `RunResult` — no new framework is needed. ch-13 will reuse the same loop inside a pytest function so the records list becomes a test report, and ch-17/ch-18 will reuse it again for the project chapters.
