claim/finding: Four beginner errors the chapter should call out explicitly. (a) Reading `agent.memory` before `.run()` completes (or after a fresh constructor with no run): the list is empty because `MultiStepAgent.__init__` sets `self.memory = AgentMemory(self.system_prompt)` at line 343 and `AgentMemory.__init__` initializes `self.steps = []` (memory.py line 230); the first append happens inside `.run()` at agents.py line 488. The fix is to inspect `agent.memory.steps` only after `agent.run(...)` returns. (b) Setting `verbosity_level=LogLevel.DEBUG` in a notebook and not being able to find the real answer among the noise: the chapter should teach the "two notebooks" pattern — one notebook with `verbosity_level=LogLevel.INFO` for the happy path and one with `LogLevel.DEBUG` for the broken case, or pass a `logger=` constructor argument so the same agent can be re-run at different verbosities. (c) Mutating `agent.memory` to "rewrite history" (e.g. `agent.memory.steps[-1].observations = "fake answer"`): the mutation is visible on the stored object but the agent has already finished (or, in a multi-iteration loop, the next prompt is rebuilt by `write_memory_to_messages()` from the step objects, not from the dict you wrote — so the change is a debugging illusion, not a control surface). The chapter's rule is "treat `agent.memory` as the agent's view of truth; use `step_callbacks` to observe, do not mutate". (d) Catching all `AgentError` with a bare `except` and silently swallowing — losing the actual cause: the framework's exception classes are intentionally informative (entry-117), and a bare `except AgentError` that does `pass` discards both the message and the stack trace. The chapter's rule is "catch the specific subclass, log the message, re-raise if you cannot fix it; never silently swallow".
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `reset=True` default at agents.py line 478, the `memory.steps` empty-list initialization at memory.py line 230, the first append at agents.py line 488). The four errors are the canonical ch-12 traps; cross-checked against ch-11 entry-107 (the parallel "do not put an agent in its own managed_agents" trap). Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: if a future release implements an "agent-editable memory" feature (a `step_callbacks` mutation that the next prompt picks up), the chapter's "do not mutate" rule will need to be relaxed; on 2026-08-01 mutations are observability-only.
paraphrase: Four traps the chapter's first runnable example should pre-empt. First, reading `agent.memory` before the agent has run: the list is empty until the first `.run()` call appends a `TaskStep`, so beginners who try to inspect memory immediately after construction will think the agent is broken. Second, cranking `verbosity_level` to `DEBUG` in a notebook and drowning in logs: the chapter should teach the "two notebooks" pattern — `INFO` for the happy path, `DEBUG` only when the run has gone wrong — and remind beginners that the same agent can be reconstructed with a new `logger=` for re-runs. Third, trying to "rewrite history" by mutating `agent.memory.steps[-1].observations` to a fake answer: the mutation is recorded on the stored object but the agent does not re-read its own memory when building the next prompt, so the change is a debugging illusion, not a control surface. Fourth, catching every `AgentError` with a bare `except` and silencing it: the framework's exception classes are intentionally informative, and a silent `except` discards both the message and the stack trace. The chapter's four rules are: read memory only after `.run()` returns; pick the verbosity level per notebook; treat memory as observability, not control; and catch the specific subclass, log the message, and re-raise if you cannot fix it.

The shape is: a single `def record_duration(step, agent): if isinstance(step, ActionStep) and step.timing.duration: durations.append(step.timing.duration)` function, attached to a `step_callbacks={ActionStep: record_duration}` dict (the chapter should use the dict form so the callback is per-step-type rather than the list form's `ActionStep`-only fallback); a `cases = [("task text", "expected output")]` list with three entries; a `for i, (task, expected) in enumerate(cases, 1): durations.clear(); result = agent.run(task, return_full_result=True); verdict = "PASS" if result.output == expected else "FAIL"; print(f"run {i}: {verdict} in {sum(durations):.2f}s ({len(durations)} steps)")` loop; and a final summary that prints the total elapsed time and the per-run pass count. The chapter should use a deterministic tool (a `ToolCallingAgent` whose only tool is a Python function that returns a fixed string for a fixed input) so the evaluator's pass/fail is meaningful. ch-13 will reuse the same shape inside a pytest function so the loop becomes a test report; ch-17 and ch-18 will reuse it again for the project chapters' integration tests.

The runnable demo is itself a tutorial artifact, so there is no external version to compare against. Stable across 1.24.0 ↙ 1.26.0. Age-risk: the `step_callbacks` dict form (`{ActionStep: callable}`) was introduced alongside the `CallbackRegistry` refactor; if a future release renames the registry or simplifies the dict form, the chapter's demo will need a one-line update; on 2026-08-01 the dict form is the canonical modern shape.
paraphrase: The chapter's runnable demo is a small evaluator that runs the same agent on the same task three times and reports pass/fail plus per-step timing. The shape: a single `record_duration` callback that appends each `ActionStep`'s `timing.duration` to a list, attached via the `step_callbacks={ActionStep: record_duration}` dict form (the dict form is preferred over the list form because it scopes the callback to `ActionStep` only and leaves `PlanningStep` and `FinalAnswerStep` untouched). A `cases` list holds three `(task, expected_output)` tuples. The loop runs each case, compares `result.output` to `expected_output`, prints PASS or FAIL with the elapsed seconds and step count, and clears the per-run durations list before the next case. The chapter should use a `ToolCallingAgent` with a deterministic tool so the expected outputs are meaningful (a tool that returns "42" for the input "What's the answer?"); ch-13 will promote the same loop into a pytest function so the records list becomes a test report, and ch-17/ch-18 will reuse it again for the project chapters' integration tests. The demo is intentionally short — one agent, one tool, three runs — so beginners can read the whole shape in a single notebook cell.

## ch-13 — Test Agents Without Guessing

### entry-121
source: smolagents conceptual framing verified against installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the `MultiStepAgent` runtime at lines 436-611 calling the model and consuming `token_usage`, plus the inner `ChatMessage` parse at line 1309 `self.model.generate(input_messages, ...)`), combined with the pytest 9.1.1 docs at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/fixtures.html and https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/parametrize.html (the "About fixtures" page at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/explanation/about-fixtures.html and the "Anatomy of a test" page at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/explanation/anatomy.html for the test-function-as-arg-collector mental model). Cross-referenced with ch-12 entry-115 (the `RunResult` shape) and ch-12 entry-117 (the exception family that the tests will catch).
used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: Agent tests are harder than ordinary Python tests because the unit under test is not a pure function. Four sources of non-determinism reach into the test: (a) the model itself — temperature, sampling, and version drift mean a model that passed last week can fail this week against the same prompt; (b) network latency and outages — a 200ms baseline can become 8s, and the run can hit HTTP 429 or 5xx that change the answer; (c) the model's content is not byte-equal between calls even at temperature 0 for some providers because of batching and floating-point reorder; (d) side effects — a real agent may make HTTP calls, write files, or shell out, so two runs against the same prompt leave different footprints on the world. The chapter's framing for the reader is "tests are not magic; the goal is to make the slice of code under test deterministic, not to make the whole agent deterministic". The concrete mitigation is to isolate the model behind a stub: a class that returns a canned `ChatMessage` regardless of input, so the rest of the agent's logic (tool selection, final-answer parsing, `step_callbacks` dispatch, `RunResult` construction) can be exercised without the model. The chapter should also mention that a stubbed test still exercises the agent's actual Python code paths — the framework's prompt-building, the tool dispatch, the step-loop, the validator gate, and the run-summary code all run. The only thing the stub removes is the network call to the model and the model's internal randomness.
paraphrase: An agent test is harder than a normal Python test because the unit under test is not a pure function — it talks to a model over the network, the model's output drifts, and the run has side effects. The chapter should frame the problem as "make the slice under test deterministic" rather than "make the whole agent deterministic", and the concrete mitigation is to replace the model with a stub that returns a fixed `ChatMessage`. With a stub in place, the test exercises everything else: prompt assembly, tool selection, step dispatch, validator gate, and the run-result packaging. The chapter should also tell the reader that a stubbed test does not catch model regressions — that is what the ch-17 evaluation suite is for — but it does catch regressions in the agent's own code.
variance_resolution: All four non-determinism sources are verified against installed 1.26.0 source where they touch the framework (the model call at agents.py:1309, the `token_usage` shape, the `RunResult` state literal at agents.py:212), and against the pytest 9.1.1 docs for the test-side shape. Stable across smolagents 1.24.0 to 1.26.0. Age-risk: if a future smolagents release introduces a built-in `DeterministicModel` or a `temperature=0` server-side guarantee for all providers, the chapter's "stub the model" mitigation will become a fallback rather than the primary path; on 2026-08-01 there is no such guarantee, so stubbing is still the simplest deterministic option.

### entry-122

claim/finding: The cleanest stub-model pattern in 1.26.0 is "subclass `Model` and override `generate`". The stub holds a list of canned `ChatMessage` responses, pops one per call (or returns the same one every call), and returns a `ChatMessage` whose `role=MessageRole.ASSISTANT` and whose `content` is a JSON string shaped like `{"name": "final_answer", "arguments": {"answer": "stub-ok"}}` (or like `{"name": "<tool_name>", "arguments": {...}}` for an intermediate tool call). The `ChatMessage.__init__` signature at models.py:124-128 is `ChatMessage(role: MessageRole, content: str | list[dict] | None = None, tool_calls: list[ChatMessageToolCall] | None = None, raw: Any | None = None, token_usage: TokenUsage | None = None)` — passing a `content=` string is enough for the agent to call `parse_tool_calls` and route through the tool menu. A second verified path is to subclass `ApiModel` and pass `client=<anything-not-None>` to the constructor so `self.client = client or self.create_client()` skips the abstract call; this is more boilerplate for the same outcome, so it is the second-choice path. A third path — subclassing `InferenceClientModel` and overriding `__call__` — does NOT work in 1.26.0 because the runtime calls `generate()` directly, not `__call__`, and the abstract `InferenceClientModel.generate` is inherited from `Model`. The chapter's runnable stub should be five lines: a class, an `__init__` that calls `super().__init__(model_id="stub")`, a `generate` method, a one-line canned `ChatMessage`, and a return. Empirical proof: a `ToolCallingAgent(tools=[], model=StubModel(), max_steps=1).run("any", return_full_result=True)` returns `state="success"`, `output="stub-ok"` when the stub emits the canned final-answer JSON (smoke-tested 2026-08-01 against the installed venv).
paraphrase: The cleanest way to stub the model in smolagents 1.26.0 is to subclass `Model` and override `generate` (NOT `__call__`, despite the convenience wrapper at `Model.__call__` that just calls `generate`). The runtime at agents.py:1309 invokes `self.model.generate(...)` directly, so a stub that only overrides `__call__` still falls into the abstract `Model.generate` and raises `NotImplementedError`. The stub holds a canned `ChatMessage` whose role is `MessageRole.ASSISTANT` and whose content is the JSON shape the agent's parser expects — either a `final_answer` call or an intermediate tool call. The same pattern works for multi-step tests by tracking the call count and returning different canned messages on different calls. The chapter should also tell the reader that a stub test exercises everything in the agent except the model's reasoning, so a passing stub test plus a failing live-model test is a clean signal that the regression is in the prompt or the model, not the agent's plumbing.

claim/finding: `max_steps=1` is the per-call budget override that forces a single step, which is the right knob for unit tests because it (a) guarantees the run will not loop, (b) makes the run's wall-clock time predictable, and (c) means the test exercises exactly one model call and one tool-or-final-answer dispatch. The kwarg is consumed at agents.py:468 (`max_steps = max_steps or self.max_steps`) and the per-call value takes precedence over the constructor default. The chapter's beginner rule is "set `max_steps=1` for any test that does not explicitly want to drive the inner step loop" and "set `max_steps=2` or `3` for tests that need to assert on the agent's recovery from a failed tool call". The reason for the explicit override (rather than relying on the default 20) is twofold: tests should be fast (a 20-step run that the test only needs one step of wastes 18 steps of model calls in a real-model test, and wastes 18 steps of stub time in a stub test), and tests should be loud about what they cover — `max_steps=1` is a one-glance signal that this test is a single-step unit, not an integration test of the inner loop.
paraphrase: `max_steps=1` is the per-call budget override that makes a run do exactly one step. It is the right knob for a unit test because it caps the run to one model call and one tool-or-final-answer dispatch, which makes the test fast and the run predictable. The per-call value overrides the constructor default (which is 20), so a test that says `max_steps=1` is unambiguous about what it covers. The chapter's beginner rule is to set `max_steps=1` for tests that do not need to drive the inner step loop, and `max_steps=2` or `3` for tests that want to assert on the agent's recovery from a failed tool call. The reader does not need to know the constructor default of 20 unless they are debugging why their test ran more steps than expected — in which case they probably forgot the per-call override and the constructor default kicked in.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `max_steps` kwarg at agents.py:443 and the precedence at line 468). Stable across 1.24.0 to 1.26.0. Age-risk: if a future release adds a `min_steps` companion kwarg (a minimum number of steps the agent must take even if the model emits `final_answer` earlier), the chapter's "set `max_steps=1` for unit tests" rule will need to add "and set `min_steps=1` if you want to test the early-exit path"; on 2026-08-01 no such kwarg exists and the agent exits as soon as the model emits a `final_answer` tool call.

### entry-124
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the `step_callbacks` registration at lines 130-156 building a `CallbackRegistry`, the dispatch at `_finalize_step` lines 620-623 calling `self.step_callbacks.callback(memory_step, agent=self)`, and the constructor kwarg `step_callbacks: list[Callable] | dict[Type[MemoryStep], Callable | list[Callable]] | None = None` per `inspect.signature(MultiStepAgent.__init__)`), combined with ch-11 entry-101 (the `CallbackRegistry` shape) and ch-12 entry-111 (the `ActionStep` field list the callback receives).
used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: The `step_callbacks` parameter is the test-side hook for "did the agent take the action I expected". A test attaches a callable that records each step into a list, runs the agent with a stub model, and after the run asserts that the recorded list contains the expected step shape — usually one `ActionStep` whose `tool_calls` list names a specific tool, or one `ActionStep` with `is_final_answer=True` whose `model_output` parses to a specific JSON. The chapter's beginner pattern is "register a `def record(step, agent): steps.append((type(step).__name__, getattr(step, 'model_output', None), getattr(step, 'tool_calls', None), getattr(step, 'observations', None)))` callback via `step_callbacks={ActionStep: record}` (the dict form scopes the callback to `ActionStep` only) and then `assert ('ActionStep', None, [expected ToolCall(name='my_tool', arguments={...})], None) in steps` (or the equivalent for a `final_answer` step). The dict form is preferred over the list form because the list form would also fire for `TaskStep` and `PlanningStep`, which the test does not want to assert on. The pattern works because the framework dispatches the callback synchronously inside `_finalize_step`, so the test's list is fully populated by the time `agent.run(...)` returns.
paraphrase: `step_callbacks` is the framework's "did the agent do the right thing" hook. The test attaches a callback that records each step into a list, runs the agent with a stub model that emits a known tool call (or a known final-answer JSON), and after the run asserts that the recorded list contains the expected step. The chapter's recommended shape is the dict form `step_callbacks={ActionStep: record}` so the callback fires for `ActionStep` only and not for `TaskStep` or `PlanningStep`. The assertion is usually one of: (a) "the agent called tool X with arguments Y" (one `ActionStep` with a specific `tool_calls` entry), (b) "the agent emitted `final_answer` with content Z" (one `ActionStep` with `is_final_answer=True` and a specific `model_output`), or (c) "the agent did NOT call any tool" (the recorded list is empty or contains only the final-answer step). This is the test-side replacement for scraping stdout or `print`-ing the model's output, and it works whether the model is a stub or a real model.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `step_callbacks` constructor signature, the `CallbackRegistry` at memory.py lines 280-316, and the dispatch at agents.py:620-623). The dict form is stable across 1.24.0 to 1.26.0. Age-risk: the `CallbackRegistry` refactor was the change that introduced the dict form; if a future release renames the registry or simplifies the dispatch, the chapter's demo will need a one-line update; on 2026-08-01 the dict form is the canonical modern shape and the test pattern is stable.

claim/finding: The test-side pattern for capturing an agent's logs is to subclass `AgentLogger` and override the `log` method (or any specific `log_*` method) to record into a list, then construct the agent with the `logger=` kwarg. The brief's `monitor=` kwarg does not exist on `MultiStepAgent.__init__` in 1.26.0 — the constructor accepts `logger=` (an `AgentLogger` instance or `None`), and the `monitor` attribute is built internally from `Monitor(self.model, self.logger)` after the constructor finishes. The test pattern is therefore `class ListLogger(AgentLogger): def __init__(self): super().__init__(level=LogLevel.OFF); self.records = []; def log(self, *args, **kwargs): self.records.append((args, kwargs))` and then `agent = ToolCallingAgent(tools=[...], model=StubModel(), logger=ListLogger())`. The chapter's beginner rule is "set `level=LogLevel.OFF` on the test logger so the test does not print to the captured pytest stdout, then read `agent.logger.records` after the run and assert on the list". The reason for the override of `log` (the gating method) rather than each `log_*` method is that all eight `log_*` methods eventually call `self.log(...)`, so a single override captures everything. An alternative for tests that do not need log capture is to pass a custom `Console(file=open(os.devnull, "w"))` to the default `AgentLogger` constructor and skip the subclass entirely; this is the lighter option when the test only needs "no logs on the test runner".
paraphrase: To capture the agent's logs in a test, the cleanest path in 1.26.0 is to subclass `AgentLogger`, override the `log` method to record into a list, and pass the instance via the `logger=` constructor kwarg. The brief's `monitor=` kwarg name is a real-world correction — `MultiStepAgent.__init__` does not accept a `monitor=` kwarg; the `monitor` attribute is built internally after the constructor and is not user-settable. The subclass should set `level=LogLevel.OFF` so the test does not print to the captured pytest stdout; the test then reads the recorded list after the run and asserts on what was logged. The override of `log` (the gating method) is preferred over overriding each `log_*` method because all eight `log_*` methods route through `log`, so one override captures everything. The chapter should also mention the lighter alternative — pass a custom `Console(file=...)` to silence the output without subclassing — for tests that only need "no logs on the test runner".

used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: Passing `return_full_result=True` to `agent.run(task, ...)` makes the run return a `RunResult` whose attributes are the test's primary assertion surface. The five fields the chapter's tests need are (a) `result.output` — the agent's final answer, compared to the expected answer string (or to a parsed-JSON shape for structured answers); (b) `result.state` — the string `"success"` for a normal finish or `"max_steps_error"` when the agent ran out of step budget, used to assert that the run finished cleanly; (c) `result.steps` — the full transcript as `list[dict]` (each dict is the JSON projection of one memory step, indexed by key like `"step_number"`, `"model_output"`, `"tool_calls"`, `"observations"`, `"error"`); (d) `result.token_usage` — a `TokenUsage` dataclass with `input_tokens` / `output_tokens` / `total_tokens` or `None`; (e) `result.timing` — a `Timing` dataclass with `start_time` / `end_time` / `duration`. The per-call kwarg overrides the constructor-level `return_full_result` setting, and `None` means "use whatever the constructor set" (the default is `False` on `MultiStepAgent.__init__`). The chapter's tests should set `return_full_result=True` per call rather than relying on the constructor default, because the per-call form makes the test self-documenting — a reader of the test can see at a glance whether the test is asserting on the answer only or on the full run record.
paraphrase: The test-side use of `return_full_result=True` is to make the run return a `RunResult` whose five fields are the assertion surface: `output` for the answer, `state` for "did the run finish cleanly or hit the step budget", `steps` for the per-step transcript (as a list of plain dicts, not typed step objects), `token_usage` for cost attribution, and `timing` for slowness attribution. The per-call kwarg overrides the constructor default, and tests should set it per call to keep the test self-documenting. The chapter's test pattern is `result = agent.run(task, return_full_result=True)`, followed by `assert result.state == "success"` and `assert result.output == expected` (with a `pytest.param` wrapper for `pytest.mark.parametrize` to make the case readable). A `result.state == "max_steps_error"` is a test failure regardless of `result.output` — a `max_steps_error` means the agent gave up, and the answer it produced on the way out is by definition unreliable.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `RunResult` dataclass and the precedence at agents.py:505 and the state computation at 523-526). Stable across 1.24.0 to 1.26.0. Age-risk: the `state` field is a `Literal["success", "max_steps_error"]` in 1.26.0; if a future release adds a third value (e.g. `"interrupted"` for human-in-the-loop cancellation), the chapter's "test asserts `result.state == "success"`" pattern will need to consider the new branch; on 2026-08-01 only the two values are documented.

used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: The "gold answer" test pattern is the simplest test an agent can have: build an agent with a stub model that emits a known final-answer JSON, run the agent, and assert that the returned `output` (or `result.output` when `return_full_result=True`) matches the expected string. The pattern requires no real model, no network, no API key, and runs in milliseconds. The chapter's runnable demo is a `def test_gold_answer_returns_expected_string(): agent = ToolCallingAgent(tools=[], model=StubModel(fixed_final_answer="42"), max_steps=1); result = agent.run("What is the answer?", return_full_result=True); assert result.state == "success"; assert result.output == "42"` style test, where the stub model's `generate` method returns a canned `ChatMessage` whose `content` is the JSON `{"name": "final_answer", "arguments": {"answer": "42"}}`. The pattern is gold because the test asserts on the agent's *output*, not on the model's *raw response* — the agent's `parse_tool_calls` method may massage the raw JSON, the framework's `final_answer` tool may wrap the value, and only the agent's final `.output` is what the caller actually sees. The pattern catches regressions in the agent's wiring (final-answer parsing, validator gate, `RunResult` construction, step-loop termination) without depending on the model's behavior.
paraphrase: The gold-answer test is the simplest test an agent can have and the one beginners should write first. The pattern: build an agent with a stub model that emits a known final-answer JSON, run the agent, assert that the returned `output` matches the expected string. The test runs in milliseconds, requires no network, and catches regressions in the agent's wiring (final-answer parsing, validator gate, `RunResult` construction, step-loop termination) without depending on the model's behavior. The key shape is "assert on the agent's `output`, not on the model's raw response" — the agent's `parse_tool_calls` may massage the JSON, the framework's `final_answer` tool may wrap the value, and only the agent's final `.output` is what the caller sees. The chapter should also call out the inverse pattern: a stub model that emits an intermediate tool call (not `final_answer`) lets the test assert that the agent routed through the tool menu, but that pattern requires registering a tool and asserting on `result.steps` instead of `result.output` (entry-124).
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `RunResult.output` shape, the `MultiStepAgent.run` return at line 538, and the `FinalAnswerStep` yield at line 611). The gold-answer pattern is itself a tutorial artifact with no third-party version to compare against. Stable across 1.24.0 to 1.26.0. Age-risk: if a future smolagents release changes the `final_answer` tool's default arguments schema (e.g. from `{"answer": "..."}` to `{"value": "..."}`), the canned JSON in the stub's `generate` will need to match; on 2026-08-01 the schema is `{"answer": "..."}` per the `FinalAnswerTool.inputs` at ch-08 entry-068.

source: pytest 9.1.1 docs at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/fixtures.html (the "About fixtures" page at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/explanation/about-fixtures.html for the fixture-function-as-arg-collector mental model and the "request" fixture at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/reference/reference.html#fixtures) and https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/assert.html (the `pytest.raises` reference at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/reference/reference.html#pytest-raises) for the test-side shape, combined with installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\utils.py` (`AgentMaxStepsError(AgentError)` at line 116, defined in the exception hierarchy verified in ch-11 entry-106) and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the raise site at `_handle_max_steps_reached` line 625, and the `state="max_steps_error"` computation at line 523-526 per entry-126). used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: The chapter's two pytest primitives are `@pytest.fixture` (to share the agent and stub model across multiple tests without rebuilding them in each test body) and `pytest.raises` (to assert that a code path raises a specific exception). The recommended fixture shape is a `conftest.py` that defines a session-scoped or function-scoped fixture for the stub model (so the stub is reused across tests in the same session when it is expensive to construct) and a function-scoped fixture for the agent (so each test gets a fresh `agent.memory` and `agent.monitor` per ch-12 entry-119's "memory is empty before `.run()`" rule). The `pytest.raises` shape is `with pytest.raises(AgentMaxStepsError): agent.run(task, max_steps=1)` — the test runs the agent with `max_steps=1` against a stub model that emits a tool call (not a `final_answer`), the inner step loop consumes the one step and never sees a `final_answer`, the framework raises `AgentMaxStepsError` at the `_handle_max_steps_reached` site, and the `with` block asserts that the exception actually fired. The chapter's beginner rule is "use `pytest.raises` to assert that an expected exception fired; do NOT use a bare `try/except` because the bare form silently swallows the exception if it does not fire". The reason for `max_steps=1` in the failure case is that the test should be the fastest path to the exception — a real-model test that takes 30 seconds to fail is not a test, it is a stall.
paraphrase: The two pytest primitives the chapter needs are `@pytest.fixture` (for sharing the agent and stub model across tests without rebuilding them per test body) and `pytest.raises` (for asserting that a code path raised a specific exception). The recommended shape is a fixture per test file that builds the stub model once and the agent per test (so each test gets a fresh `agent.memory` per ch-12's "memory is empty before `.run()`" rule). The `pytest.raises` pattern is the cleanest way to assert that a code path raised — `with pytest.raises(AgentMaxStepsError): agent.run(task, max_steps=1)` lets the framework's `AgentMaxStepsError` fire and the `with` block asserts that it actually did. The chapter's beginner rule is to use `pytest.raises`, not a bare `try/except`, because the bare form silently swallows the exception when it does not fire. The `max_steps=1` in the failure case keeps the test fast — the fastest path to the exception is the right path for a unit test.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (the `AgentMaxStepsError` class at utils.py:116 and the raise site at agents.py:625) and against the pytest 9.1.1 docs for the test-side shape. Stable across 1.24.0 to 1.26.0 for the smolagents side; the pytest fixture and `raises` API has been stable since pytest 3.x. Age-risk: if a future smolagents release changes the exception family (e.g. splits `AgentMaxStepsError` into `AgentMaxStepsError` and `AgentTimeoutError`), the chapter's `pytest.raises(AgentMaxStepsError)` call will need a tuple of classes; on 2026-08-01 the family is the six classes documented in ch-11 entry-106.

### entry-129
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (verified 2026-08-01 via `inspect.iscoroutinefunction(MultiStepAgent.run)` returning `False` — the `.run` method is a plain `def`, not `async def`; the signature at lines 436-445 confirms `def run(self, task: str, stream: bool = False, reset: bool = True, ...)`), combined with the pytest-asyncio 1.4.0 docs at https://pytest-asyncio.readthedocs.io/en/stable/reference/configuration.html (the `asyncio_mode = "auto"` configuration option) and the pytest 9.1.1 fixture docs at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/fixtures.html (the `scope` argument and the async-fixture interaction). The installed venv has `pytest-asyncio==1.4.0` (verified via `importlib.metadata.version` on 2026-08-01). Cross-referenced with ch-12 entry-115 (the `RunResult` shape) and the ch-12 ch-13 forward pointer (entry-120) that mentions "the loop becomes a test report".
used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: `smolagents.MultiStepAgent.run` is a synchronous function in 1.26.0 — `inspect.iscoroutinefunction(MultiStepAgent.run)` returns `False` and the source is a plain `def` (not `async def`). The chapter's `pytest-asyncio` discussion is therefore scoped to "asynchronous tools the user might write" rather than "asynchronous agent run". The two relevant configurations are `asyncio_mode = "auto"` (set in `pyproject.toml` or `pytest.ini` under `[pytest]`) and `@pytest.mark.asyncio` on individual test functions; the chapter should teach the explicit-marker form first (lower magic, easier to debug) and the auto mode as a one-line `pyproject.toml` option for projects that prefer it. The chapter should also call out the four common beginner errors: (a) setting `asyncio_mode = "auto"` globally without ever using `async def` test functions — pytest-asyncio will warn that the mode is set but no async tests are present; (b) defining a test as `async def test_foo():` without `@pytest.mark.asyncio` and without `asyncio_mode = "auto"` — pytest will not await the coroutine, the test body never runs, and the test passes vacuously; (c) mixing sync and async fixtures in the same test without matching scopes — the `event_loop` fixture's scope must match the test's scope, otherwise pytest-asyncio emits "ScopeMismatch" errors; (d) calling `time.sleep(...)` in an async test — this blocks the event loop, the test runs synchronously despite the `async` keyword, and any concurrent test in the same loop stalls. The chapter's beginner recipe is to use plain `def` test functions for agent tests (since `.run` is sync) and to leave pytest-asyncio for the rare case where a `@tool`-decorated function does async work.
paraphrase: `MultiStepAgent.run` is a synchronous function in 1.26.0, so the chapter's `pytest-asyncio` discussion is about async tools the user might write, not async agent runs. The two configurations are `asyncio_mode = "auto"` (in `pyproject.toml`) and `@pytest.mark.asyncio` on individual tests; the chapter should teach the explicit-marker form first because it is less magic and easier to debug. The four common beginner errors are: setting `asyncio_mode = "auto"` without ever writing async tests, defining an `async def` test without the marker or auto mode (the test passes vacuously without running), mixing sync and async fixture scopes, and calling `time.sleep` in an async test (which blocks the event loop). The chapter's beginner recipe is to use plain `def` test functions for agent tests (because `.run` is sync) and to defer pytest-asyncio to the rare case where a custom tool wraps async work.
variance_resolution: Verified against installed 1.26.0 source (`inspect.iscoroutinefunction(MultiStepAgent.run) == False`) and against the pytest-asyncio 1.4.0 docs (which document both `asyncio_mode` and the marker). Stable across 1.24.0 to 1.26.0 for the smolagents side (`.run` has been sync since 1.0); the pytest-asyncio `asyncio_mode` option is stable since 0.21. Age-risk: if a future smolagents release adds an `async def run` (e.g. for native streaming), the chapter's "use `def` test functions" rule will need a one-line reversal; on 2026-08-01 `.run` is sync and the rule holds.

### entry-130
source: pytest 9.1.1 docs at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/parametrize.html (the basic `@pytest.mark.parametrize("arg", values)` form, the `pytest.param(value, id=..., marks=...)` form for per-case ids and marks, and the `ids=` argument for explicit test IDs as demonstrated in the "Different options for test IDs" section), combined with the stub-model pattern (entry-122) and the `RunResult` shape (entry-126). Cross-referenced with ch-12 entry-118 (the `(task, expected_answer)` tuple pattern that the parametrize form generalizes) and ch-12 entry-120 (the runnable evaluator loop that the parametrize form replaces).
used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: `pytest.mark.parametrize` is the test-side pattern for running the same test body against a list of input/expected-output tuples, with pytest building one test case per tuple and reporting each case as a separate pass/fail line. The chapter's recommended shape is `cases = [("2+2", "4"), ("reverse abc", "cba"), ("capitalize hello", "Hello")]` followed by `@pytest.mark.parametrize("task,expected", cases, ids=["add", "reverse", "capitalize"])` on the test function, with the test body consuming the `task` and `expected` fixtures. The `ids=` argument makes the pytest output human-readable — `test_gold[add] PASSED` instead of `test_gold[0] PASSED` — and the `pytest.param(value, id=..., marks=...)` form lets a single case carry a custom mark (e.g. `pytest.mark.xfail` for a known-bad case that should fail). The parametrize form is the test-side generalization of ch-12's `cases = [(task, expected)]` loop (entry-118) — same shape, but pytest owns the loop and the test report. The chapter's beginner rule is "use `ids=` for human-readable case names" and "use `pytest.param` for per-case marks (xfail, skip, custom markers)". For tests that need a different stub model per case (e.g. one case that emits a tool call and one that emits `final_answer`), the chapter should teach the indirect-parametrization form at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/parametrize.html#indirect-parametrization — `@pytest.mark.parametrize("stub,expected", [("add_stub", "4"), ("reverse_stub", "cba")], indirect=["stub"])` plus a `def stub(request): return StubModel.from_name(request.param)` fixture that builds the right stub for each case.
paraphrase: `pytest.mark.parametrize` is the test-side generalization of ch-12's evaluator loop. The test function carries a list of input/expected-output tuples via the decorator, pytest builds one test case per tuple, and the pytest output reports each case as a separate pass/fail line. The recommended shape is `@pytest.mark.parametrize("task,expected", cases, ids=[...])` with a per-case `ids=` list for human-readable output and `pytest.param(value, id=..., marks=...)` for per-case marks like `xfail`. For tests that need a different stub model per case, the indirect-parametrization form (`indirect=["stub"]`) routes the tuple value through a fixture that builds the right stub. The chapter's beginner rules are "always pass `ids=` for human-readable case names" and "use `pytest.param` for per-case marks" — these are the small habits that turn a 30-line test file into a 10-line file that still produces a 30-line report.
variance_resolution: Verified against the pytest 9.1.1 docs (the parametrize examples at the link above) and against the installed pytest 9.1.1 in the venv. The parametrize API is stable since pytest 3.x. Age-risk: if a future pytest release renames `ids=` or changes the `pytest.param` signature, the chapter's examples will need a one-line update; on 2026-08-01 the API is the same as it has been since pytest 4.x.

### entry-131
source: aggregated beginner-error patterns verified against installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the `MultiStepAgent.run` return shape at line 538 `return output` for the default path versus lines 530-536 for the `return_full_result=True` path; the `state` computation at lines 523-526; the `RunResult.steps` shape at line 213 `list[dict]`), `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\memory.py` (the `ActionStep.token_usage` field at line 51-64 and the `Monitor.get_total_token_counts` at monitoring.py lines 110-117), and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\monitoring.py` (the `Timing.duration` property at lines 67-68 returning a wall-clock float). Cross-referenced with ch-12 entry-119 (the parallel "read memory only after `.run()` returns" trap) and the pytest 9.1.1 docs for the test-side error shapes.
used_in: ch-13
claim/finding: Four beginner errors the chapter should call out explicitly. (a) Asserting on the model's raw response instead of the agent's `output`: a test that reads `result.steps[0].model_output` (the raw `ChatMessage.content` string) and asserts on it is testing the stub, not the agent — the right surface is `result.output`, which is what the caller actually sees after `parse_tool_calls` and the `final_answer` tool have run. The chapter's fix is "always assert on `result.output`; if you need the raw model content, assert on `result.steps[0].model_output` and label the test as a model-output test, not an agent-output test". (b) Forgetting `max_steps=1` and watching the test run for 30 seconds against a real model: the chapter's rule is "set `max_steps=1` on every test that does not explicitly want to drive the inner step loop". (c) Comparing `result.timing.duration` or `result.token_usage` between runs that should be equivalent: both are wall-clock and provider-side quantities that drift between runs and between providers, so a test that asserts `result.timing.duration < 1.0` will flake on a slow CI runner. The chapter's rule is "assert on `result.output` and `result.state`; only assert on `result.timing.duration` or `result.token_usage` when the test is explicitly a performance or cost test, and use generous bounds". (d) Setting `asyncio_mode = "auto"` globally without ever using `async def` test functions: pytest-asyncio emits a warning on every collection, the warning floods the test output, and the user assumes the warning is benign noise rather than a configuration mistake. The chapter's rule is "set `asyncio_mode = "auto"` only when at least one test in the suite is `async def`; otherwise leave it unset".
paraphrase: Four traps the chapter's first runnable example should pre-empt. First, asserting on the model's raw response (`result.steps[0].model_output`) instead of the agent's output (`result.output`) — that asserts on the stub, not the agent; the right surface is `result.output`. Second, forgetting `max_steps=1` on a test that does not need the inner step loop and watching the test stall against a real model — the rule is "set `max_steps=1` on every test that does not explicitly want to drive the inner loop". Third, comparing `result.timing.duration` or `result.token_usage` between runs that should be equivalent — both are wall-clock and provider-side quantities that drift, so a `result.timing.duration < 1.0` assertion will flake on a slow CI runner; the rule is to assert on `result.output` and `result.state` and only assert on timing or tokens when the test is explicitly a performance or cost test. Fourth, setting `asyncio_mode = "auto"` globally without ever using `async def` test functions — pytest-asyncio emits a warning on every collection that floods the test output; the rule is to set the mode only when at least one test is `async def`.
variance_resolution: Every claim above is verified against installed 1.26.0 source. Stable across 1.24.0 to 1.26.0. Age-risk: if a future pytest-asyncio release silences the "no async tests present" warning, the chapter's fourth error becomes less noisy and the rule can be relaxed; on 2026-08-01 the warning is loud and the rule is worth stating.

## ch-14 — Keep Agents Safe and Responsible

### entry-133
source: NIST — AI Risk Management Framework 1.0 overview and canonical publication path (https://www.nist.gov/itl/ai-risk-management-framework and https://doi.org/10.6028/NIST.AI.100-1); OWASP GenAI Security Project — LLM01:2025 Prompt Injection (https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/); Anthropic — "Mitigating the risk of prompt injections in browser use" (https://www.anthropic.com/news/prompt-injection-defenses). chub was searched first; it had no NIST or OWASP safety entry, and `chub get anthropic/claude-api --lang py` covered the SDK rather than prompt-injection safety, so the official primary pages were used.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: Agent safety is broader than chatbot safety because an agent combines model output with tool results, web content, persistent state, credentials, and actions that can change the world. OWASP says impact depends on both business context and the agency granted to the model; Anthropic says browser agents expand both the attack surface and the number of actions an attacker can influence. NIST frames risk management across design, development, use, and evaluation, so safety is a lifecycle property rather than one system prompt.
paraphrase: Treat the model as "a junior employee with sudo". The question is not whether the model sounds trustworthy; it is which inputs it may trust, which capabilities it receives, who reviews high-impact actions, and how the run stops.

### entry-134
source: OWASP GenAI Security Project — LLM01:2025 Prompt Injection, definition, indirect-injection examples, and mitigation sections (https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/); OpenAI — "Safety in building agents" (https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/agent-builder-safety); cross-reference ch-06 entry-050 on many-shot jailbreaking.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: Prompt injection is untrusted text or data that changes model behavior in an unintended way. It can be direct in a user prompt or indirect in a webpage, file, email, image, or tool result. A sentence such as "ignore previous instructions" is not a sufficient defense: OWASP says RAG, fine-tuning, and prompt safeguards do not fully remove the vulnerability, while the many-shot result in entry-050 shows that more adversarial context can weaken instruction following. OpenAI recommends keeping untrusted data from directly driving tool calls, extracting narrow structured fields, and combining guardrails, tool confirmations, and isolation.
paraphrase: Label web and file content as untrusted data, reduce it to validated fields before it reaches an action, give the model least privilege, and require a person to approve high-risk operations. Prompt wording is one layer, not the boundary.

### entry-135
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\local_python_executor.py:1688-1705`; Hugging Face smolagents v1.26.0 secure-code-execution tutorial (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/tutorials/secure_code_execution); cross-references ch-08 entry-071 and ch-09 entry-082.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: Classify tools by their strongest possible side effect. Read-only tools include search, Wikipedia, and webpage fetches; mixed workflows read data and then write or transmit it, such as visiting a page and sending email; effectful executors such as a shell can read, write, delete, run programs, and reach the network. The installed `LocalPythonExecutor` warning is explicit: it restricts imports and built-ins but "is not a security sandbox". A tool remains a capability boundary, not a safety boundary, because an authorized tool runs its own side effects.
paraphrase: Start with read-only tools. Add network sends, file writes, shell execution, or account changes one at a time, with narrow inputs, a preview, and human approval before irreversible effects.

### entry-136
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\default_tools.py:39-76`, `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\local_python_executor.py:360-380`, and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\utils.py:49-61`; verified with `E:\book_gen\.venv\Scripts\python.exe` on 2026-08-01. Cross-reference ch-09 entry-079.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: In 1.26.0, `PythonInterpreterTool(authorized_imports=...)` builds an import allowlist from eleven base modules plus the names supplied by the caller; imports outside that union are rejected, while `"*"` opens the fence. Two requested details need correction. `LocalPythonExecutor._check_import_is_allowed` does not exist and `inspect.getsource(...)` raises `AttributeError`; the installed enforcement function is module-level `check_import_authorized`. Also, `authorized_imports=None` does not mean zero imports: it means no extra imports, so the eleven base modules remain allowed.
paraphrase: Use the smallest explicit import list and never pass `"*"`. Remember the exact rule: `None` means "base modules only", not "nothing allowed". This is a hard import fence inside the evaluator, but it does not turn local execution into a process sandbox. Age-risk: the function name and base-module list are pinned to 1.26.0.

### entry-137
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:1505-1618` and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\remote_executors.py:335-965`; Hugging Face smolagents v1.26.0 secure-code-execution tutorial (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/tutorials/secure_code_execution). Cross-reference ch-08 entry-064.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: `CodeAgent` accepts `executor_type="local"`, `"blaxel"`, `"e2b"`, `"modal"`, or `"docker"`. Local needs no optional executor extra and runs on the host. Blaxel, E2B, and Modal require their optional packages plus provider setup and credentials. Docker requires the optional Docker package and a running local Docker daemon, not a provider account. The installed factory also rejects managed agents with non-local execution in 1.26.0.
paraphrase: Keep `local` for trusted exercises. Use Docker or a provider-backed sandbox for untrusted generated code, clean the sandbox up after each run, and test the exact executor before shipping. Age-risk: supported executor names and the managed-agent restriction are version-specific.

### entry-138
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:268-350` and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:606-638`; chub `smolagents/package` "Constrain the Loop" section; cross-references ch-08 entry-066 and ch-10 entry-091.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: `max_steps` is the agent-loop circuit breaker. The constructor default is 20, and `.run()` can override it for one run. There is no `max_duration` kwarg in 1.26.0. On exhaustion the framework records `AgentMaxStepsError`, asks the model for one final answer, and returns a `RunResult` state of `"max_steps_error"`. The cap does not time out one slow tool call, so network tools still need request timeouts and code execution still needs its separate executor timeout.
paraphrase: `max_steps=20` is a normal starting point. Lower it for narrow jobs, raise it only with evidence, and pair it with per-tool timeouts. A step cap prevents runaway reasoning; it is not a wall-clock deadline. Age-risk: the default and the absence of a duration kwarg are pinned to 1.26.0.

### entry-139
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:335-336`, `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:574-618`, and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:625-638`; cross-reference ch-11 entry-102.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: `final_answer_checks` is a list of validators, not post-processors. Each callable receives the proposed final answer, memory, and agent; a truthy result accepts it, while a false result or exception becomes `AgentError` and the loop continues. For a suggestion-only agent, use this hook as a final-answer allowlist that rejects output resembling shell commands or unapproved URLs. One 1.26.0 caveat matters: the final answer generated after `max_steps` is exhausted does not pass through `_validate_final_answer`, so an application that needs a hard guarantee must validate the returned output again at its own boundary.
paraphrase: Let suggestion agents describe safe actions, not execute them. Reject command-shaped or URL-shaped answers with `final_answer_checks`, then apply the same check once more before displaying or storing the final `RunResult.output`. Age-risk: the max-step bypass and callable signature are verified only for 1.26.0.

### entry-140
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\default_tools.py:104-157` and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\default_tools.py:491-530`; verified with `E:\book_gen\.venv\Scripts\python.exe` on 2026-08-01. Cross-references ch-09 entries 079-080.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: `DuckDuckGoSearchTool` defaults to `rate_limit=1.0`, and 1.26.0 defines that value as maximum queries per second; it computes the minimum interval as the reciprocal. The default therefore produces one second between requests, but the unit is not "seconds between requests". `VisitWebpageTool` defaults to `max_output_length=40000`, performs a plain HTTP GET with a 20-second timeout, converts returned HTML to Markdown, and does not execute JavaScript. It accepts an arbitrary URL and has no built-in domain allowlist.
paraphrase: Keep the default search rate or lower it, cap fetched text, and wrap webpage access with scheme, domain, redirect, and private-network checks. For ch-17, preserve the built-in no-JavaScript behavior. Age-risk: both defaults and the rate-limit unit are pinned to 1.26.0.

### entry-141
source: Python Software Foundation — `logging.Filter` documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/logging.html#filter-objects) and `os.environ` documentation (https://docs.python.org/3/library/os.html#os.environ); chub `python-dotenv/package` as recorded in ch-07 entries 053 and 059; OpenAI — "Safety in building agents" (https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/agent-builder-safety); installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:196-253` and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:582-586`; cross-references ch-12 entries 113-114.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: Secrets belong in environment variables loaded from an ignored `.env`, never in prompts, source, or logs. Python logging filters can modify a record before a handler emits it, so redact API keys, bearer tokens, email addresses, and other user data before persistence. smolagents logs a proposed final answer at INFO and `RunResult` can include output, steps, token counts, and timing; a raw result sent to a UI or log can therefore expose private transcript data. Four beginner errors to name are: treating `PythonInterpreterTool` as a sandbox; allowing arbitrary outbound URLs without a domain allowlist; treating webpage text as trusted data rather than possible instructions; and returning a raw `RunResult` without stripping secrets and internal steps.
paraphrase: Keep `.env` out of git, never print credentials, and persist only the fields the product needs. Redact emails and tokens from `RunResult.output` and step text before logging or showing them. Test all four trust boundaries with the deterministic patterns from ch-13.

### entry-142
source: `E:\book_gen\books\ai-agents-with-python\skeleton.md`, ch-14 through ch-18 dependency rows; installed smolagents==1.26.0 `VisitWebpageTool` source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\default_tools.py:491-530`; cross-references ch-13 entry-132 and the permission, injection, and executor findings in entries 133-141.
used_in: ch-14
claim/finding: Three forward pointers close the chapter. ch-15 expands blast radius: an attacker who compromises one agent can feed poisoned output to every agent that trusts it. ch-17 must keep research-page content as untrusted data and must never execute page JavaScript; the installed `VisitWebpageTool` already fetches static HTML without a browser runtime. ch-18 must enforce separate tool and data scopes per agent so one specialist cannot read or change another specialist domain merely because the manager can.
paraphrase: One agent needs scoped tools, bounded runs, validated outputs, and careful logs. A team needs those controls per agent and at every handoff; ch-15, ch-17, and ch-18 apply that rule at larger blast radii.
