## ch-15 — Coordinate Multiple Agents

### entry-143
source: ch-01 entry-007 (Anthropic "Building effective agents", 2024-12-19, "Building blocks, workflows, and agents" section) and ch-11 entry-103 (per-agent max_steps and planning_interval semantics); both read from `E:\book_gen\books\ai-agents-with-python\research-log.md` on 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: A single agent loads its system prompt, tool descriptions, prior memory, and step history into one LLM call. As tasks grow, the prompt grows, tool descriptions compete for the model's attention, and instructions for one role leak into another. Multi-agent splits the work by giving each agent a narrower prompt, a narrower tool list, and a focused description, then letting a manager route work to the right one. This is the second rung of Anthropic's spectrum: a workflow whose steps are themselves small agentic loops.
paraphrase: One agent with everything is fine for small jobs. For larger jobs, give each agent its own system prompt, its own tool list, and its own description; let a manager route work to the right one. The chapter's first beat is "why split at all"; the answer is specialization, shorter prompts, and tighter tool lists, not parallelism.

### entry-144
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature (verified `managed_agents: list | None = None` kwarg), `_setup_managed_agents` source at lines 102-120 (`self.managed_agents = {agent.name: agent for agent in managed_agents}` plus the JSON-schema `inputs` rewrite at lines 111-120). Cross-reference ch-11 entry-099.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: In smolagents 1.26.0, both `CodeAgent(tools=[...], managed_agents=[researcher, writer])` and `ToolCallingAgent(tools=[...], managed_agents=[researcher, writer])` accept a list of agents under `managed_agents=`. `_setup_managed_agents` asserts that every entry has a non-empty `name` and `description`, then stores them in a `dict` keyed by `agent.name`. Each child is rewritten with an `inputs` JSON schema whose named keys are `task` and `additional_args`, plus an `output_type = "string"`, so the parent treats the child exactly like a callable tool.
paraphrase: The "manager" is just an agent that was given a list under `managed_agents=[...]`. The "managed" name refers to the agents inside that list; they are exposed to the manager by their `agent.name` and called exactly like a `@tool`-wrapped function. Each child must have a unique non-empty `name` and a non-empty `description`, otherwise the framework raises an assertion error at construction. Chapter mental model: manager = parent agent; managed = child agents the parent can call by name.

### entry-145
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__call__` source at lines 601-623 (the `populate_template` calls at lines 605-608 and 614-615, with `variables=dict(name=self.name, task=task)` and `variables=dict(name=self.name, final_answer=report)`), and the default Jinja templates at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\prompts\code_agent.yaml` under the `managed_agent:` key. Cross-reference ch-11 entry-099.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: When the manager calls a managed agent, the framework formats two Jinja templates from `prompt_templates["managed_agent"]`: the `task` template (filled with the child's name and the new task string) is what gets fed into the child; the `report` template (filled with the child's name and its `final_answer`) is what the manager receives back. The actual Jinja variable names in the 1.26.0 default template are `{{name}}`, `{{task}}` for the task prompt and `{{name}}`, `{{final_answer}}` for the report. The dispatch's expected keys `{{managed_agent.report}}` and `{{managed_agent.task}}` were speculative; the verified 1.26.0 keys are the inner Jinja names listed above, not nested paths.
quote: Here is the final answer from your managed agent '{{name}}':
{{final_answer}}
paraphrase: Two Jinja templates format every call. The task prompt that gets sent into the child is rendered from `{{name}}` and `{{task}}`; the report prompt that gets returned to the parent is rendered from `{{name}}` and `{{final_answer}}`. The variable names are inner Jinja keys, not dotted paths. Any chapter copy that copies the dispatch's expected key names will render as a literal placeholder in the child's prompt. Mark this entry as an age-risk: a user-supplied `prompt_templates={...}` override can rename the variables, so the chapter should teach the verified defaults and note that custom prompts are an advanced lever.
variance_resolution: Verified directly against installed 1.26.0 source on 2026-08-01; the v1.26.0 reference page does not document the Jinja variable names, so the YAML defaults are the only authoritative source. Cross-reference ch-11 entry-099 confirms the dispatch had anticipated different key names.

### entry-146
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source: each `MultiStepAgent` instantiates its own `AgentMemory` (verified in `MultiStepAgent.__init__` via `self.memory = AgentMemory()` at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`) and its own `step_number` counter; `_setup_managed_agents` only rewrites each child's `inputs` and `output_type`, not its memory. Cross-reference ch-11 entry-099 and ch-10 entry-093.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: Each agent, including a managed agent, owns its own `self.memory` (an `AgentMemory` instance) and its own step counter. The manager does not see the actions the managed agent took unless the child puts them in its final answer. Cross-agent state must be passed explicitly: either through `additional_args` on the call (which the manager puts into its own `self.state` for the next step), or by having the manager capture the child's `final_answer` and re-inject it into a later step's prompt.
paraphrase: Two agents in a multi-agent system do not share a blackboard. Each one's memory is local. If the manager needs to see what the researcher found, the manager has to wait for the child's `final_answer` and then put it into a later step's prompt or `additional_args`. Chapter rule: if the manager needs it, pass it explicitly.

### entry-147
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__call__` calls `self.run(full_task, **kwargs)` at line 609, where `self` is the managed agent (the child), and `MultiStepAgent.run` resolves `max_steps = max_steps or self.max_steps` at line 201, so the child uses its OWN `max_steps` value, not the manager's. Cross-reference ch-11 entry-103 and ch-14 entry-138.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: `max_steps` does NOT cascade from manager to managed agents. The managed agent's `__call__` constructs the inner task and calls `self.run(...)` on the CHILD's `MultiStepAgent`, which reads `self.max_steps` (the child's constructor default of 20 unless overridden). Setting `manager_kwargs={"max_steps": 5}` does NOT reduce the specialist's step budget; the specialist's constructor must pass `max_steps=5` separately when it is built.
paraphrase: `max_steps` is per-agent. The manager's step cap does not shrink the specialist's step cap. If you want a tight loop for a fast specialist, pass `max_steps=5` when you CONSTRUCT that specialist, not when you construct the manager. This is one of the four beginner errors the chapter should call out explicitly.

### entry-148
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` stores `self.planning_interval` at line 63 (default `None`), and `_run_stream` triggers a planning step at lines 283-284 when `self.step_number == 1` or `(self.step_number - 1) % self.planning_interval == 0`. Cross-reference ch-10 entry-088 (planning_interval semantics).
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: `planning_interval` adds an internal planner step to ONE agent's existing ReAct loop: when set to a positive integer N, a `PlanningStep` is inserted every N steps inside the same `self._run_stream`. `managed_agents` spawns separate agent instances that the manager invokes as tools. They solve different problems: `planning_interval` is one agent that re-plans mid-run; `managed_agents` is a team of agents where each one runs its own loop and returns its own answer.
paraphrase: More thinking inside one agent is `planning_interval`. More workers in a team is `managed_agents`. They are not interchangeable. A manager with `planning_interval=4` does not delegate; it just stops every four steps to write a fresh plan. A managed agent does not get the manager's plan; it gets the formatted task prompt and runs its own loop.

### entry-149
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__call__` wraps the child's `RunResult.output` via `populate_template(self.prompt_templates["managed_agent"]["report"], ...)` at line 614; the manager reads that wrapped string as the tool output of one of its own steps. Cross-reference ch-10 entry-093 (`additional_args` kwarg on `MultiStepAgent.run`).
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: When a managed agent finishes, the manager receives the formatted string returned by the child's `__call__`. The manager can then put that string into `additional_args` of its next step (via `self.run(..., additional_args={"research_notes": child_output})`) so the next action step has access to it, or it can stop and call `final_answer`. There is no automatic merging; the manager must choose what to do with the child's report.
paraphrase: A managed-agent handoff is: the manager calls the child, gets a wrapped string back, and decides what to do next. Common choices are to drop the report into the next step's `additional_args`, ask another managed agent to evaluate it, or stop and call `final_answer` with it. Nothing happens automatically; the manager is in charge of every handoff.

### entry-150
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent._run_stream` runs ONE model call per step and processes its tool calls sequentially within that step (the `while not returned_final_answer and self.step_number <= max_steps` loop at line 278); the managed-agent invocation site is `MultiStepAgent.__call__` at lines 601-623, which is synchronous and returns a single string. Cross-reference ch-11 entry-104.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: `managed_agents` are invoked sequentially in smolagents 1.26.0. The manager calls one at a time; there is no native fan-out within a single step. To achieve parallel-style aggregation, the manager must issue multiple `managed_agent.task` calls in different steps (one step per specialist call) and then read each report in a later step. True parallel fan-out (concurrent managed-agent calls within a single step) is not supported in 1.26.0. Marked as a current limitation, not a future promise.
paraphrase: A manager that wants three independent opinions issues three separate calls in three separate steps, then aggregates. The framework does not fan out within a single step. If you need concurrent execution, you have to drop down to `asyncio` or a worker pool outside the manager's loop. Age-risk: the sequential-invocation rule is pinned to 1.26.0; a future smolagents release that adds parallel managed calls would invalidate this guidance.

### entry-151
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: each managed agent is a separate `MultiStepAgent` with its own `tools`, `model`, `executor_type`, and `final_answer_checks` (verified via `_setup_tools` at lines 122-135, the constructor kwargs, and `_validate_final_answer` at lines 346-351). Cross-reference ch-14 entries 135-139 and entry-141; cross-reference ch-11 entry-099.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: Multi-agent systems widen the blast radius. A compromised managed agent can return poisoned text that the manager trusts and propagates into `final_answer`. The defenses are per-agent and applied at every handoff: separate tool lists per role (a research agent does not get a `python_interpreter`); `executor_type='docker'` for any write-heavy specialist; narrow `authorized_imports`; `final_answer_checks` on every specialist that returns untrusted text; and redaction of secrets from any `RunResult.output` before it lands in a log or UI.
paraphrase: One agent needs scoped tools, bounded runs, validated outputs, and careful logs (ch-14). A team needs those controls per agent and at every handoff. The chapter's first safety beat is blast radius: a compromised researcher poisons the manager; a compromised writer leaks whatever the manager trusted. The mitigations are not new; they are the ch-14 controls applied at every agent boundary. Cross-reference ch-14 entry-141 for the logging and redaction baseline.

### entry-152
source: Anthropic "Building effective agents" (https://www.anthropic.com/research/building-effective-agents), published 2024-12-19, "Orchestrator-workers", "Evaluator-optimizer", and "Routing" subsections, read on 2026-08-01. Cross-reference ch-01 entry-007 (Anthropic mental model) and ch-11 entry-098 (Anthropic five-pattern taxonomy as recorded in the ch-11 research log).
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: Three reusable multi-agent shapes are worth naming in ch-15. (a) Orchestrator-workers: one manager dynamically decomposes a task and dispatches to specialists; this is the shape that `managed_agents=[...]` implements directly. (b) Sequential handoff: agent A then agent B then a manager aggregates; this matches a small chain of `.run()` calls per ch-11 entry-104, with the manager role played by the last agent in the chain. (c) Evaluator-optimizer: a single agent produces, a separate reviewer agent critiques; this matches the conditional-loop pattern in ch-11 entry-105 with two managed agents, a producer and a critic, run until the critic accepts.
paraphrase: Three shapes. Orchestrator-workers is "one manager, several specialists"; `managed_agents=[...]` is the smolagents implementation. Sequential handoff is `A then B then manager`; chain three agents in code per ch-11 entry-104. Evaluator-optimizer is "producer plus critic"; run two managed agents in a loop per ch-11 entry-105, with the critic's verdict as the loop guard. The chapter names these three because they map cleanly onto what smolagents 1.26.0 supports; the other two Anthropic patterns (chaining and parallelization) live inside one agent and are ch-11 territory.

### entry-153
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source verified across `MultiStepAgent.__call__` at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py:601-623`, `MultiStepAgent.run` at lines 169-271, `_setup_managed_agents` at lines 102-120, `_setup_tools` at lines 122-135, and the executor factory at lines 1505-1618. Cross-references ch-11 entry-099, ch-14 entries 136-137, and ch-14 entry-141.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: Four traps the chapter's first multi-agent example should pre-empt. (a) Expecting shared memory between the manager and a managed agent: there is none by default; pass context explicitly via `additional_args` (per entry-146 and ch-14 entry-141). (b) Assuming a manager-level step cap cascades to specialists: it does not; each agent's `max_steps` is per-instance (per entry-147 and ch-11 entry-103). (c) Assuming `managed_agents=[a, b]` runs `a` and `b` in parallel: it does not; the manager invokes them sequentially, one per step (per entry-150). (d) Letting a managed agent with `executor_type='local'` and broad `authorized_imports` call host-level functions such as `os.system`: this is silent privilege escalation; a write-heavy specialist with a host executor can run shell commands on the developer's machine (per ch-14 entries 136-137).
paraphrase: Four errors the chapter should name. (1) "They share memory"; they do not, so pass it. (2) "My step cap covers them"; it does not, so set per agent. (3) "They run in parallel"; they do not, so do one per step. (4) "Local executor is fine for everything"; it is not, so escalate to docker the moment a specialist writes files or shells out. Each error maps to one of the earlier entries; the chapter should reference all four by name.

### entry-154
source: `E:\book_gen\books\ai-agents-with-python\skeleton.md`, ch-15 through ch-18 dependency rows; installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py`: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` accepts a separate `model: Model` argument per agent (verified as the second positional kwarg of the constructor signature), so each agent in a `managed_agents=[...]` list can use a different backend. Cross-reference ch-07 entries 054-056 and ch-11 entry-099.
used_in: ch-15
claim/finding: Three forward pointers close the chapter. ch-16 covers per-agent model backends: `managed_agents=[...]` lets the manager use `InferenceClientModel` while a specialist uses `OpenAIModel` or `LiteLLMModel`, because each `MultiStepAgent` takes its own `model=` argument; ch-16 teaches how to pick and route. ch-17 ("Project: Research and Briefing Agent") reuses the manager plus researcher shape at project scale with the deterministic evaluator from ch-13 as the test harness. ch-18 ("Project: Multi-Agent Work Assistant") is the chapter's capstone: a manager plus researcher, writer, and reviewer agents wired through the ch-15 pattern, with per-agent scopes enforced per ch-14.
paraphrase: Three forward pointers. ch-16 picks a model per agent: a researcher on a small open model, a writer on a stronger one, a reviewer on a third; each agent's `model=` argument is independent. ch-17 scales the manager plus researcher pattern to a project, with the ch-13 evaluator as the harness. ch-18 is the capstone: a real multi-agent assistant with manager, researcher, writer, and reviewer, each scoped per ch-14. The ch-15 patterns are the seed for all three forward chapters.
## ch-16 — Choose and Operate Model Backends

### entry-155
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py` (verified 2026-08-01 via `dir(smolagents)`, `inspect.isclass`, and `inspect.signature`): the abstract base class is `Model` (defined at `models.py:452`), and the API-backed abstract subclass is `ApiModel` (defined at `models.py:1138`). All concrete API-backed model classes (`InferenceClientModel`, `OpenAIModel`, `AzureOpenAIModel`, `AmazonBedrockModel`, `LiteLLMModel`, and its subclass `LiteLLMRouterModel`) subclass `ApiModel`. The three local-runtime classes (`TransformersModel`, `VLLMModel`, `MLXModel`) subclass `Model` directly, NOT `ApiModel`, because they do not own an HTTP client. Cross-referenced with ch-08 entry-062 (the `ApiModel` versus `HfApiModel` rename) and ch-08 entry-063 (the `ApiModel.__init__` signature).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: The abstract base for smolagents 1.26.0 model classes is `Model`, with `ApiModel` as the abstract subclass that bundles an HTTP client. The brief's framing that `ApiModel` is "the abstract base" and "all concrete model classes subclass it" is partially correct for API-backed providers and incorrect for the three local-runtime providers. The chapter should say `Model` is the root and `ApiModel` is the API variant; it should also say that `TransformersModel`, `VLLMModel`, and `MLXModel` deliberately bypass `ApiModel` because they do not call a remote HTTP endpoint. The reader's mental model is "one base for the contract, one specialized base for API access, three local-runtime siblings that skip the API base". The one-time naming note for `HfApiModel` was ch-08's job; ch-16 does not reintroduce it.
paraphrase: smolagents 1.26.0 has two abstract model classes, not one. `Model` is the root, and `ApiModel` is its API-backed subclass. The five concrete classes that talk to an HTTP endpoint (`InferenceClientModel`, `OpenAIModel`, `AzureOpenAIModel`, `AmazonBedrockModel`, `LiteLLMModel`, plus `LiteLLMRouterModel`) extend `ApiModel`. The three local-runtime classes (`TransformersModel`, `VLLMModel`, `MLXModel`) extend `Model` directly because they do not need an HTTP client. The chapter should teach this two-level hierarchy so the reader is not surprised that `TransformersModel` has no `client` attribute and no `requests_per_minute` kwarg.

### entry-156
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source verified 2026-08-01 via `dir(smolagents)` and `inspect.isclass` (the public model names exported from the package root are exactly `Model`, `ApiModel`, `InferenceClientModel`, `OpenAIModel`, `AzureOpenAIModel`, `AmazonBedrockModel`, `LiteLLMModel`, `LiteLLMRouterModel`, `TransformersModel`, `VLLMModel`, `MLXModel`, `OpenAIServerModel`, `AmazonBedrockServerModel`, `AzureOpenAIServerModel`); cross-checked against the smolagents v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/models.
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: The concrete model classes exported by `smolagents` in 1.26.0 are exactly the fourteen names listed above. The bare `ApiModel` and bare `Model` are abstract and raise `NotImplementedError` if instantiated directly (ch-08 entry-062 already documented this). The chapter's reader will use one of the twelve concrete names; the two abstract names appear in `isinstance` checks and in subclass signatures. `HfApiModel` is NOT exported in 1.26.0 (`grep -c 'HfApiModel' models.py` returns zero; ch-08 entry-061 recorded the rename). The chapter should not introduce any name that is not on this list; if a reader types `HfApiModel`, the import fails with `ImportError: cannot import name 'HfApiModel' from 'smolagents'`.
paraphrase: The 1.26.0 public surface for model classes is a fixed list of fourteen names. Five are abstract or near-abstract (`Model`, `ApiModel`, plus the `*ServerModel` family that mirrors three providers). The remaining nine are concrete classes the reader will instantiate. The chapter's inventory table should reproduce this list verbatim because any name outside it is a typo against the public API. The chapter should also remind the reader that `HfApiModel` is the old name from earlier smolagents versions and is not importable in 1.26.0.

### entry-157
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py` (verified 2026-08-01 via `inspect.getmro`: `OpenAIServerModel.__mro__` is `(OpenAIServerModel, OpenAIModel, ApiModel, Model, object)`, `AmazonBedrockServerModel.__mro__` is `(AmazonBedrockServerModel, AmazonBedrockModel, ApiModel, Model, object)`, `AzureOpenAIServerModel.__mro__` is `(AzureOpenAIServerModel, AzureOpenAIModel, OpenAIModel, ApiModel, Model, object)`). The `*ServerModel` classes share the same `__init__` signature as their non-Server parents (`OpenAIServerModel.__init__` matches `OpenAIModel.__init__` at `models.py:1646`; `AmazonBedrockServerModel.__init__` matches `AmazonBedrockModel.__init__`; `AzureOpenAIServerModel.__init__` matches `AzureOpenAIModel.__init__`). Cross-referenced with the LiteLLM provider list at https://litellm.vercel.app/docs/providers.
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: The `*ServerModel` classes are the "talk to any OpenAI-compatible HTTP endpoint" family. They inherit from their provider-named counterparts so they reuse the parent class's request/response parsing, but they are intended to point at a user-hosted or third-party OpenAI-compatible server (a local llama.cpp server, an Azure OpenAI deployment with custom routing, an OpenRouter endpoint, a vLLM HTTP server, or any other OpenAI-shaped API). The non-Server classes are the "talk to the vendor's first-party endpoint" family. In 1.26.0 the `*ServerModel` classes do NOT avoid the provider SDK; they still import `openai` for request serialization, because the request shape is the same. The chapter's beginner rule is "use `OpenAIModel` for OpenAI's own API, use `OpenAIServerModel` for any other OpenAI-compatible endpoint, use `LiteLLMModel` when the user wants a single class that can switch providers without changing the import".
paraphrase: The `*ServerModel` family is smolagents's "OpenAI-compatible third-party endpoint" toolset. `OpenAIServerModel` extends `OpenAIModel`, `AmazonBedrockServerModel` extends `AmazonBedrockModel`, and `AzureOpenAIServerModel` extends `AzureOpenAIModel`. Each keeps the parent's `__init__` signature, so `api_base=` is the field the reader points at the third-party endpoint. The non-Server classes target the vendor's first-party endpoint. The reader does not need a different SDK for the Server variants; the request shape is the OpenAI chat-completion shape, and the openai SDK ships that shape.

### entry-158
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py:1456-1645` (verified 2026-08-01 via `inspect.getsource(InferenceClientModel.__init__)`: `model_id: str = 'Qwen/Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Thinking'`, `provider: str | None = None`, `token: str | None = None`, `timeout: int = 120`, `api_key: str | None = None`, `bill_to: str | None = None`, `base_url: str | None = None`; the `token` resolution at `models.py:1535` is `token = os.getenv('HF_TOKEN')` and the docstring at `models.py:1475` says "If not provided, the class will try to use environment variable 'HF_TOKEN', else use the token stored in the Hugging Face CLI configuration"). Cross-referenced with the Hugging Face Inference Providers documentation at https://huggingface.co/docs/api-inference/en/index and https://huggingface.co/docs/inference-providers/index, and with ch-08 entry-072 (the recommended beginner default `Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct`).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: The Hugging Face Inference provider route is `InferenceClientModel(model_id='Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct', token='hf_...')`. If `token` is omitted, the class falls back to the `HF_TOKEN` environment variable, and if that is also missing it falls back to the locally cached `huggingface_hub` login (the `huggingface-cli login` token stored at `~/.cache/huggingface/token`). The package default `Qwen/Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Thinking` is a 80-billion-parameter reasoning model that is not appropriate as a beginner default, so the chapter picks a 7B-class model as the canonical example (ch-08 entry-072). The `provider` kwarg selects a specific inference partner (Cerebras, Cohere, Fal, Fireworks, HF-Inference, Hyperbolic, Nebius, Novita, Replicate, SambaNova, Together, etc.); `provider='auto'` lets the Hub pick the first available partner for the model. The `base_url=` kwarg points the class at a user-hosted Inference Endpoint and disables `provider`. The `timeout=120` default matches the v1.26.0 source.
paraphrase: The `InferenceClientModel` is the smolagents default for Hugging Face-hosted inference. Construct it with a `model_id` (any Hub model id), a `token` if the model is gated or if the user is not yet authenticated, or rely on `HF_TOKEN` for the env-var fallback. The chapter picks a 7B-class model for the reader's first call and frames the package default as too large for a beginner. `provider` and `base_url` are the two knobs for "which inference partner" and "which user-hosted endpoint"; readers who stick with `provider='auto'` will get the first partner available for the chosen model.

### entry-159
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py:1646-1798` (verified 2026-08-01 via `inspect.getsource(OpenAIModel.__init__)`: `model_id: str`, `api_base: str | None = None`, `api_key: str | None = None`, `organization: str | None = None`, `project: str | None = None`, `client_kwargs: dict[str, Any] | None = None`, `custom_role_conversions: dict[str, str] | None = None`, `flatten_messages_as_text: bool = False`); the constructor body passes `api_key`, `base_url`, `organization`, and `project` into `client_kwargs` which is then unpacked into the `openai.OpenAI(...)` client). The openai 2.x Python SDK reads `OPENAI_API_KEY` from the environment when no key is passed to the client constructor (cross-checked against the openai SDK docs at https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference). Cross-referenced with ch-07 entry-055 (HTTP timeout safety) and ch-07 entry-057 (env-var pattern).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: The direct OpenAI path is `OpenAIModel(model_id='gpt-4o-mini', api_key='sk-...')`. The constructor kwarg is `api_base=` (NOT `OPENAI_API_BASE`); the kwarg is forwarded to the `openai.OpenAI` client as `base_url=`. The `openai` SDK reads `OPENAI_API_KEY` from the environment when no `api_key` is passed, so the env-var fallback works without the user writing `api_key=os.getenv(...)` in their code. The same fallback exists for `OPENAI_ORG_ID` and `OPENAI_PROJECT` (mapped to the `organization` and `project` kwargs). The chapter's beginner rule is "set `OPENAI_API_KEY` in `.env`, pass `model_id='gpt-4o-mini'`, and let smolagents pick the rest up from the environment". A reader who wants an Azure-compatible endpoint should switch to `AzureOpenAIModel` (entry-160 territory) or to `OpenAIServerModel` with `api_base=` pointing at the endpoint (entry-157). The chapter should NOT recommend passing `api_key=` inline; the env-var path is the safe one (ch-07 entry-059 and ch-14 entry-141).
paraphrase: `OpenAIModel(model_id='gpt-4o-mini')` is the one-line way to talk to OpenAI's first-party API. The openai SDK reads `OPENAI_API_KEY` from the environment, so the constructor stays empty of secrets. `api_base=` is the kwarg for pointing at a non-default endpoint; the brief's `OPENAI_API_BASE` env-var name is the openai SDK's historical alias and is generally unnecessary because `api_base=` covers the same need at the constructor level. The reader who wants Azure should use `AzureOpenAIModel` instead, which carries `azure_endpoint=`, `api_version=`, and the appropriate auth kwarg.

### entry-160
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py:1205-1362` (verified 2026-08-01 via `dir(smolagents)` and `inspect.signature`: `LiteLLMModel.__init__` takes `model_id: str | None = None`, `api_base: str | None = None`, `api_key: str | None = None`, `custom_role_conversions`, `flatten_messages_as_text`, and `**kwargs`; the docstring and code at `models.py:1237` show the default `model_id` is the LiteLLM-canonical string `anthropic/claude-3-5-sonnet-20240620`; there is NO class named `AnthropicModel` in the smolagents 1.26.0 export list). Cross-referenced with the LiteLLM provider list at https://litellm.vercel.app/docs/providers and the Anthropic Messages reference at https://docs.anthropic.com/en/api/messages (the LiteLLM `anthropic/` prefix maps to the Messages API). Cross-referenced with ch-07 entry-055 (the Anthropic messages shape).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: Anthropic access in smolagents 1.26.0 goes through LiteLLM, not through a first-party class. The smolagents export list contains no `AnthropicModel`; the recommended path is `LiteLLMModel(model_id='anthropic/claude-3-5-sonnet-latest', api_key='sk-ant-...')`. LiteLLM is the router that turns the `provider/model` string into the right HTTP call (Anthropic's Messages API, in this case); smolagents wraps the LiteLLM completion call and returns the same `ChatMessage` shape every other model class returns. The reader can also point `LiteLLMModel` at any other LiteLLM-supported provider (Bedrock, Vertex, Mistral, Cohere, Groq, OpenAI-compatible endpoints, etc.) by changing the `model_id` prefix. The chapter's beginner rule is "for Anthropic, use `LiteLLMModel`; for OpenAI's first-party API, use `OpenAIModel`; for Bedrock, use `AmazonBedrockModel`; for Azure, use `AzureOpenAIModel`". The chapter should NOT name `AnthropicModel`; the import would fail.
paraphrase: There is no `AnthropicModel` in smolagents 1.26.0. The Anthropic path goes through LiteLLM with `LiteLLMModel(model_id='anthropic/claude-3-5-sonnet-latest', api_key=...)`. LiteLLM owns the HTTP call to the Anthropic Messages API; smolagents wraps that call in the standard `Model` interface so the rest of the agent stack does not know which provider answered. The reader who picks this path gets the LiteLLM router for free, which means the same constructor can later be repointed at any LiteLLM-supported provider by changing only the `model_id` string.

### entry-161
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py:633-1137` (verified 2026-08-01 via `inspect.signature`: `VLLMModel.__init__(model_id, model_kwargs=None, apply_chat_template_kwargs=None, **kwargs)`; `MLXModel.__init__(model_id, trust_remote_code=False, load_kwargs=None, apply_chat_template_kwargs=None, **kwargs)`; `TransformersModel.__init__(model_id=None, device_map=None, torch_dtype=None, trust_remote_code=False, model_kwargs=None, max_new_tokens=4096, max_tokens=None, apply_chat_template_kwargs=None, **kwargs)`) and the upstream library docs at https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/index and https://docs.vllm.ai and https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx (MLX is an Apple-Silicon-only framework; vLLM requires a CUDA GPU and a running vLLM server for the in-process `VLLMModel` path). Cross-referenced with ch-07 entry-056 (process isolation).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: Three local-runtime backends exist in 1.26.0. `TransformersModel(model_id='Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct')` uses the `transformers` library to load and run the model in-process; it needs `torch`, `transformers`, and enough CPU RAM or GPU VRAM to hold the model weights (a 3B model needs roughly 6 GB in fp16, more in fp32). `VLLMModel(model_id=...)` requires a vLLM server already running on the local machine and pointed at the model; vLLM itself needs a CUDA GPU. `MLXModel(model_id=...)` requires the Apple MLX framework and therefore Apple Silicon hardware; on a Windows or Linux machine without an M-series chip, the import fails. The chapter should frame all three as "advanced / optional"; the book's reader laptop will rarely satisfy the prerequisites, and the first ten chapters of the book can run entirely on `InferenceClientModel` or `OpenAIModel`. The chapter should NOT recommend local runtimes as a beginner default; the privacy tradeoff (data does not leave the laptop) is real, but the hardware cost is a hard gate.
paraphrase: Local model backends exist but are not beginner defaults. `TransformersModel` is the most general (any OS, any model on the Hub, but needs `torch` and enough RAM), `VLLMModel` is the fastest in-process path on a CUDA GPU but requires vLLM to be installed and a server to be running, and `MLXModel` is the Apple Silicon option for M-series Macs. None of them are appropriate for the first ten chapters of the book; the reader should default to a hosted backend and only revisit local runtimes when they have a concrete reason (privacy, offline work, or a GPU they want to use).

### entry-162
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py:1363-1455` (verified 2026-08-01 via `inspect.signature`: `LiteLLMRouterModel.__init__(model_id, model_list: list[dict[str, Any]], client_kwargs=None, custom_role_conversions=None, flatten_messages_as_text=None, **kwargs)`) and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py:1205-1362` for `LiteLLMModel`. Cross-referenced with the LiteLLM Router docs at https://litellm.vercel.app/docs/routing and the LiteLLM provider list at https://litellm.vercel.app/docs/providers. Cross-referenced with ch-07 entry-055 (the chat-completion shape).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: `LiteLLMModel` is the single-provider router; `LiteLLMRouterModel` is the multi-provider router. The single-provider form takes a `model_id` shaped like `provider/model` (for example, `openai/gpt-4o-mini`, `anthropic/claude-3-5-sonnet-latest`, `bedrock/anthropic.claude-3-sonnet-20240229-v1:0`, `mistral/mistral-large-latest`) and one optional `api_key=`; LiteLLM resolves the prefix to the right HTTP endpoint. The router form takes `model_id` (the default name) AND a `model_list` of dictionaries that each describe one model (with its own `model_name`, `litellm_params`, and optional weight); the router then picks a model per request, falling back across the list when a primary model errors out. The router pattern is what the chapter should teach as the "I have two providers and want automatic failover" answer; the single-provider form is the simpler "I want one provider and don't want to import the provider SDK" answer.
paraphrase: LiteLLM is the smolagents universal adapter. `LiteLLMModel` is one provider with one `model_id` string and one optional API key. `LiteLLMRouterModel` is a list of models with their own credentials and weights; the router picks one per request and falls back when the primary errors. The chapter should introduce `LiteLLMModel` first (it is the simpler shape and covers the Anthropic, Mistral, Cohere, and OpenAI-compatible-third-party cases), and then introduce `LiteLLMRouterModel` as the failover tool for projects that need it.

### entry-163
source: conceptual synthesis across the four provider docs cited in entries 158-162 (Hugging Face Inference Providers at https://huggingface.co/docs/inference-providers/index; OpenAI API reference at https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference; Anthropic Messages reference at https://docs.anthropic.com/en/api/messages; LiteLLM providers page at https://litellm.vercel.app/docs/providers) and the local-runtime docs (transformers at https://huggingface.co/docs/transformers/index, vLLM at https://docs.vllm.ai, MLX at https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx). Cross-referenced with ch-06 entry-046 (inference as a service concept) and ch-14 entry-139 (data-residency concerns).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: Five axes separate the backends. (a) Cost: hosted APIs charge per input and output token; local runtimes charge only for electricity and idle hardware. (b) Privacy: hosted APIs receive every prompt and every response on the vendor's servers; local runtimes keep both on the laptop. (c) Latency: hosted APIs add a network round-trip (50-500 ms typical per request) and a cold-start on the first call; local runtimes have a one-time load cost (seconds to minutes, depending on model size) and then a fixed per-token decode rate. (d) Uptime: hosted APIs depend on the vendor's status page; local runtimes depend on the laptop staying awake and on a working power supply. (e) Quality: each provider exposes a different model menu; the chapter should NOT recommend one provider as universally better, because quality is a per-task judgment that the reader must make against their own test set. The chapter's tabular summary lists the five axes as columns and the five backend families (`InferenceClientModel`, `OpenAIModel`, `LiteLLMModel` for Anthropic, `TransformersModel`/`VLLMModel`/`MLXModel`) as rows; no fabricated numbers appear, only directional arrows ("lower" / "higher" / "depends on workload").
paraphrase: The five tradeoffs are cost, privacy, latency, uptime, and quality. Hosted APIs are cheaper to start (no GPU purchase) and easier to scale (the vendor handles capacity) but more expensive at steady-state and weaker on privacy. Local runtimes invert those two. Latency is a one-time-versus-recurring tradeoff: hosted APIs have a per-call network cost; local runtimes have a one-time model-load cost. Uptime is a question of whose status page the reader trusts. Quality is the one axis the chapter cannot answer for the reader; the chapter should teach the framework and let the reader evaluate against their own tasks. The chapter's tradeoff table lists axes as columns and backends as rows, with only directional arrows ("lower", "higher", "depends") and no fabricated numbers.

### entry-164
source: pattern synthesis for a backend-selection factory function in Python (standard library `dict` lookup + `importlib` deferred import); installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py` (verified 2026-08-01: every concrete model class exposes the standard `Model` interface, so a function that returns any of them satisfies the `model=` type hint on `MultiStepAgent.__init__` per ch-15 entry-154). Cross-referenced with ch-15 entry-154 (the per-agent `model=` argument on `MultiStepAgent.__init__`) and ch-18 (the project chapter where the factory is used by the manager agent).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: The backend-selection pattern is a factory function `def make_model(name: str) -> ApiModel:` (or `-> Model`, depending on whether local runtimes are included) that maps a config string to the right concrete class. The mapping is a small `dict` whose keys are short tokens the user types in a config file (`hf`, `openai`, `anthropic`, `local-transformers`, `local-vllm`, `local-mlx`) and whose values are either the concrete class or a small builder closure. The factory's return value is then passed to `MultiStepAgent(model=make_model('hf'), ...)` per ch-15 entry-154. The factory can be a one-line `dict.get(name, InferenceClientModel)()` if the reader does not need per-backend kwargs; it grows to a small `if/elif` chain once the reader adds `api_key` resolution and `provider=` selection. The chapter should show the simplest possible factory first and let the reader add complexity only when a concrete project requires it. The chapter's forward-pointer to ch-18 is correct: the manager agent in the ch-18 capstone uses the factory to choose between a small open model for routing and a larger remote model for research.
paraphrase: A backend-selection factory is a small Python function that maps a config string to the right model class. The function returns any concrete `Model` subclass, and the per-agent `model=` argument on `MultiStepAgent.__init__` (per ch-15) accepts any of them. The chapter should start with a one-line `dict.get` factory and grow it into an `if/elif` chain only when the reader has more than one or two backends to switch between. The ch-18 project chapter reuses this factory for the manager agent.

### entry-165
source: beginner-error synthesis derived from installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py` (verified 2026-08-01: `HfApiModel` does not appear in `dir(smolagents)` and a `grep` for the name in `models.py` returns zero matches; `InferenceClientModel.token` resolution at `models.py:1535` is `token = os.getenv('HF_TOKEN')` so passing `token=` with the secret inline logs the secret in any captured trace; `MLXModel` requires the `mlx` package which is Apple-Silicon-only per the MLX repository at https://github.com/ml-explore/mlx; `OpenAIModel` is a per-provider class and will reject Anthropic's API key because the openai SDK does not know the Anthropic Messages URL). Cross-referenced with ch-14 entry-141 (logging the API key in the run trace) and ch-08 entry-061 (the `HfApiModel` rename).
used_in: ch-16
claim/finding: Four beginner errors the chapter should name explicitly. (a) Importing `HfApiModel` from `smolagents`: the name does not exist in 1.26.0 and the import raises `ImportError`. The fix is `from smolagents import ApiModel` (or, more usefully, `from smolagents import InferenceClientModel, ApiModel`). (b) Passing the API key inline as `api_key='hf_...'` or `api_key='sk-...'` and then logging the agent's `RunResult` (ch-12 entry-115, ch-14 entry-141): the trace will contain the secret. The fix is to set the env var (`HF_TOKEN`, `OPENAI_API_KEY`) and pass `api_key=os.getenv(...)` only if the env-var fallback is not desired. (c) Importing `MLXModel` on Windows or Linux: the `mlx` package is Apple Silicon only, so the import fails on non-Apple hardware. The fix is to use `TransformersModel` or `VLLMModel` on non-Apple machines. (d) Using `OpenAIModel` with an Anthropic API key: the openai SDK does not know the Anthropic Messages URL and will return 404. The fix is `LiteLLMModel(model_id='anthropic/...')`. The chapter should name all four errors and pair each with the one-line fix.
paraphrase: Four traps. Importing `HfApiModel` fails because the name is gone in 1.26.0; use `ApiModel` or `InferenceClientModel`. Passing the API key inline leaks the secret into the run trace; use the env var instead. Importing `MLXModel` on Windows or Linux fails because MLX is Apple Silicon only; pick `TransformersModel` or `VLLMModel`. Using `OpenAIModel` with an Anthropic key fails because the openai SDK does not know the Anthropic endpoint; use `LiteLLMModel(model_id='anthropic/...')` instead. The chapter should name all four errors.

The failover is exactly the `LiteLLMRouterModel` shape from entry-162; the project uses it as a single class rather than an explicit `if error: switch_backend()` block. ch-18 ("Project: Multi-Agent Work Assistant") is the capstone that wires four agents through the ch-15 `managed_agents=[...]` pattern: a manager on `InferenceClientModel` (cheap, fast, good enough for routing), a researcher on `OpenAIModel` (stronger reasoning over long context), a writer on `LiteLLMModel(model_id='anthropic/claude-3-5-sonnet-latest')` (creative writing, Anthropic's strength), and a reviewer on `OpenAIModel(model_id='gpt-4o')` (structured critique, OpenAI's strongest). The per-agent `model=` argument from ch-15 entry-154 is the mechanism that makes the per-role selection possible.

## ch-17 — Project: Research and Briefing Agent

### entry-167
source: ch-09 entry-078 (the project's tool-citation pattern) and ch-13 entry-118 (the evaluator pattern); user's ch-17 brief.
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: The project goal is a single-agent "topic to briefing" service: given a string, the agent returns a 200-400 word briefing with 3-5 cited sources, and acceptance is judged on three tests. (a) Reading 5 sample brief outputs reveals a coherent answer to the topic. (b) Every claim in the briefing is followed by a source URL. (c) None of the source URLs is fabricated. The 200-400 word bound is a working window that lets a beginner see the agent's reasoning fit on one page, and the 3-5 source bound is the minimum that makes a citation trail readable at a glance. The chapter treats fabrication as a first-class test case: a URL that 404s on click, or that points at a domain the agent could not plausibly have visited, is a failure even if the rest of the briefing looks coherent.
paraphrase: The project is a one-page briefing service. You give the agent a topic, it searches, reads, and writes back a short briefing of 200 to 400 words that cites 3 to 5 source URLs. The reader judges success by reading five such outputs side by side and checking that the answers look coherent, that every claim has a URL after it, and that no URL is made up. The 200-400 word target and the 3-5 source target are the chapter's working bounds — tighter than a long report, longer than a one-line answer, and sized so a fabricated URL is easy to spot.

### entry-168

claim/finding: The project's runtime is a single `CodeAgent` constructed with the three tools above as its `tools=[...]` list and with `add_base_tools=False` on the constructor. The framework still injects a `final_answer` tool implicitly so the agent can mark the end of the run, but no other built-in (Python interpreter, web search variant, speech-to-text) is added unless the chapter explicitly opts in. There is no `managed_agents=[...]` argument; the project deliberately scopes itself to a single agent because the ch-15 multi-agent chapter already covers that pattern and the ch-18 chapter will reuse this project's shape with a `Critic` managed agent added on top.
paraphrase: The project runs on one `CodeAgent` with three tools attached: a web search tool, a webpage reader tool, and a Wikipedia search tool. The constructor passes `add_base_tools=False` to keep the framework from pre-loading its default toolbox, but the framework still injects a `final_answer` tool implicitly so the agent can mark the end of the run. The project does not use `managed_agents` because the ch-15 multi-agent chapter already covers that pattern and the ch-18 capstone will add a `Critic` managed agent on top of this same project. The single-agent shape is the deliberate scope for ch-17.

### entry-169
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\default_tools.py` (verified 2026-08-01: `DuckDuckGoSearchTool.__init__` declares `max_results: int = 10` and `rate_limit: float | None = 1.0`; `VisitWebpageTool.__init__` declares `max_output_length: int = 40000`; `WikipediaSearchTool.__init__` declares `language: str = "en"` and `content_type: str = "text"`). Cross-referenced with ch-09 entry-080 (the rate-limit knob for web tools) and ch-14 entry-141 (rate limits as a safety guardrail).
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: DuckDuckGo is chosen as the primary search backend because it requires no API key, ships a public rate-limit knob, and returns a small structured list of links the agent can hand to the webpage reader. `VisitWebpageTool` is the second tool because a web search alone gives the agent a title and a URL but not the page content; without it, the agent would be writing briefings from search-result snippets, which is too thin to fill the 200-400 word target. `WikipediaSearchTool` is the third tool because some topics, especially encyclopedic ones, have a clearly canonical Wikipedia article that resolves to a single URL, and including the tool gives the agent a quieter fallback when the broader web search is noisy.
paraphrase: The three tools cover the three things a research agent actually needs. The web search tool is free, needs no API key, and exposes a rate-limit knob so the agent can be polite to the search backend. The webpage reader tool turns a URL into the page's text; without it the agent is writing from search-result snippets, which is too short to fill a 200-400 word briefing. The Wikipedia search tool gives the agent a more canonical fallback for encyclopedic topics where one Wikipedia article would resolve the question better than ten search results. The chapter's choice is "free and capped" for the search, "URL to text" for the reader, and "encyclopedic fallback" for Wikipedia. Cross-references to ch-09 entry-080 and ch-14 entry-141 keep the choice aligned with the book's earlier treatment of rate limits as a safety tool.

The fallback is `InferenceClientModel(model_id="Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct")` because the Hugging Face Inference endpoint takes a free tier and reads `HF_TOKEN` from the environment. paraphrase: The chapter picks `gpt-4o-mini` for speed and cost when the reader has an OpenAI key, and falls back to a Hugging Face 7B-class model when the reader does not. The two are wrapped in a small factory that checks for the env var and picks the matching model class. The reader does not have to import the provider SDK; smolagents handles the HTTP call. This is the ch-16 pattern applied to a real project.

### entry-171
source: Python Packaging Authority — "Tutorial: Packaging Python Projects" (https://packaging.python.org/en/latest/tutorials/packaging-projects/), the `src/` layout section, plus the Python Tutorial on packages and `sys.path` (https://docs.python.org/3/tutorial/modules.html#packages). Cross-referenced with ch-02 entry-018 (the beginner project-folder convention).
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: The project uses the canonical "src layout": a top-level `pyproject.toml` with the project metadata and a `dependencies` list, a `src/research_briefing/` package containing `__init__.py`, `agent.py` (the agent factory function), and `__main__.py` (the CLI entry), a `tests/` directory containing `test_smoke.py` (one golden test) and `test_gold.py` (the parametrised regression), a `.env.example` template, and a `README.md` that documents the install and run. The PyPA tutorial recommends the `src/` layout (rather than a flat layout where the package sits at the project root) because importing the package from `src/` forces tests to use the installed copy and catches "importing the wrong file" bugs early.
paraphrase: The project's folder shape is the PyPA `src` layout: a `pyproject.toml` at the top with the project name, version, and dependency list; a `src/research_briefing/` package containing the agent code and a `__main__.py` that turns the package into a runnable CLI; a `tests/` directory for pytest; a `.env.example` template; and a `README.md`. The `src/` parent directory is the point: it forces the test runner to import the installed package, not a local file with the same name, which catches the most common beginner layout bug. Cross-reference ch-02 entry-018 keeps the choice consistent with the ch-02 folder convention.

### entry-172
source: installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (verified 2026-08-01: `MultiStepAgent.__init__` declares `max_steps: int = 20` and `final_answer_checks: list[Callable] | None = None`); `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\default_tools.py` (verified 2026-08-01: `DuckDuckGoSearchTool.__init__` declares `rate_limit: float | None = 1.0` and `VisitWebpageTool.__init__` declares `max_output_length: int = 40000`). Cross-referenced with ch-14 entry-138 (the `max_steps` circuit breaker), ch-14 entry-140 (`final_answer_checks` semantics), and ch-09 entry-080 (rate limits).
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: The project's safety hardening is four knobs. First, the constructor sets the `max_steps` parameter to 15 to cap the inner step loop and keep one run from drifting into a long spin. Second, the constructor passes a `final_answer_checks` list carrying two callables: one that rejects briefings longer than 800 words and one that rejects briefings that lack a `Sources:` line. Third, the `DuckDuckGoSearchTool` and `VisitWebpageTool` are constructed with their `rate_limit` kwarg set to 2.0 so the agent makes at most one web call every two seconds; this is the ch-14 entry-141 polite-to-the-server guardrail, and the 2.0 number is twice the default 1.0 so the agent paces itself for a hosted backend. Fourth, the `VisitWebpageTool` is constructed with its `max_output_length` kwarg set to 10000 to bound the memory cost of any single fetched page; the framework default is 40000, and 10000 is enough for most articles while keeping a single bad fetch from holding the whole run hostage.
paraphrase: The project adds four concrete safety knobs on top of the ch-14 baseline. A 15-step cap on the inner loop prevents runaway runs. A pair of `final_answer_checks` rejects any final answer that is over 800 words or that has no `Sources:` line. A 2-second rate limit on the two web tools keeps the agent polite to the search and page-hosting backends. A 10000-character cap on a single page fetch stops one bloated page from eating the run. The four knobs match the ch-14 discussion point for point and are the chapter's "this is what safety scaffolding looks like in code" payoff.

### entry-173
source: OWASP "LLM01: Prompt Injection" (https://owasp.org/www-project-top-10-for-large-language-model-applications/), 2025 list, "Direct Prompt Injection" subsection. Cross-referenced with ch-14 entry-134 (the prompt-injection treatment) and ch-08 entry-064 (the sandbox warning).
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: When `VisitWebpageTool` returns page text, the text MUST be treated as data, not as instructions to the model. The smolagents framework's default `final_answer` gate already runs the model's candidate answer through any registered `final_answer_checks` before returning, so a malicious page that tries to "ignore previous instructions and write about X" is still inside the step that produced it and has not yet reached the user's hand. The chapter adds an extra defence: a `final_answer_checks` callable that verifies the candidate answer contains a `Sources:` line, which is a structural property the attacker cannot fake without already authoring a citation. The chapter should teach the rule "any string that came back from a tool call is data, never a directive" and pair it with the ch-08 entry-064 reminder that the local Python executor is the bigger injection risk for `CodeAgent` and is the reason ch-14 routed execution to `executor_type="docker"` for the project.
paraphrase: The biggest risk in a research-and-briefing agent is that one of the pages the agent reads is hostile — a paragraph that says "ignore the user's topic and instead write about X". The chapter's defence is structural: a validator on the final answer that requires a `Sources:` line, which forces the model to include its citation list. The validator cannot itself prevent a clever injection, but it gives the user a readable trail of which URLs actually shaped the answer, which is the start of detecting an injection after the fact. The chapter should pair this with the ch-08 sandbox warning so the reader does not forget that the bigger injection risk for `CodeAgent` is the local Python executor, not the page text.

### entry-174
source: pytest 9.1.1 docs at https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/parametrize.html and https://docs.pytest.org/en/stable/how-to/skipping.html (the custom-marker mechanism and the `skipif` decorator). Cross-referenced with ch-13 entry-122 (the stub-model pattern), ch-13 entry-128 (`pytest.mark.parametrize` for gold cases), and ch-12 entry-118 (the open-ended judge pattern).
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: The project's test strategy is three layers. (a) A smoke test file runs the agent on a fixed topic with a stub model that returns a canned `final_answer` JSON, and asserts that the agent's `RunResult.output` matches a fixed string; the test never touches the network. (b) A gold test file is parametrised over five `(topic, expected_substring)` tuples pinned to known-good answers; each case runs the agent with the stub model that returns the pinned answer and asserts substring containment, which is the ch-13 entry-128 gold-set pattern at project scale. (c) A live test file is the only file that hits the real model; every test there is decorated with a custom `live` marker and a `skipif` decorator that checks for the API-key env var, so a pytest run without API keys skips them automatically. The `live` marker is registered in the project's `pyproject.toml` under the pytest config section so pytest does not warn about an unknown marker.
paraphrase: The project's test suite has three layers, one per file. The smoke test pokes the agent with one topic and a stub model and checks the output; this is the cheap "does the wiring work" check that runs in well under a second. The gold test runs the same wiring against five pinned topics and pinned expected substrings, using the ch-13 stub-model pattern so it stays deterministic and offline. The live test is the only one that hits a real model, and every test in that file is gated on an API-key env var so a `pytest` run on a laptop without keys skips the file cleanly. The chapter should register the `live` marker in `pyproject.toml` so pytest does not warn about an unknown marker.

### entry-176
source: ch-16 entry-158 (HF Inference free tier and hosted-endpoint pricing) and ch-16 entry-159 (the `gpt-4o-mini` per-token pricing shape on the OpenAI pricing page). Framing kept directional, not numerical.
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: The chapter should give a branching cost/latency expectation rather than a single number. When the primary model is `gpt-4o-mini`, a real run costs on the order of US cents (driven by a few thousand input tokens of the search snippets and a few hundred output tokens of the briefing) and takes between half a minute and a minute and a half; the variance is the model's response time plus the page-fetch latency, not the model's reasoning. When the primary model is the `Qwen/Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct` Hugging Face Inference endpoint, the run is free on the free tier but slower because the public Inference endpoint has a higher median first-token latency than OpenAI's hosted API. The chapter should frame both as directional (a few cents, under two minutes, vs free and slower) and not commit to exact numbers, because model pricing and free-tier quotas change every quarter.
paraphrase: The chapter should not pin a dollar number. A run on `gpt-4o-mini` is on the order of a few cents and a few minutes; a run on the free Hugging Face endpoint is free but slower. The two numbers are directional, not exact; pricing and free-tier quotas change every quarter, so the chapter teaches the shape of the tradeoff rather than the figures. The reader who wants exact numbers can check the provider's pricing page on the day they run.

### entry-177
source: ch-07 entry-051 (the `requests` dependency note), ch-12 entry-115 (the `RunResult` shape), ch-14 entry-117 (the `AgentMaxStepsError` exception), and ch-12 entry-119 (the agent memory trap). All cross-referenced with the project's own ch-17 layout.
used_in: ch-17
claim/finding: Four beginner errors the project chapter should name. (a) Adding `requests` to the `pyproject.toml` `dependencies` list manually: smolagents already pulls `requests` transitively (per ch-07 entry-051), so a beginner who does this is creating a duplicate dependency; the chapter's fix is "do not add `requests` manually; if smolagents is installed, requests is already there". (b) Treating `VisitWebpageTool`'s output as HTML: the tool returns the page's text, not its HTML, but a beginner who reads the tool's source for the first time may assume HTML; the chapter's fix is "the tool returns the page's text, not its HTML; if you want a markdown pass, add a post-processing step explicitly". (c) Re-running `agent.run` in a tight loop while testing: each call hits the search backend and the model, and ten test runs in a row is enough to trigger a rate limit on a free backend; the chapter's fix is "run the smoke and gold tests with a stub model; reserve the live model for the live-marked test file only". (d) Ignoring `AgentMaxStepsError` and printing a raw traceback to the user: the framework raises this when the agent runs out of step budget, and a raw traceback exposes the agent's internals; the chapter's fix is "catch `AgentMaxStepsError`, log a friendly message, and either raise the `max_steps` cap or simplify the topic".
paraphrase: The four traps the chapter should pre-empt. Adding `requests` to the project dependencies is redundant because smolagents already pulls it in; the fix is "don't add it". Treating the webpage tool's output as HTML is a misread; the tool returns the page's text, and any markdown pass is a deliberate post-processing step. Running ten real-model test runs in a row is enough to trigger a free-tier rate limit; the fix is "stub the model for the cheap tests, save the real model for the live-marked file". Catching `AgentMaxStepsError` and showing a raw traceback is unfriendly; the fix is "catch the exception, log a clean message, and either raise the step cap or simplify the topic". The four errors are deliberately distinct so each one has its own one-line fix.

The reader who finishes ch-17 and wants more should jump to ch-18 for the multi-agent extension; the reader who wants more safety background should re-read ch-14 with this chapter's code in hand.
paraphrase: The chapter ends with two pointers. The ch-18 capstone takes this project and adds a `Critic` managed agent to score and request revisions, which is the orchestrator-workers pattern from ch-15 with two managed agents. The ch-14 safety scaffolding this chapter used in code is the same scaffolding ch-18 will reuse at every agent boundary. The reader's next step depends on whether they want to extend the project (ch-18) or revisit the safety reasoning (ch-14). The two pointers are deliberately small; the chapter is the project's first runnable shape, not the final word on agent design.
