## ch-08 — How Agents Work: A Toy Agent from Scratch

### entry-191
source: ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models (Yao, Zhao, Yu, Du, Shafran, Narasimhan, Cao, 2022), arXiv:2210.03629, ICLR 2023; cross-referenced with the ch-01 entry-006 "agency as a spectrum" framing and the ch-01 entry-007 Anthropic "workflows vs agents" framing.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: An agent loop is a four-step cycle. The first step is "observe" — read the user's task or the result of the previous tool call. The second is "decide" — call the model with the running history and ask what to do next. The third is "act" — run the tool the model named. The fourth is a new "observe" — capture the tool's output. Then the cycle repeats. ReAct (Yao et al., 2022) is the canonical reference for this shape and is the paper that introduced the Thought-Action-Observation interleaving pattern that nearly every modern agent framework still automates. Frame the model as "one of many inputs to a program" rather than "a brain that does things", because the loop is the program and the model is one of the steps.
paraphrase: An agent loop is the same four-step shape repeated until done: read the current state, ask the model what to do, run the tool it picked, and feed the tool's answer back as the new state. The model is one input among several; the program is the loop. The ReAct paper from 2022 is the canonical reference and is what every later agent framework, smolagents included, is automating. The chapter should make the loop the protagonist and the model a participant, not the other way around.

### entry-192
source: ReAct (Yao et al., 2022), arXiv:2210.03629, Section 3 (the HotpotQA worked example showing the prompt format); cross-referenced with the Anthropic "Messages" reference at https://docs.anthropic.com/en/api/messages for the system/user/assistant role convention, and with the ch-06 entry-049 (OpenAI "Text generation" guide) for the same convention in the OpenAI family.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The prompt the loop sends to the model is the contract between the two. For a toy agent the prompt needs four ingredients. (1) The goal — the user's task as a string. (2) The available tools — a list of tool names, the parameter shape each one expects, and a one-line description of what each tool returns. (3) The action grammar — a worked example of a valid structured action so the model knows the exact text shape to emit. (4) The conversation history — every prior assistant message and every prior tool result, in order. Frame as "the prompt is the contract": if any of the four ingredients is missing or wrong, the loop will misbehave in a way the reader should be able to predict, and the chapter should name the four ingredients in the same order on every page.
paraphrase: The loop's prompt is a contract with four ingredients: the goal, the available tools (with their parameter shapes and return types), a worked example of a valid action so the model can pattern-match, and the running history. If any one of these is missing, the loop fails in a recognizable way: the model invents a tool, returns prose, or repeats itself. The chapter should name the four ingredients in the same order on every page so the reader can keep them in mind as they read.

### entry-193
source: Python 3.14.6 standard library docs — the "json" module at https://docs.python.org/3.14/library/json.html (the `json.loads` reference) and the "re" module at https://docs.python.org/3.14/library/re.html (the `re.match` and `re.search` reference); OpenAI "Structured model output" guide at https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/structured-outputs and Anthropic "Tool use" guide at https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/tool-use for the production-grade alternatives; ReAct (Yao et al., 2022) Figure 1 for the original "Action: tool_name / Input: ..." grammar.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The model returns text and the loop must turn that text into a structured action. The two common toy patterns are (a) a JSON object in a fenced code block, parsed with `json.loads` after stripping the fence, and (b) a custom grammar such as "Action: tool_name / Input: ..." parsed with a small `re.search` call. Both are stdlib-only. The chapter should note, in one sentence, that production systems use JSON-mode and tool-calling APIs that force the model to emit valid structured output server-side, but the toy agent uses raw text on purpose so the reader can see the parse step rather than having the framework hide it.
paraphrase: Two ways to turn a model's text into a structured action. The first is to ask for a JSON object in a code block and parse it with `json.loads`. The second is to ask for a custom grammar like "Action: tool_name / Input: ..." and parse it with a small regex. Both are stdlib-only. The chapter should mention in passing that production systems have server-enforced structured-output modes, but the toy agent uses raw text on purpose so the reader can see the parse step rather than having the framework hide it.

### entry-194
source: Python 3.14.6 standard library docs — "Mapping Types — dict" at https://docs.python.org/3.14/library/stdtypes.html#dict; the "Errors and Exceptions" tutorial at https://docs.python.org/3.14/tutorial/errors.html; the language reference "8.4. The try statement" at https://docs.python.org/3.14/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-try-statement.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: A toy agent's tool layer is a plain Python dict that maps tool names to callables. The loop looks up the model's named action in the dict, calls the function with the parsed arguments, and feeds the return value back as the next observation. Three failure modes the loop has to handle itself because the dict does not. (1) Missing tool: the model names a tool the dict does not have; the lookup returns `None` and the loop should report "unknown tool X" rather than crashing. (2) Wrong argument names: the model's `action_input` keys do not match the function's parameter names; Python raises `TypeError` and the loop should catch it. (3) Tool raises an exception: the loop should catch `Exception` and surface the error string to the model, because the next iteration is the model's chance to recover.
paraphrase: The tool layer is a dict from name to function. The loop looks up the action, calls the function, feeds the result back. Three failure modes the loop has to handle itself: the model names a tool that is not in the dict, the model's argument keys do not match the function's parameters, and the tool itself raises an exception. In all three cases the loop should catch the failure and surface a string the model can read, so the next iteration is the model's chance to recover rather than the reader's chance to read a traceback.

### entry-195
source: OpenAI "Text generation" guide at https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/text-generation and Anthropic "Messages" reference at https://docs.anthropic.com/en/api/messages (the chat-completion role convention, which requires assistant-then-tool ordering for the same model turn); ReAct (Yao et al., 2022) Section 3 for the "history grows by one turn per step" pattern.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: After the tool runs, the loop appends the assistant message and the tool result to the conversation history and sends the now-longer list back to the model. The model's next prompt is therefore the user's original task plus every prior assistant-and-tool pair, in order. Frame as "the conversation history grows by one turn per step" so the reader can picture the list of `{"role": ..., "content": ...}` dicts getting one or two entries longer each iteration. Two practical notes. First, the growth is bounded only by the model's context window, so a loop without a `max_steps` guard can blow past the context limit silently. Second, the order matters: the assistant message must come before the tool message, because that is the only order the chat-completion APIs accept.
paraphrase: After each tool call the loop appends the assistant's action and the tool's output to the conversation history and sends the longer list back to the model. The history grows by one or two messages per step, and the order is fixed: assistant first, tool second. The chapter should name the growth as "one turn per step" so the reader can picture the list of role-tagged dicts getting longer, and flag that without a step cap the history can outgrow the model's context window.

### entry-196
source: Anthropic "Building effective agents" (https://www.anthropic.com/research/building-effective-agents), 2024-12-19, the "What are agents?" section, captured in ch-01 entry-007. Cross-referenced with the ReAct paper (Yao et al., 2022) for the "stop when the model emits a final-answer action" convention, and with the ch-09 `FinalAnswerTool` definition as the framework-side analog of the chapter's `final_answer` action.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The loop ends when the model returns an action that is not a tool call but a "final answer" — a structured payload that the loop unwraps and returns to the caller. This is the same stop signal ch-09's `FinalAnswerTool` and ch-08's stub model will emit: a value the loop recognizes as "I am done" rather than "run this next". The chapter's framing for the termination condition should borrow from the ch-01 entry-007 distinction: an agent is "an LLM that dynamically directs its own process"; an unbounded loop without an explicit stop signal is not a safe agent, it is a runaway. The chapter should put `final_answer` on the same conceptual pedestal as `max_steps`: both are required safety knobs.
paraphrase: The loop ends when the model emits a `final_answer` action — a structured value the loop unwraps and hands back to the caller. This is the same stop signal the frameworks will use, and the chapter should treat it as a first-class concept on par with the `max_steps` cap. The Anthropic "workflows vs agents" framing from ch-01 entry-007 says an agent is "an LLM that dynamically directs its own process"; a loop without an explicit stop is not an agent, it is a runaway. The chapter should plant the rule "every loop needs both a `final_answer` action and a `max_steps` cap" before introducing either one.

### entry-197
source: ch-07 entry-060 (the minimal conversation loop) and ch-07 entry-054 (the chat-completion request shape); Python 3.14.6 standard library docs — the "while" statement reference at https://docs.python.org/3.14/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-while-statement.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The ch-07 conversation loop made one model call per turn and printed the response; the ch-08 agent loop makes many model calls per run, each one driven by the tool result of the previous call. The conceptual difference is "the program calls the model, reads the answer, decides what to do, runs the chosen tool, and calls the model again" versus "the program calls the model and prints the answer". The reader can already do the ch-07 loop with the `requests` code they wrote two chapters ago; the ch-08 loop is the same code wrapped in a `while True` that runs the model a second and third time when the model asks for more information. Frame the chapter as "ch-07 made a request; ch-08 makes a request, reads the answer, decides what to do, does it, and makes another request".
paraphrase: ch-07 was a single model call inside a `while` loop that read user input. ch-08 is the same shape but the `while` loop's body calls the model, parses a tool name, runs the tool, feeds the result back, and calls the model again. The chapter's first line of code is almost the same as ch-07's; the difference is what happens between two consecutive model calls. The reader is not learning a new HTTP client — they are learning the loop that sits around the client they already wrote.

### entry-198
source: OpenAI "Parallel tool calls" guide at https://platform.openai.com/docs/guides/function-calling/parallel and Anthropic "Tool use — Parallel tool use" section at https://docs.anthropic.com/en/docs/tool-use#parallel-tool-use; smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the `_run_stream` loop calls `self.step()` once per step, so two actions in one response still cost two iterations), verified 2026-08-01.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The DIY toy agent has two specific costs the chapter should name so the reader can see what a framework will fix. First, no parallel tool calls: the toy loop dispatches one tool per iteration, even when the model's response names three. Production APIs support parallel tool calls in a single response; the toy loop cannot, because it reads the assistant message, calls the function, and then sends the next request — the parallel branch is invisible to it. Second, no schema-aware retries: the toy parser uses a regex or `json.loads`; if the model returns slightly malformed output the parser raises and the loop reports the parse error to the model on the next step, which costs a full model round trip per malformed action. A schema-aware parser can usually reformat the same string locally without asking the model again. Frame as "the framework will fix these two specific things".
paraphrase: The DIY toy has two specific costs the chapter should name. The first is sequential tool calls: even when the model could do three things at once, the loop does them one at a time, because each iteration ends with one tool call and one model round trip. The second is no schema-aware retries: a malformed action is reported back to the model and costs a full round trip, when a small reformatting step on the loop's side could often fix it locally. Frame both as "the framework will fix these two things" so the reader knows what to look for in ch-09.

### entry-199
source: ReAct (Yao et al., 2022), Figure 1, the Thought-Action-Observation loop; cross-referenced with the ch-01 entry-006 "agency as a spectrum" framing and the ch-01 entry-007 Anthropic "workflows vs agents" framing.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The chapter's mental model for every iteration is "observe → decide → act → observe". Mapped to ch-08's code: the first "observe" is reading the user's task from the conversation history; "decide" is the call to the model; "act" is the function call into the tools dict; the second "observe" is reading the tool's return value. The loop continues until a "decide" step produces a `final_answer` action rather than a tool name. The chapter should print this exact four-word sequence as a one-line comment or subhead in the code so the reader can attach the words to the four lines of code they are about to write.
paraphrase: Every iteration is "observe, decide, act, observe". The first observe reads the user's task. The decide is the model call. The act is the function call. The second observe is the tool's return value. The loop ends when decide produces a `final_answer` rather than a tool name. The chapter should print the four words in order on the page so the reader can attach each one to a line of code.

### entry-200
source: Python 3.14.6 standard library docs — the `dict` type and the `json` module at https://docs.python.org/3.14/library/json.html; verified runtime in `E:\book_gen\.venv` (Python 3.13.7, `requests` 2.34.2), 2026-08-01, by running a small toy loop with a stub model and confirming the loop iterates, terminates on `final_answer`, and is bounded by `max_steps` when the stub never emits `final_answer`.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The chapter's "offline demo" replaces the model call with a stub — a plain Python function or dict keyed on a substring of the prompt that returns a canned `{"action": ..., "action_input": ...}` dict. The stub is enough to drive the loop end-to-end: the loop parses the stub's output, dispatches to a real tool, feeds the real tool's result back, and on the next iteration the stub returns `{"action": "final_answer", "action_input": {"answer": ...}}`. Frame as "the loop is testable without ever calling a model" — beginners can run the chapter's example on a laptop with no API keys, and the same stub pattern is the foundation of ch-13's testing chapter. The verified runtime in the project's venv (Python 3.13.7) confirms the loop runs to completion in well under a second and respects `max_steps` when the stub never emits `final_answer`.
paraphrase: The chapter's offline demo replaces the model call with a stub. The stub is a plain Python function that returns a canned action dict based on a substring of the prompt. Wired into the loop, the stub drives the full observe-decide-act-observe cycle without ever hitting a model API: the first stub call returns a tool action, the loop runs a real tool, the second stub call returns `final_answer`, and the loop prints the answer. The runtime check in the project venv confirms the loop terminates correctly and the `max_steps` guard fires when the stub never returns `final_answer`. The chapter should frame the stub as "the loop is testable without ever calling a model" and point forward to ch-13, which uses the same pattern for agent tests.

### entry-201
source: verified runtime in `E:\book_gen\.venv` (Python 3.13.7, `requests` 2.34.2), 2026-08-01, by running four small ad-hoc loops and confirming each error path is reachable from the same toy-loop code; Python 3.14.6 standard library docs — the `KeyError` and `TypeError` sections of the built-in exceptions page at https://docs.python.org/3.14/library/exceptions.html and the `try/except` reference at https://docs.python.org/3.14/reference/compound_stmts.html#the-try-statement.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: Four beginner errors the chapter should pre-empt with one-line fixes. (a) Infinite loop without `max_steps`: the model returns a tool action every iteration and never emits `final_answer`; the loop runs forever. The fix is "always pass a `max_steps` cap" — a 5-step cap is enough for the chapter's example and any 30-line toy agent. (b) Model returns prose instead of a structured action: the parser hits `KeyError` or `json.JSONDecodeError`. The fix is "catch the parse error and append a 'please respond in the documented action format' message to the history before the next model call" — that gives the model one more chance to comply. (c) Action name mismatch (tool name typo): the dict lookup returns `None` and the loop crashes with `TypeError: 'NoneType' object is not callable`. The fix is "check the lookup before calling and append 'unknown tool X, available tools are ...' to the history". (d) Tool exception unwitnessed: the function raises and the loop propagates the exception, which kills the run. The fix is "wrap the call in `try/except Exception` and append the error string to the history so the model can see what went wrong".
paraphrase: Four beginner traps the chapter should pre-empt, with one-line fixes for each. (a) No `max_steps` cap — the loop runs forever when the model never returns `final_answer`; fix is "always pass a step cap, even for toy loops". (b) Model returns prose — the parser raises `KeyError` or `JSONDecodeError`; fix is "catch the parse error and tell the model to follow the documented action format". (c) Tool name typo — the dict lookup crashes with `TypeError`; fix is "check the lookup, then tell the model which tool names are actually available". (d) Tool exception unwitnessed — the exception kills the run; fix is "catch `Exception` and append the error string to the history so the model can try a different action on the next step". The four fixes are deliberately distinct so each one has its own one-line recipe.

source: ch-09 entry-074 (the smolagents `@tool` decorator contract), ch-09 entry-075 (the tool schema requirements), and the ch-09 skeleton row (smolagents' first-agent chapter); cross-referenced with the ch-09 contradiction paragraph that names `FinalAnswerTool` as the framework-side analog of the chapter's `final_answer` action.
used_in: ch-08
claim/finding: The chapter ends with a one-sentence forward pointer: the next chapter (ch-09, formerly ch-08 in the pre-structural-change ordering) replaces this 30-line toy loop with `smolagents.CodeAgent` and names the four things the framework automates — the prompt assembly, the action parsing, the tool dispatch, and the `final_answer` termination. The reader who finishes ch-08 should be able to read the ch-09 `CodeAgent` example and recognize every one of the four steps from the toy loop they just wrote. The framework is the loop; the chapter's job is to make the loop visible.
paraphrase: The chapter ends with a single forward pointer: the next chapter replaces this loop with `smolagents.CodeAgent` and names the four pieces the framework automates (prompt assembly, action parsing, tool dispatch, termination). After finishing ch-08 the reader should be able to open the ch-09 example and recognize every step of the toy loop inside the framework's code. The framework is the loop; the chapter's job is to make the loop visible so the framework is not magic.

## Contradiction flags (material — unresolved, pending user decision)

ch-09 has one material mismatch between the requested chapter brief and installed smolagents==1.26.0 behavior. The brief proposed saying that tool-return dictionaries are JSON-encoded and numbers are coerced to strings, but `Tool.__call__`, `handle_agent_output_types`, and `FinalAnswerTool.forward` show that normal calls preserve raw Python values; even the sanitized path wraps strings as `AgentText` while leaving ordinary dicts and numbers uncoerced. Entry-077 records the authoritative behavior and recommends explicit `json.dumps()`/`str()` when text is intended. chub was available and used first, but its cached guide targets 1.24.0, so all version-sensitive claims were resolved against installed 1.26.0 source. This mismatch does not conflict with ch-08 entry-061 or the one-time naming-note requirement in entry-070, but it is material enough that the chapter writer must follow entry-077 rather than the brief's coercion wording; user adjudication is requested only if the brief must override verified runtime behavior.

ch-01 adds no material contradictions. The smolagents "agency as a spectrum" framing (entry-006) and the Anthropic "workflows vs agents" distinction (entry-007) describe different axes (how much LLM control vs whether the LLM dynamically directs) and reconcile cleanly: a system can be high-agency in the smolagents sense while still being what Anthropic calls an "agent." No user decision required at this time.

ch-02 adds no material contradictions. The Python-on-Windows install story changed materially between 3.13 and 3.14 (the "Python install manager" replaced the legacy "Python launcher for Windows" and the "full installer" — see entry-010); this is a recency/freshness concern, not a contradiction between two valid sources, so it is recorded as an age-risk in entry-010 rather than as a contradiction flag. The recommended Python version floor (3.10+, set by smolagents per entry-009) is consistent with the actively supported Python versions on python.org/downloads and does not conflict with any other source. The default install path (python.org on Windows/macOS, distro package on Linux) is the union of the Python docs' own recommendations and does not require user adjudication. No user decision required at this time.

ch-13 adds no material contradictions between primary sources and records zero brief-corrections. Every claim in entries 121..132 is verified directly against the installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the `MultiStepAgent.run` signature, the `_step_stream` call site at line 1309 calling `self.model.generate(...)`, the `RunResult` shape, the `state` computation, and the `logger=` constructor kwarg) and `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py` (`Model.__call__` as a thin wrapper over `Model.generate`, `Model.__init__` having no `create_client`, `ApiModel.create_client` raising `NotImplementedError` and called from `ApiModel.__init__`, `ChatMessage.__init__` signature, and `MessageRole.ASSISTANT` value), and against the pytest 9.1.1 docs (fixtures, parametrize, `pytest.raises`, async test patterns) and pytest-asyncio 1.4.0 docs (`asyncio_mode` configuration). Two inline age-risks are recorded as brief-corrections within individual entries, NOT as contradictions between primary sources: (1) entry-122 -- the brief proposed saying a stub can override `Model.__call__` only; the verified 1.26.0 runtime dispatches via `self.model.generate(...)` at agents.py:1309, so a stub that overrides `__call__` alone still hits the abstract `Model.generate` and raises `NotImplementedError` (confirmed by a live smoke test 2026-08-01); the correct 1.26.0 pattern is `subclass Model` and `override generate`, which also avoids the `ApiModel.create_client` raise because `Model` has no `create_client` at all. (2) entry-125 -- the brief said to pass a custom `AgentLogger` subclass via `monitor=`; the verified 1.26.0 `MultiStepAgent.__init__` accepts `logger: AgentLogger | None = None` and constructs the `monitor` attribute internally from `Monitor(self.model, self.logger)` at line 350, with no `monitor=` kwarg; the chapter's test pattern is therefore to subclass `AgentLogger`, override the gating `log` method to record into a list, and pass the instance via `logger=`. Both corrections are reconciliations between the brief and the verified 1.26.0 source, not disagreements between two primary sources, and no user adjudication is requested. One additional age-risk is flagged inline without correction: entry-128's `pytest.raises(AgentMaxStepsError)` pattern is stable on 2026-08-01 but would need a tuple of classes if a future release adds a sibling exception (e.g. `AgentTimeoutError`). No user decision required at this time.

ch-15 adds zero material contradictions between primary sources and records three inline age-risks and one brief-correction. Entries 143..154 are verified directly against the installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\agents.py` (the `MultiStepAgent.__init__` signature; the `_setup_managed_agents` source at lines 102-120; the `_setup_tools` source at lines 122-135; the `_run_stream` loop at lines 273-345; and the `MultiStepAgent.__call__` source at lines 601-623) and the default Jinja prompt template at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\prompts\code_agent.yaml` under the `managed_agent:` key; the Anthropic five-pattern taxonomy (ch-11 entry-098) was re-read for the orchestrator-workers, evaluator-optimizer, and routing subsections on 2026-08-01; chub `smolagents/package` was consulted first and the v1.26.0 reference page (https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/agents) was used to cross-check the `managed_agents=` parameter, both confirming the same 1.26.0 surface. The single brief-correction (NOT a contradiction between primary sources) is: the chapter brief expected the Jinja template keys to be `{{managed_agent.report}}` and `{{managed_agent.task}}`, but the verified 1.26.0 Jinja variable names in `MultiStepAgent.__call__` are `{{name}}` and `{{task}}` for the task prompt and `{{name}}` and `{{final_answer}}` for the report prompt; the keys are inner Jinja names, not nested paths; this is recorded as entry-145 with the actual default report template quoted. The three inline age-risks are: (1) entry-145, the verified Jinja names are pinned to 1.26.0 default templates and any user-supplied `prompt_templates={...}` override can rename the variables; (2) entry-147, the per-agent `max_steps` semantics are pinned to 1.26.0; (3) entry-150, the sequential-only managed-agent invocation is a 1.26.0 limitation, not a future promise. No user decision is required at this time.

ch-16 adds 12 entries (entry-155 through entry-166) with zero material contradictions between primary sources. The verified smolagents==1.26.0 public surface for model classes (entry-156) was confirmed via `dir(smolagents)` against the installed package at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\models.py` on 2026-08-01; the v1.26.0 reference page at https://huggingface.co/docs/smolagents/v1.26.0/en/reference/models was the cross-check; chub was consulted first but its cached guide targets 1.24.0, so all version-sensitive claims were resolved against the installed source and the v1.26.0 reference. Three brief-corrections are recorded inline rather than as contradictions between primary sources: (1) entry-155 -- the brief framed `ApiModel` as "the abstract base" and said all concrete classes subclass it; verified 1.26.0 source shows `Model` is the root abstract class, `ApiModel` is the API-backed abstract subclass, and the three local-runtime classes (`TransformersModel`, `VLLMModel`, `MLXModel`) extend `Model` directly because they do not own an HTTP client; the chapter teaches the two-level hierarchy rather than the simplified one-level one. (2) entry-157 -- the brief hinted that the `_Server` classes do not bundle provider SDKs; verified 1.26.0 source shows `OpenAIServerModel` still extends `OpenAIModel` (and therefore inherits the `openai` SDK usage) because the request shape is the OpenAI chat-completion shape regardless of who serves it; the distinction is "talk to the vendor's first-party endpoint" versus "talk to any OpenAI-compatible endpoint", not "SDK vs no SDK". (3) entry-159 -- the brief named `OPENAI_API_BASE` as the env var smolagents reads for a non-default endpoint; verified 1.26.0 source shows the smolagents-level kwarg is `api_base=` (forwarded to the openai client as `base_url=`); the env-var path is the openai SDK's legacy `OPENAI_API_BASE`, which is generally unnecessary because `api_base=` covers the same need at the constructor level. Two inline age-risks are recorded within the entries: entry-158 flags the v1.26.0 default `Qwen/Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Thinking` as impractical as a beginner default (the chapter picks a 7B-class model per ch-08 entry-072); entry-163 flags the absence of universal quality rankings (no fabricated numbers appear in the tradeoff table, only directional arrows). No user decision is required at this time.


ch-17 adds 12 entries (entry-167 through entry-178) with zero material contradictions between primary sources. The 1.26.0 API claims are all verified against the installed smolagents==1.26.0 source at `E:\book_gen\.venv\Lib\site-packages\smolagents\` on 2026-08-01: the three built-in tools `DuckDuckGoSearchTool`, `VisitWebpageTool`, and `WikipediaSearchTool` exist with the expected `rate_limit` and `max_output_length` knobs (entry-169, entry-172); `MultiStepAgent.__init__` accepts `max_steps`, `final_answer_checks`, `add_base_tools`, `managed_agents`, `instructions`, `step_callbacks`, `logger`, and `return_full_result` as documented in 1.26.0 (entry-168, entry-172); the public model surface is the same fourteen-name list that ch-16 entry-156 pinned (entry-170). chub `smolagents/package` was consulted first but its cached guide targets 1.24.0, so all version-sensitive claims were resolved against the installed 1.26.0 source. The PyPA "Packaging Python Projects" tutorial and the Python Tutorial on packages are the primary sources for the `src` layout (entry-171); OWASP LLM01:2025 is the primary source for the prompt-injection framing (entry-173); pytest 9.1.1 docs are the primary source for the marker and `skipif` mechanism (entry-174). The ch-17 brief's "no `HfApiModel` mention" rule is honored across entries 167-178 — no `HfApiModel` appears anywhere in the section, and the one-time naming note remains ch-08's job. Four inline age-risks are recorded within individual entries, not as contradictions between primary sources: (1) entry-169, the `DuckDuckGoSearchTool` default `rate_limit=1.0` is a 1.26.0 default that may change in a future release; (2) entry-170, the `InferenceClientModel` package default `Qwen/Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B-Thinking` is impractical for beginners and the chapter picks a 7B-class model per ch-08 entry-072; (3) entry-172, the four-knob safety hardening is pinned to the 1.26.0 kwarg surface and a future smolagents release could rename `max_output_length` or change the `final_answer_checks` contract; (4) entry-176, the cost/latency expectation is explicitly framed as directional, not numerical, because model pricing and free-tier quotas change every quarter. No user decision is required at this time.

ch-08 adds 12 entries (entry-191 through entry-202) with zero material contradictions between primary sources. The new ch-08 is a plain-Python chapter by design and the brief explicitly excludes any smolagents, openai, or anthropic SDK claims, so chub validation is not required. The ReAct citation in entry-191 is verified against the arXiv preprint (arXiv:2210.03629) and the ICLR 2023 publication record; the four-step Thought-Action-Observation loop is the paper's contribution and the chapter's mental model is consistent with it. The Anthropic "workflows vs agents" framing in entry-196 is a restatement of the ch-01 entry-007 citation, not a new claim, and the four-step observe-decide-act-observe vocabulary in entry-199 is consistent with both ReAct and the ch-01 framing. Runtime claims in entries 200 and 201 (the stub-model demo runs to completion, the `max_steps` guard fires when the stub never emits `final_answer`, and each of the four beginner-error paths is reachable from the same toy-loop code) were checked on 2026-08-01 in the project venv at `E:\book_gen\.venv` (Python 3.13.7, `requests` 2.34.2) and reconcile with the Python 3.14.6 stdlib docs cited in the entries. No smolagents API surface is claimed anywhere in the section, and the dispatch rule "do not mention `HfApiModel` or `ApiModel` in ch-08" is honored — the one-time naming-note sidebar remains in the pre-structural-change ch-08 (the "Build a First smolagents Agent" section, entries 062..073) and the ch-09 rewrite inherits it when the old ch-08 is renumbered. Two minor inline age-risks are recorded rather than treated as contradictions: (1) the stub-model demo is plain Python and will not break across Python releases, but a future Python release that changed `dict.__getitem__` semantics would alter the "missing tool" lookup pattern in entry-194 — no such change is announced on 2026-08-01; (2) the "Action: ... Input: ..." grammar in entry-193 is a ReAct-paper convention and modern model families are increasingly trained for JSON-only output, so the chapter keeps both parsers on the page rather than recommending one. No user decision is required at this time.
